**Summary.** This paper proposes Complex Psiformer, a novel neural network ansatz that uses complex-valued features and attention mechanisms to accurately model the wavefunctions of interacting electrons in magnetic moiré superlattices. By incorporating complex phases and magnetic boundary conditions, the model significantly outperforms its real-valued counterpart in minimizing the system's energy. This work establishes a robust variational tool for studying charge order and electronic structure in finite magnetic quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit handling of complex phases and magnetic boundary conditions within a neural network framework provides a powerful, modern tool for simulating complex quantum phases relevant to condensed matter physics, bridging ML and strongly correlated systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing an effective variational search method for magnetic many-electron wavefunctions, which requires optimizing both amplitude and phase due to strong electronic correlations.

**Main result.** The Complex Psiformer architecture achieves lower ground state energies compared to its real-valued counterpart, demonstrating the advantage of complex representations for describing correlated electron states in magnetic moiré systems.

**Method.** The authors introduce Complex Psiformer, a neural network architecture that incorporates complex hidden features and Hermitian-magnitude attention (HMCVA) to construct the many-electron wavefunction.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on interacting two-dimensional electron gas systems placed in a triangular moiré potential under a perpendicular magnetic field. The Hamiltonian includes kinetic, moiré potential, and electron-electron interaction terms, subject to magnetic boundary conditions.

**Key observables.** Ground state energy, charge order structure factors ($S(K)$), sublattice occupation patterns ($f_3$), and gauge-invariant current maps.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic flux quanta ($n_\phi$), system size (e.g., 25-cell supercell), and the comparison between complex and real network representations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that organizing hidden electron features as complex amplitudes provides necessary radial nonlinearities and Hermitian overlaps for accurate variational optimization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the moiré geometry, the comparison between Complex Psiformer and Real Psiformer architectures, and the dependence of density anisotropy on training seeds.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model (interacting electrons in a moiré lattice with magnetic flux). It then details the advanced neural network architectures (Complex Psiformer vs. Real Psiformer) designed to handle complex phases and magnetic boundary conditions. Finally, it presents benchmarks comparing the energy and structural properties derived from both complex and real ansatzes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic many-electron wavefunctions require amplitude and phase to be optimized together. Whether a complex internal representation improves this variational search is a practical question for neural wavefunction design. We introduce Complex Psiformer for interacting electrons in a magnetic moiré continuum, combining complex hidden features and Hermitian-magnitude attention with magnetic boundary conditions and fermionic antisymmetry. After the same number of optimization steps, Complex Psiformer reaches lower energies than Real Psiformer in two finite supercells. Both Psiformers also improve on their respective neural Hartree-Fock references. Across five training seeds in the 25-cell system, the mean Complex advantage is 1.458 meV per electron, with a smaller observed spread. A separately trained two-electron Complex state has a smaller energy gap to a finite configuration interaction reference than its Real counterpart. In the Complex states, flux scans show nonmonotonic density correlations and weaker honeycomb mean-density modulation at higher flux, while connected fluctuations persist. Gauge invariant current maps provide a qualitative comparison of local circulation in the optimized states. These benchmarks support the combined architecture as a variational ansatz for studying energies and charge arrangements in finite magnetic systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a quantitative link between the steady-state Non-Equilibrium Green's Function (NEGF) method and the time-domain wave packet (WP) approach for molecular electron transport. By showing that the WP transmission is an energy-weighted average of the NEGF result, the authors allow current-voltage characteristics to be calculated directly from time propagation. This consistency check is vital for accurately modeling complex phenomena like spin-dependent transport in chiral molecules.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides a rigorous mathematical bridge between two powerful but distinct formalisms (steady-state vs. time-domain) used to study open quantum systems, which is crucial for understanding molecular electronics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the theoretical challenge of connecting steady-state descriptions of electron transport (NEGF) with time-domain dynamics (Wave Packet method) in molecular junctions.

**Main result.** A quantitative correspondence is derived showing that the time-averaged transmission from the wave packet method equals a spectral average of the energy-resolved transmission calculated via NEGF.

**Method.** The authors employ both the Non-Equilibrium Green's Function (NEGF) formalism for steady-state transport and the time-dependent wave packet propagation method for transient dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system models electron transport through a molecular junction coupled to electrodes, extended to include spin-phonon coupling to study chirality-induced spin selectivity (CISS).

**Key observables.** Electron transmission probability, current-voltage characteristics, and spin polarization.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bias voltage (V), wave packet width ($\Delta$), and coupling strengths ($\alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The correspondence holds for molecular tight-binding and spin-phonon models, and the analysis often assumes zero temperature.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare WP and NEGF results for total transmittance and spin polarizations, and show I-V curves derived from both methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the NEGF framework for steady-state transport, then introduces the WP method for time dynamics, derives the quantitative link between the two, and finally applies this equivalence to complex models like spin-phonon systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electron transmission from an input electrode, through a molecular system, to an output electrode has been widely studied using the steady-state non-equilibrium Green's function (NEGF) method. Recently, the wave packet method, which provides access to the transient dynamics of electrons as well as internal molecular degrees of freedom, has been employed to investigate enantiospecific electron transport through chiral molecules. In this work, we derive the quantitative relation between the transmission of a finite-size wave packet and the energy-resolved NEGF transmission, showing that the former corresponds to a spectral average of the latter weighted by the wave packet energy distribution. Exploiting this correspondence, we construct non-Gaussian auxiliary wave packets whose spectral weight encodes the Landauer energy-window, allowing current-voltage characteristics to be obtained directly from time propagation. We further show that the correspondence extends to spin-resolved transport in a spin-phonon model of chirality-induced spin selectivity.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study models polar molecules whose interactions are controlled by microwave fields and static electric fields. By deriving an effective interaction potential, the authors predict that the system can enter a supersolid stripe phase even with small microwave ellipticity. This work advances the understanding of quantum gas control and exotic phase realization in ultracold molecular systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the control of quantum many-body systems using tailored light fields, providing a mechanism to realize exotic phases like supersolids in molecular BECs, which is highly relevant to modern AMO research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates microwave-controlled interactions and the resulting stripe phase formation in a system of polar molecules trapped in external fields.

**Main result.** The research demonstrates that even small microwave ellipticity can induce a supersolid stripe phase in a quasi-two-dimensional molecular Bose-Einstein condensate.

**Method.** The study employs perturbation theory to derive effective interaction potentials, which are then used in the extended Gross-Pitaevskii equation (GPE) and analyzed via Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) equations.

**Model / system.** The system consists of polar molecules subjected to a static electric field and an elliptically polarized microwave field. The interactions are modeled using derived effective potentials, leading to a mean-field description for the gas dynamics.

**Key observables.** Collective excitation spectrum, ground state density, total loss rate coefficient, and the formation of the stripe phase.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Static field strength (F), microwave ellipticity ($\xi$), detuning ($\Delta$), and Rabi frequency ($\Omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The effective interaction approach is validated against coupled-channel calculations, and the GPE approach assumes the applicability of mean-field theory.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of loss rates and scattering ratios on field parameters, and show the excitation spectrum of the condensate as a function of ellipticity, revealing the transition to a stripe phase.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by deriving the effective interaction potential via perturbation theory, analyzing the resulting loss mechanisms, and finally applying the effective interaction within the GPE framework to calculate and map out the excitation spectrum, revealing the stripe phase transition.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study polar molecules where short-range losses are suppressed by a shielding scheme involving a static electric field and an elliptically polarized microwave field. Using perturbation theory, we derive the effective interaction potential and validate it against coupled channel calculations. We identify a parameter regime where two-body losses are strongly suppressed and the extended mean-field description of dilute molecular Bose-Einstein condensates is justified. We calculate the collective excitations and show that intriguingly, supersolidity in quasi-two-dimensional confinement emerges as a stripe phase even at small values of microwave ellipticity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents novel, zero-overhead walking circuits for the honeycomb Floquet code to combat leakage errors in quantum computation. By dynamically removing leakage while preserving code distance, the circuits show superior error correction capabilities compared to existing methods. The work highlights the importance of entropic effects, predicting a rapid 'waterfall' improvement in error rates at finite physical error levels.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on leakage mitigation and the observation of non-standard error scaling regimes (waterfall) provide advanced insights into the practical limits of fault-tolerant quantum computation beyond simple asymptotic bounds.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the significant challenge of leakage errors in quantum error correction (QEC), which induce correlated errors and degrade code performance.

**Main result.** The proposed zero-overhead walking circuits for the honeycomb Floquet code can correct as many leakage errors as Pauli errors, and exhibit a pronounced 'waterfall regime' where performance surpasses distance-limited scaling predictions.

**Method.** The authors introduce and analyze two specific walking circuits (swirling and sliding) designed to periodically remove leakage while dynamically protecting logical qubits using anticommuting two-body measurements.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the honeycomb Floquet code (hFC) implemented on a hexagonal lattice, analyzed under several physically motivated leakage noise models (e.g., depolarizing, skip-gate leakage).

**Key observables.** Logical Error Rate ($p_L$), Code Distance, Qubit Lifetime, and the scaling exponent ($\alpha$) of the error rate.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Physical error rate ($p$), leakage noise strength, and the comparison between asymptotic low-error regimes and finite-error 'waterfall' regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The circuits are designed to be 'zero-overhead,' meaning they do not require additional qubits, gates, or time steps for leakage removal.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the hFC on the lattice, the four leakage noise models considered, and plots showing the dependence of the logical error rate on the physical error rate, highlighting the waterfall regime.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the hFC and the leakage problem, details the construction of the zero-overhead circuits (swirling/sliding), analyzes performance under various leakage models via numerical simulation, and compares results against the walking surface code.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Leakage, occurring when a qubit undetectably exits the computational subspace, poses a significant challenge for quantum error correction by inducing correlated errors in space and time. These correlations reduce both the code threshold and the effective code distance. To address this challenge, we introduce two zero-overhead walking circuits for the honeycomb Floquet code (hFC), termed the swirling and sliding circuits, which periodically remove leakage while dynamically protecting logical qubits via a schedule of anticommuting two-body measurements. Unlike previous dynamic circuits for the hFC, ours preserves the distance to Pauli errors. We further study their performance under leakage and find that, for two leakage noise models, they may correct as many leakage errors as Pauli errors, improving on the more widely known walking surface code. Through numerical simulation, we observe that finite-error-rate performance under leakage is strongly influenced by entropic effects, producing a pronounced waterfall regime in which the logical error rate decreases much more rapidly with physical error rate than the expected distance-limited scaling. In this regime, even when asymptotic predictions suggest otherwise, the hFC can outperform the same-distance walking surface code in error rates and sub-threshold logical error scaling.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, gradient-based algorithm (gradBEA) to optimize the single-particle basis used in Matrix Product States for fermionic systems. By minimizing a block entropy area cost function, the method iteratively refines the state representation, achieving superior convergence and accuracy compared to previous local optimization techniques. It provides a powerful, generalizable tool for simulating complex quantum many-body physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of a non-local, gradient-based optimization technique for tensor network representations is a powerful algorithmic advance for simulating strongly correlated quantum systems, potentially improving upon standard DMRG limitations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is efficiently finding the ground state eigenstates of many-body Hamiltonians for interacting fermions, which is computationally intractable due to the exponential growth of the Hilbert space.

**Main result.** The proposed gradient-based method (gradBEA) achieves significantly faster convergence and yields lower ground state energies compared to established local optimization protocols.

**Method.** It introduces a systematic block entropy area based mode optimization algorithm that uses the gradient of an entanglement cost function to drive an iterative, non-local update of the single-particle basis within the Matrix Product State framework.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to interacting fermions, modeled using Matrix Product States (MPS). Specific examples include spinless 'tt'V' models on a square lattice, the Hubbard model, and the $	ext{Fe}_4	ext{S}_4$ cluster.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the ground state energy ($E_0/t$) and the entanglement measure, the block entropy area ($B_\mu$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The optimization depends on the entanglement measure ($\mu$-Rényi entropy $S^{(i)}_\mu$) and the parameters ($\vec{	heta}$) governing the unitary rotations ($U^{(s)}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The optimization is performed iteratively by following the steepest descent path defined by the gradient, and the method is restricted to optimizing rotations within the spanned single-particle space.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares the ground state energy convergence of gradBEA against local methods for the $	ext{tt}'	ext{V}$ model. Figure 2 illustrates the iterative loop and the tensor diagram used in the calculation.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the systematic derivation of the gradient of the block entropy area, outlines the three-step iterative optimization protocol (DMRG simulation, gradient calculation, time evolution via PS-TDVP), and validates the method on various lattice and molecular models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a systematic block entropy area based mode optimization algorithm for many-body quantum states of interacting fermions represented by matrix product states. From the gradient of a global cost function, the block entropy area, a long-ranged, non-interacting effective disentangler Hamiltonian is formed. We then simulate the time-dependent Schrödinger equation driven by the disentangler Hamiltonian by employing the time-dependent variational principle based on projector splitting, and minimize the cost function. The combination of the density matrix renormalization group with this gradient-based entanglement minimization forms an efficient low-rank iterative ground-state algorithm that also provides an optimized single-particle basis for matrix product state representation. We demonstrate the method on two-dimensional lattice models of interacting fermions and the Fe${_4}$S${_4}$ cluster, and show its robustness and superiority over earlier protocols using nearest-neighbor mode rotations and reorderings.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study analyzes the phase transitions and critical temperature of a Bose gas coupled coherently in a harmonic trap. By using semiclassical and HFB-Popov methods, the authors show that coherent coupling modifies $T_c$, and that repulsive interactions stabilize the system's response to this coupling. The results provide a detailed phase diagram mapping the transition from magnetic to thermal states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the interplay between coherent quantum coupling, spatial confinement, and interatomic interactions on fundamental quantum phase transitions in ultracold atomic systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the condensation temperature and magnetic phase transitions in a Bose gas subjected to coherent coupling (Rabi coupling) within a harmonic trap.

**Main result.** Coherent coupling enhances the critical temperature, and repulsive interactions substantially suppress the variation of this temperature with increasing coupling strength.

**Method.** The study employs the semiclassical description for the noninteracting case and the Hartree–Fock–Bogoliubov–Popov (HFB-Popov) framework for interactions.

**Model / system.** The system is a Bose gas confined in a 3D harmonic trap, modeled with a Hamiltonian incorporating a coherent coupling term ($\sigma_x$). The analysis covers both noninteracting and interacting regimes.

**Key observables.** Critical temperature ($T_c$), condensate fraction ($N_0/N$), and the magnetic phase diagram (Ferromagnetic $	o$ Paramagnetic $	o$ Thermal gas).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coherent coupling strength ($\Omega$), particle number ($N$), and interaction strength ($g$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The semiclassical approximation ($k_B T \gg \hbar\omega$) is used for the density of states, and the analysis incorporates finite-size corrections.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the excitation spectrum showing level splitting, the density of states ($ho_{3D}, ho_{2D}$) for varying coupling, and the normalized $T_c$ and condensate fraction versus temperature.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from noninteracting analysis using semiclassical methods to incorporating finite-size corrections. It then extends the analysis to include repulsive interactions using the HFB-Popov framework to map the full phase diagram.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate condensation and finite-temperature magnetic phase transitions in a coherently (Rabi) coupled Bose gas confined in a three-dimensional harmonic trap. For the noninteracting system, we derive analytical expressions for the critical temperature and condensate fraction using a semiclassical description of the single-particle spectrum and density of states. At fixed particle number, coherent coupling enhances the critical temperature relative to the uncoupled system, and this enhancement decreases with increasing particle number $N$. The finite-size correction, in contrast, lowers the transition temperature, with its effect diminishing for larger $N$. We then incorporate repulsive interactions within the Hartree--Fock--Bogoliubov--Popov framework to investigate the finite-temperature phase diagram in the temperature--Rabi coupling plane. The system undergoes successive transitions from a ferromagnetic to a paramagnetic condensate and, at higher temperature, to a thermal gas. The transition boundaries obtained from the vanishing of the condensate fraction show excellent agreement with the analytical Hartree-Fock predictions. We find that the interactions substantially suppress the variation in the condensation temperature with an increase in the coherent coupling.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an engineering framework for quantum networking by characterizing noise and state degradation over real metropolitan fiber links. The authors adapt classical communication metrics like SINR and BER to the quantum domain, measuring impairments across polarization, time, and frequency. This work transforms quantum networking from a theoretical exercise into a quantifiable engineering design problem for the Quantum Internet.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides concrete, measurable metrics (like the quantum link budget) that bridge fundamental quantum optics theory with practical, large-scale engineering deployment challenges in quantum communication.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish an engineering foundation for deploying quantum networks over existing metropolitan fiber infrastructure by quantitatively characterizing channel noise and quantum state degradation.

**Main result.** The quantum link can be characterized by a small set of measurable parameters, transforming quantum networking from a pure physics demonstration into a solvable engineering design problem.

**Method.** The authors developed a unified framework by creating quantum analogs of classical metrics (SINR and BER) and applying them to real-world measurements on a deployed fiber loop.

**Model / system.** The study uses a 7.3 km metropolitan fiber loop in the QuantumInternet.it testbed, comparing quantum signals (C-band) to classical traffic (O-band). The analysis models noise using a photon-counting SINR analog and state degradation across polarization, time, and frequency using BER analogs.

**Key observables.** Dark-count rate, interference count from classical traffic, channel-induced degradation, and temporal drift for polarization, time, and frequency.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The link budget is built from measurable parameters, including the ratio of signal to noise/interference counts, and the spectral characteristics of Raman noise.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes the use of a shared physical medium, allocating classical traffic to the O-band and quantum signals to the C-band.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates that quantum state degradation is captured by two complementary descriptions: the SINR analog (noise side) and the BER analog (error side, resolved across polarization, time, and frequency).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the foundation by first defining the necessary metrics (SINR/BER analogs), then experimentally measuring noise (dark counts, crosstalk) and state degradation (polarization, time, frequency drift) on a deployed loop, culminating in a comprehensive quantum link budget.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Deploying quantum networks over existing network infrastructures requires the same engineering foundations that underpin classical communications: quantitative models of the channel's noise and of the impairments it imposes on the transmitted information. In this work, we build such a foundation on experimental measurements, grounding the quantum-network counterparts of the two cornerstone metrics of classical link characterization - namely, the SINR and the BER - on a 7.3 km deployed metropolitan-scale fiber-loop interconnecting two campuses of the University of Naples Federico II within the national QuantumInternet.it testbed. On the noise side, we adopt a photon-counting quantum analog of the SINR - in which dark counts constitute the intrinsic noise and the photons generated by classical traffic (through either spontaneous Raman scattering or inter-fiber crosstalk) constitute the interference - and we quantify each contribution directly on the deployed loop. On the bit-error side, we consider the main degrees-of-freedom available to encode a quantum state within an optical photon - namely, polarization, time, and frequency - and we quantify for each degree the channel-induced degradation and its drift over time. These results show that a quantum fiber link, like its classical counterpart, can be captured by a small set of measurable parameters, turning quantum networking over deployed fiber from a physics demonstration into an engineering design problem. Together, they provide the key ingredients of a quantum link budget for the Quantum Internet.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a generalized entropy formula for tensor network models representing TQFTs with continuous gauge groups. By imposing specific constraints on the network structure, the authors derive an entropy that incorporates infinite excitations, successfully connecting the model to 3D gravity theories. This work advances the use of tensor networks to probe fundamental concepts in quantum gravity and topological order.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of tensor networks to model fundamental concepts like topological entanglement entropy and gravity provides a concrete, computable framework for understanding quantum gravity phenomena, which is relevant to understanding complex many-body ground states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Deriving a generalized entropy formula for tensor network models that describe diffeomorphism invariant states of Topological Quantum Field Theories (TQFTs) with continuous gauge groups.

**Main result.** The derived entropy formula successfully generalizes topological entanglement entropy to incorporate infinite excitations from continuous gauge groups, providing an analogue to holographic entropy in 3D gravity.

**Method.** The authors use advanced algebraic techniques, including defining a factorization map and imposing electric/magnetic constraints on the tensor network structure, to calculate the entropy.

**Model / system.** The model is a topological tensor network defined on a surface $\Sigma$, which is used as a toy model for the holographic map in quantum gravity. It is designed to prepare states corresponding to TQFTs with gauge groups like $\text{SL}(2, \mathbb{R})$.

**Key observables.** The entropy $S(\rho_R) = H[p_\pi] + \langle\hat{A}\rangle_\rho + S_{\text{bulk}}(\rho_R)$, where $\langle\hat{A}\rangle_\rho$ is the universal term analogous to the area term.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The gauge group (e.g., $\text{SL}(2, \mathbb{R})$), the genus and boundary components of the surface $\Sigma$, and the small Newton's constant $G_N$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a factorization map $V$ to handle gauge constraints and relies on the equivalence between the factorization map approach and the algebraic approach.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a lattice tessellating the disk, distinguishing between bulk and boundary vertices, which is central to defining the entropy calculation.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the entropy formula by first defining the physical Hilbert space using constraints (electric and magnetic) on the tensor network structure, leading to the final generalized entropy expression.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive an entropy formula for recently proposed tensor network models which prepare diffeomorphism invariant states of topological quantum field theories with non-compact and/or continuous gauge groups. We show that our entropy formula generalizes the notion of ``topological entanglement entropy'' to incorporate the infinite number of particle-like excitations in such theories. When our networks are endowed with gauge group $\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})$, we can interpret them as models of three-dimensional gravity with small Newton's constant and possibly non-invertible metrics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes quantum fluctuations of a massive scalar field inside a charged black hole, specifically focusing on the Cauchy horizon. The key finding is that quantum effects cause divergences in the stress-energy tensor on the CH, suggesting a stronger singularity than classical theory predicts. This work probes the limits of semi-classical gravity in extreme astrophysical environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on general relativity, the study of divergences and backreaction in extreme spacetime geometries shares conceptual parallels with understanding instabilities or strong coupling regimes in quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the behavior of quantum massive scalar fields near the Cauchy horizon (CH) of a charged Reissner-Nordström black hole, focusing on quantum divergences and their implications for spacetime structure.

**Main result.** The vacuum polarization and stress-energy tensor components diverge on the CH for massive fields, suggesting a strong singularity via backreaction, unlike the classical prediction.

**Method.** The authors employ a semi-classical approach, calculating renormalized expectation values of quantum fields using techniques like the extended coordinate method in curved spacetime.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum massive scalar field propagating in the interior of a charged, spherically-symmetric Reissner-Nordström black hole spacetime.

**Key observables.** Vacuum polarization ($\langle \hat{\Phi}^2 angle$), components of the renormalized quantum stress-energy tensor ($\langle \hat{T}_{\mu
u} angle$), and energy fluxes in Eddington-Finkelstein and Kruskal coordinates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Black hole charge ($Q$), field mass ($\mu$), coupling constant ($\xi$), and the choice of quantum state (Hartle-Hawking vs. Unruh).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the RN background and relies on the semi-classical approximation where quantum effects source classical spacetime curvature.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the divergence of vacuum polarization and stress-energy tensor components near the CH for varying field masses and charges, and show the dependence of fluxes on parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper analyzes the quantum field behavior by comparing massive and massless cases, calculating divergences in the vacuum polarization and stress-energy tensor components, and discussing the implications for the stability and singularity structure of the Cauchy horizon.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate a quantum massive scalar field in the interior of a charged and spherically-symmetric (Reissner-Nordström) black hole. We examine the behaviour for varying values of the black hole charge, field mass and coupling constant when the field is in two quantum states: Hartle-Hawking (representing a black hole in thermal equilibrium) and Unruh (representing a black hole evaporating via the emission of Hawking radiation). We show that the vacuum polarization as well as the angular components of the quantum stress-energy tensor diverge on the Cauchy horizon, in stark contrast to what happens for massless fields. We also calculate the energy fluxes in Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates $\{u,v\}$. We show that these fluxes on the Cauchy horizon do not generically vanish. This implies, in particular, that in regular, Kruskal coordinates $\{U,V\}$ the ingoing flux diverges like $V^{-2}$ on the Cauchy horizon (where $V=0$). This divergence suggests that its backreaction via the semiclassical Einstein equations would yield a strong singularity, as opposed to its weaker, classical counterpart. Interestingly, there are exceptions, in which the energy fluxes in Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates vanish: (i) in the extremal limit (where the black hole is maximally charged); (ii) certain fine-tuned regions of parameter space, where the fluxes change sign.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically examines quantum droplets in a two-component Bose mixture confined by an optical lattice, analyzing the crossover from 3D to quasi-1D regimes. Using functional-integral methods, the authors show that the self-binding mechanism persists across this crossover, with the attraction sourced differently in the 3D versus 1D limits. The results quantify the influence of p-wave interactions on this induced binding.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical understanding of how dimensionality and specific interspecies interactions (p-wave) govern the stability and binding mechanism of quantum droplets in engineered optical lattices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates quantum droplets in a symmetric two-component Bose mixture confined by an optical lattice, specifically focusing on the crossover between different dimensional regimes.

**Main result.** The system supports a self-bound minimum across the dimensional crossover, with the attraction arising from different physical mechanisms (lattice vs. fluctuations) depending on the regime.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Euclidean functional-integral framework, calculating observables like ground-state energy and quantum depletion at the Gaussian level.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-component Bose mixture ($\psi_1, \psi_2$) subjected to a 2D transverse optical lattice, incorporating s-wave and p-wave interspecies interactions.

**Key observables.** Ground-state energy, quantum depletion, and the equilibrium density across the dimensional crossover.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lattice depths ($t_1, t_2$), interspecies coupling ratio ($y = g_{12}/g$), and p-wave interaction strength ($g_p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes equal masses and intraspecies couplings, and uses the lowest-band approximation for the lattice confinement.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the lattice structure and the crossover from 3D to quasi-1D regimes, and show the energy per particle versus density, confirming the existence of a finite-density minimum.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from deriving the Euclidean action in the tight-binding regime, calculating the ground-state energy and quantum depletion via Gaussian fluctuations, and finally analyzing the resulting equation of state across the dimensional crossover.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study quantum droplets in a symmetric two-component Bose mixture with interspecies $p$-wave interactions and a two-dimensional transverse optical lattice. The lattice drives a crossover from an anisotropic three-dimensional gas to weakly coupled one-dimensional tubes. We calculate the ground-state energy and quantum depletion at the Gaussian level and derive their limiting forms. At $y=g_{12}/g=-0.95$, where the bare mean field is repulsive and no free-space droplet exists, the calculated bulk equation of state supports a self-bound minimum across the crossover: a negative lattice contribution at order $n^{2}$ supplies the attraction in the three-dimensional regime, and attractive fluctuations do so in the quasi-one-dimensional regime, with the intermediate, transdimensional range described quantitatively by neither limit. The interspecies $p$-wave interaction modifies only the spin branch. In the parameter range studied, increasing its strength lowers the equilibrium density across the crossover, consistently with a weakening of the induced binding.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides an integrated theoretical and computational study of the Kagome 3J model, aiming to understand Quantum Spin Liquid behavior in materials like YCu3(OH)6Br2. By employing advanced tensor network and machine learning techniques, the authors map out the phase diagram and predict characteristic signatures, such as specific heat anomalies, which are then directly compared to experimental measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous combination of advanced quantum simulation techniques (NQS/ViT, XTRG/tanTRG) to predict measurable thermodynamic quantities like specific heat makes this work highly relevant for experimental condensed matter physicists studying frustrated magnets.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the ground-state and finite-temperature properties of the Kagome antiferromagnetic 3J model to characterize the nature and stability of putative Quantum Spin Liquid (QSL) phases.

**Main result.** The research identifies an intermediate QSL phase sandwiched between two magnetically ordered phases, and successfully connects the model's theoretical predictions (like specific heat anomalies) to measurable experimental signatures in 3J materials.

**Method.** The study employs a combination of advanced numerical techniques, including DMRG, Neural Quantum States (NQS) with Vision Transformers (ViT), and thermal tensor-network methods (XTRG and tanTRG).

**Model / system.** The system is the Kagome antiferromagnetic 3J Model, designed for the candidate Dirac QSL material YCu3(OH)6Br2[Br1-x(OH)x]. The Hamiltonian involves three inequivalent antiferromagnetic couplings (J1, J2, J7).

**Key observables.** Specific heat (Cv/T), static spin structure factors S(k), and entanglement entropy Se(x).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The model is analyzed across various coupling regimes (J1, J2, J7) and compares results to experimental measurements across different temperature scales.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed thermal and structural signatures are robust indicators for distinguishing between ordered, QSL, and KSL phases, and that the ML/tensor network methods accurately capture the system's physics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the ground-state phase diagram, static spin structure factors S(k) distinguishing phases, and comparisons of calculated specific heat (Cv/T) against experimental data.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the model using multiple computational tools: first, establishing the phase diagram using DMRG/NQS; second, characterizing phases via S(k) and entanglement entropy; and finally, validating the findings by calculating and comparing finite-temperature specific heat using XTRG and tanTRG against real materials.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the ground-state and finite-temperature properties of the kagome antiferromagnetic Heisenberg model with three inequivalent couplings, dubbed the 3J model, which is designed for the candidate Dirac quantum spin liquid (QSL) material YCu$_3$(OH)$_6$Br$_2$[Br$_{1-x}$(OH)$_x$] (see, e.g., Zeng et al., 2024). Employing large-scale density-matrix renormalization group (DMRG) supplemented by neural quantum states (NQS) simulations, we identify an intermediate QSL phase between two magnetically ordered phases. We also find that this QSL is separated from the kagome spin liquid ground state at the isotropic limit. To establish a direct comparison with experiments, we compute the specific heat of the model by means of advanced exponential (XTRG) and tangent-space (tanTRG) thermal tensor-network methods. In the magnetically ordered phase, the specific heat over temperature exhibits a shoulder at a temperature that is a fraction of the coupling strength $J_{hex}$, which disappears in the QSL phase. These universal behaviors are consistent with the experimentally observed specific heat in 3J materials for both ordered and QSL candidate samples. Our work thus connects microscopic models with experimentally measurable signatures, exemplifying an integrated approach (see Meng et al., 2026) to understanding QSL phenomena in frustrated quantum magnets, with 3J materials serving as a representative case and providing a foundation for future studies.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that by creating a flux mismatch in stacked 2D materials, the destructive effect of interlayer tunneling can be circumvented. This allows for the electrical control and quantized transfer of topological invariants, such as the Chern number, leading to programmable Chern phases and novel metallic transport regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of using synthetic dimensions (bias/momentum space) to engineer topological transitions is highly relevant to understanding engineered quantum states in condensed matter systems, bridging concepts from topological insulators to controllable quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is how to electrically control topological electronic states in stacked 2D materials without the interlayer tunneling term destroying the necessary band crossings.

**Main result.** The authors show that flux mismatch between layers creates topologically obstructed tunneling, allowing for the controlled, quantized transfer of Chern numbers and the realization of programmable Chern phases.

**Method.** The mechanism is analyzed by projecting the Hamiltonian onto resonant multiplets, identifying topological obstructions (zeros in projected tunneling), and calculating the resulting Chern number transfer via Berry flux integration.

**Model / system.** The system is a Hofstadter bilayer, modeled as stacked 2D materials (like graphene/hBN) where the layers experience different magnetic fluxes ($\alpha_1 
eq \alpha_2$). The physics is governed by the interplay between interlayer tunneling and the magnetic flux mismatch.

**Key observables.** Chern number ($C$), Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}$), Berry flux ($\chi_j$), and the existence of Weyl monopoles in momentum-bias space.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Flux mismatch ($\alpha_1 
eq \alpha_2$), interlayer bias ($m$), and the relative strength of tunneling versus gap energies.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on projecting the system onto two resonant multiplets and assumes the accuracy of the decoupled-layer overlap approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the obstructed tunneling mechanism, the two spectral routes for Chern transfer (insulating vs. metallic), and the resulting continuous, nonquantized Hall response during topology changes.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem (tunneling gap closing), introduces the mechanism (topological obstruction due to flux mismatch), derives the consequences (Chern transfer, reentrant metal), and verifies the findings using multiple theoretical calculations (Berry flux, TKNN).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Stacked two-dimensional materials provide a promising platform for electrically controlling topological electronic states. However, tunneling between layers hybridizes their bands and removes the crossings needed to change topology. We show that this does not always have to be the case. Band crossings and associated Weyl points are topologically enforced in Hofstadter bilayers whose layers experience different magnetic fluxes. When resonant magnetic Bloch multiplets carry unequal Chern numbers, their projected tunneling is topologically obstructed and must vanish at isolated momenta. Sweeping the layer bias through these zeros creates synthetic Weyl monopoles in momentum-bias space that transfer the Chern mismatch. We demonstrate two consequences: a direct transition between insulating Chern phases and a reentrant compensated metal in which Lifshitz transitions bound the metallic window while internal Weyl events reconstruct the band topology. Consequently, the fixed-filling Hall response remains continuous and nonquantized even as integer Chern number is transferred between bands. Berry-flux, TKNN, and interface calculations independently verify the mechanism and its multichannel chiral signature. We outline realizations in Moire and anomalous-Hall heterostructures, establishing flux mismatch as an experimentally accessible route to electrically programmable Chern phases and chiral transport.

</details>

### [Full-Rank Noise Forbids Long-Range Entanglement Swapping](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11021v1)

**Authors:** Xuan Du Trinh, Nicholas Pardave, Angie Huang, Xiangyi Meng, Nengkun Yu  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.11021v1>

Tracing out the measured qubits leaves the state ρN of the boundary pair A0BN, in which each outcome enters as the filter map Mi(X) = M ∗ i XM T i . The outcomes |Ψi⟩need not be Bell states or maximally entangled. The states are prepared under one- sided, rank-2 amplitude-damping (AD) and rank-4 depolariz- ing (DP) channels respectively on IBM’s Heron R3 processor (ibm boston), with an initial concurrence C(AD) ≈C(DP) ≈ 0.7. The ratio monotonically decays to zero (solid), signaling the sudden death of C(DP) as the theory predicts (dashed). **Summary.** This work establishes a fundamental difference in entanglement persistence based on the rank of noise in quantum repeater links. Full-rank noise inevitably leads to a finite 'entanglement horizon,' while rank-deficient links can sustain entanglement indefinitely through specific postselection strategies. This has implications for designing robust quantum networks.

**Why it may be interesting.** The distinction between full-rank and low-rank noise provides a powerful, physically motivated criterion for the feasibility of long-distance quantum communication, linking quantum information theory to the structure of quantum channels.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether long-range entanglement can be established using quantum repeaters when the elementary links are corrupted by full-rank noise, especially under adaptive postselection.

**Main result.** Full-rank noise imposes a finite 'entanglement horizon' beyond which entanglement swapping fails, whereas rank-deficient links can sustain entanglement indefinitely on specific postselected branches.

**Method.** The authors use the Choi operator formalism and analyze the decay of the end-to-end state by comparing the behavior of full-rank versus low-rank link channels.

**Model / system.** The system models quantum repeaters extending entanglement over noisy two-qubit links, analyzed using concepts from matrix-product density operators in many-body physics.

**Key observables.** Concurrence, the entanglement horizon, and the network 'score' $\beta(P)$ are key observables used to quantify entanglement decay.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The link rank (full vs. low), the noise parameter $\eta$, and the depth $N$ of the repeater chain are crucial parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on reducing the full-rank problem to an isotropic chain model, and some derivations use approximations for small noise/storage loads.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 compares the decay of concurrence for full-rank (DP) versus rank-deficient (AD) channels, showing the theoretical distinction between the two noise types.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the finite horizon for full-rank links (Theorem 1) to constructing low-rank counterexamples, and finally analyzing the role of storage and purification in maintaining entanglement.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum repeaters extend entanglement by swapping noisy elementary links. We prove that full-rank noise forbids this at long range: for any entangled full-rank two-qubit link, there is a finite depth beyond which no end-to-end entanglement can be established regardless of the measurement outcomes on intermediate qubits, even under any adaptive postselected strategy. This limit is set by one spectral parameter of the link, giving a no-go criterion for repeater routing. In contrast, we construct link state families of rank three and rank two that admit postselected measurement outcome branches of exponentially small probability but with strictly positive concurrence at every finite depth. Swapping experiments on a superconducting processor show that links of equal initial concurrence but different rank behave differently under postselected swapping. In the language of many-body physics, the chain is a matrix-product density operator, and full-rank bonds forbid long-range localizable entanglement, while rank-deficient bonds can sustain it.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a geometric Ginzburg-Landau theory to explain Charge Density Wave (CDW) formation in materials like TMDs. It shows that quantum geometry provides essential, previously uncaptured contributions to the phase transition free energy. The resulting theory yields robust criteria that successfully predict CDW ordering wavevectors where conventional electronic models fail.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit incorporation of quantum geometry into a phase transition theory provides a powerful, generalizable tool for understanding how subtle electronic structure effects dictate macroscopic material properties like CDW formation, which is relevant to many strongly correlated systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Deriving a geometric Ginzburg-Landau theory to accurately describe Charge Density Wave (CDW) formation and commensurability transitions, especially in systems where standard electronic criteria fail.

**Main result.** The theory establishes that quantum geometry plays an essential role, providing a general criterion for CDW formation that successfully predicts ordering wavevectors in TMDs where simpler models fail.

**Method.** The authors derive an effective Ginzburg-Landau functional by integrating out phonon degrees of freedom from an electron-phonon Hamiltonian, leading to modified susceptibility tensors.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on Charge Density Waves (CDWs) in Transition Metal Dichalcogenides (TMDs) with hexagonal lattices, utilizing a Ginzburg-Landau framework expanded to include higher-order geometric terms.

**Key observables.** The CDW driver ($\Theta_Q$), the quadratic susceptibility tensor ($\chi$), and the coefficients of the GL functional ($\Pi_Q, |\gamma_Q|, eta_Q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The wavevector $Q$, the lattice symmetry ($C_3$), and the relative strength of quantum geometric enhancements versus bare susceptibility.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the coupling function $g_q$ is flat over relevant $q$ states to isolate electronic contributions, and that the system can be reduced to a scalar order parameter due to susceptibility anisotropy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometric mechanism for CDW formation using three symmetry-related wavevectors in hexagonal systems, and plot the CDW driver ($\Theta_Q$) for various TMDs, showing geometric enhancements selecting the correct wavevector.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from deriving the geometric GL theory for CDW instability, establishing the general criterion, benchmarking it on TMDs, and refining the theory by including higher-order terms (like sextic terms) to resolve discrepancies in the predicted ordering wavevector.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The concept of quantum geometry has recently led to reinvigorated insights in a wide range of fields including physical responses, superconductivity, and optical transitions, with effects most pronounced in systems with nearly flat dispersion. Here, we show that it plays an essential role in charge density wave formation (CDW) -- an important physical phenomenon that is responsible for driving various sharp changes in material transport properties including metal-insulator transitions. We derive an effective Ginzburg-Landau theory including uncharted contributions and, as a highlight, discover a general criterion for both CDW formation and commensurability transitions where underlying electron-phonon interactions manifest purely as electronic quantum geometric enhancements/suppressions. We benchmark our framework in a class of transition-metal dichalcogenides and resolve a longstanding puzzle where well-established purely kinetic CDW criteria fail in describing the correct ordering wavevector. Besides rendering robust criteria and fundamental insights that are immediately relevant to several experimental charge ordering systems, our theory can also be applied directly to other phonon-mediated phases such as superconductivity, and can be used as an important tool to explore the interplay between various such states. More generally, our framework provides a recipe for investigating the role of quantum geometry in phase transitions.

</details>

**Authors:** Bishnu P. Belbase, Arjun Unnikrishnan, Piyush Chhallare, Mohan B. Neupane, Eun Sang Choi, Sebastian Erdmann, Muhammad U. Akbar, Mamoun Hemmida, H. -A. Krug von Nidda, Philipp Gegenwart, Arnab Banerjee

**Summary.** This paper experimentally investigates TlYbS2, a triangular-lattice antiferromagnet candidate for quantum spin liquid physics. Using multiple measurements, the authors confirm strong magnetic anisotropy and observe a specific heat anomaly suggesting weak magnetic ordering. The results place the material near the boundary between magnetic order and quantum disorder, providing key experimental data for understanding frustrated magnetism.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides detailed experimental evidence on how geometric frustration and crystal anisotropy drive the system toward or away from a quantum spin liquid state, which is a central topic in modern condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the competition between magnetic long-range order and quantum spin liquid (QSL) behavior in geometrically frustrated triangular-lattice antiferromagnets.

**Main result.** The material exhibits a weak thermodynamic anomaly near 530 mK, and its low-temperature specific heat follows $C_m \propto T^{1.8}$, suggesting it lies near the boundary between weak magnetic order and a quantum-disordered state.

**Method.** The research employed a comprehensive set of experimental techniques, including DC magnetization, AC susceptibility, electron spin resonance (ESR), and specific heat measurements across a wide temperature range.

**Model / system.** The physical system is single-crystal TlYbS2, a Yb-based delafossite compound forming quasi-two-dimensional triangular layers of Yb3+ ions, whose low-energy magnetism is modeled by an effective spin-1/2 Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Specific heat ($C_m \propto T^{1.8}$), magnetization plateaus (e.g., 1/3 plateau), and the temperature/field dependence of magnetic susceptibility.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Easy-plane magnetic anisotropy ($g_{\perp}/g_{\parallel} \approx 6.9$), and the critical temperature/field associated with the observed anomalies ($\sim 530$ mK).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The low-energy physics is effectively described by a spin-1/2 Hamiltonian, and the comparison to other Yb-based compounds helps map out the phase diagram.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structure, the magnetic anisotropy observed in magnetization curves under different field orientations, and the temperature dependence of the specific heat, highlighting the anomaly near 530 mK.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first characterizing the crystal structure, then presenting measurements of magnetic properties (magnetization, susceptibility, ESR) to establish anisotropy, followed by specific heat measurements to probe the low-temperature magnetic excitations and phase transitions under applied fields.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Geometrically frustrated triangular-lattice antiferromagnets exhibit a delicate competition between magnetic order and quantum spin liquid (QSL) behavior, with the Yb-based delafossite family $A$Yb$X_2$ providing a structurally clean platform for exploring this physics. Here, we report a comprehensive study of single-crystal TlYbS$_2$ using DC magnetization, AC susceptibility, electron spin resonance (ESR), and specific heat measurements extending from room temperature to the millikelvin regime. Single-crystal X-ray diffraction confirms a trigonal $R\bar{3}m$ structure comprising well-separated triangular layers of Yb$^{3+}$ ions with no detectable site disorder. At zero field, a weak thermodynamic anomaly is observed near $530$ mK, which may indicate the onset of a weakly ordered state similar to that reported in KYbSe$_2$. Below $300$ mK, the zero-field magnetic specific heat follows $C_{\rm m}\propto T^{1.8}$, close to a quadratic temperature dependence, in contrast to the linear temperature dependence reported for the sister compound TlYbSe$_2$, which has been described in terms of the interplay between spinons and thermally excited gauge-flux excitations. Magnetization and ESR measurements establish pronounced easy-plane magnetic anisotropy, with $g_\perp/g_\parallel \approx 6.9$. The anomaly near $530$ mK exhibits a strongly anisotropic response to magnetic field. For $H\perp c$, it remains visible up to approximately $2$ T and continuously evolves toward a field-induced ordered phase above approximately $2.5$ T, followed by a sequence of field-induced phases that includes a $1/3$ magnetization plateau between approximately $5$ and $8$ T and full polarization near $17$ T. In contrast, for $H\parallel c$, the anomaly weakens and is suppressed near $3$ T, consistent with the recently reported confinement--deconfinement transition from an ordered state to a field-induced QSL.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a novel quantum tomography technique for studying spin correlations in hadronic decays ($\Lambda V$ pairs). It addresses the fundamental challenge of reconstructing quantum states when experimental measurements only access a subset of parameters. The key achievement is establishing a rigorous, computable criterion—checking the minimum eigenvalue of the reconstructed density matrix—to certify entanglement despite the missing information.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the physical system is particle/nuclear physics, the methodology—developing rigorous criteria for entanglement detection from severely incomplete quantum measurements—is highly relevant to open quantum systems and quantum information theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper formulates and applies a Qubit–Qutrit Quantum Tomography (QQQT) technique to study spin correlations in hadronic final states, specifically $\Lambda\phi$ and $\Lambda K^{*0}$ systems.

**Main result.** The authors establish exact criteria for certifying entanglement from the experimentally incomplete density matrix, reducing the problem to checking the positive semi-definiteness of the reconstructed state ($ho_{	ext{meas}}$).

**Method.** The technique involves reconstructing the density matrix from a limited set of measurable spin parameters, utilizing partial time-reversal operations to define the measurable state $ho_{	ext{meas}}$.

**Model / system.** The system under study is the $\Lambda V$ pair ($\Lambda$ is spin-1/2, $V$ is spin-1, treated as a qutrit), residing in a $2 	imes 3$ Hilbert space. The analysis compares this to the fully measurable $\Lambdaar{\Lambda}$ system.

**Key observables.** The minimum eigenvalue ($\lambda_{\min}$) of the partially transposed density matrix ($ho^T_B$), which serves as the diagnostic tool for entanglement certification.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The 23 experimentally accessible spin parameters, contrasted with the 12 inaccessible parameters related to the meson dipole sector.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the missing parameters are due to kinematic limitations (the meson dipole sector), and the core theoretical tools (Theorems 1 and 2) are applied based on this structure.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 defines the coordinate system for the $\Lambda V$ pair rest frame, and Figure 2 numerically verifies the theorems by showing the sign of $\lambda_{\min}(ho_{	ext{meas}})$ for separable versus entangled states.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the challenge of incomplete tomography in $\Lambda V$ systems, develops the QQQT formalism, proves theorems relating separability/entanglement to the sign of $\lambda_{\min}(ho_{	ext{meas}})$, and finally compares the results to the fully measurable $\Lambdaar{\Lambda}$ system.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum-information observables have emerged in recent years as new tools in nuclear and particle physics, from entanglement in top-quark pairs to spin correlations in $Λ\barΛ$ production. Extending these studies to unequal-spin hadronic final states poses a fundamental challenge: the $6\times6$ density matrix of a qubit-qutrit system contains 35 independent spin parameters, but the decays of $ΛV$ pairs, with $V=φ$ or $K^{*0}$, provide access to only 23 due to the hidden vector polarization from the strong decay. In this Letter, we formulate a qubit-qutrit quantum tomography (QQQT) technique for these spin-$\tfrac{1}{2}\otimes1$ systems and establish exact criteria for entanglement certification from the \textit{incomplete} density matrix. Compared with the $Λ\barΛ$ system, QQQT of $Λφ$ and $ΛK^{*0}$ provides a new probe of nonperturbative QCD hadronization, enabling a direct comparison of the spin evolution of entangled quark pairs produced from the vacuum as they hadronize into a baryon or a vector meson.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a novel, self-guided protocol to certify quantum nonlocality in complex quantum networks. It uses an optimization routine (CSPSA) driven by local Pauli shadows to find optimal measurement settings that maximize the violation of nonlinear Bell inequalities. This two-stage approach allows for robust certification of nonclassical correlations even when dealing with noise or limited experimental resources.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a highly practical, minimal-assumption framework for testing fundamental quantum properties (nonlocality) in complex, realistic quantum hardware setups, which is crucial for quantum network development.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of certifying quantum nonlocality in complex quantum networks, moving beyond standard Bell inequalities to nonlinear constraints.

**Main result.** A self-guided, two-stage protocol is introduced that efficiently optimizes measurement settings to maximize the violation of network Bell inequalities, even for noisy or non-maximally entangled states.

**Method.** The protocol uses the Complex Simultaneous Perturbation Stochastic Approximation (CSPSA) algorithm to variationally optimize measurement settings, employing local Pauli classical shadows during the search phase, followed by a direct, shadow-free evaluation for the final certificate.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum network involving multiple independent sources distributing systems among several distant parties, exemplified by the triangle network structure.

**Key observables.** Violation of nonlinear network Bell inequalities (e.g., Wagon-Wheel inequality) relative to a classical bound.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of parties ($n$), the structure of the network, and the noise level of the quantum states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol assumes a two-stage structure separating a device-dependent search (using shadows) from a certificate based only on observed statistics and causal structure.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the cyclic structure of the triangular network connected by three independent sources (SAB, SBC, SAC) linking nodes A, B, and C.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework by contrasting classical and quantum correlation constraints in networks. It then details the self-guided, two-stage optimization protocol using CSPSA and Pauli shadows. Finally, it validates the method by simulating it on the triangle network using the Wagon-Wheel inequality.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bell's theorem shows that quantum theory is incompatible with local hidden-variable models. In recent years, research on nonlocality has moved beyond Bell's original scenario to quantum networks, where multiple independent sources distribute physical systems among distant parties, giving rise to correlations certified by nonlinear rather than standard Bell inequalities. Here, we introduce a self-guided protocol that variationally optimizes each party's measurement to maximize the violation of a network Bell inequality, with the violation evaluated efficiently at each step via local Pauli classical shadows and the search driven by the Complex Simultaneous Perturbation Stochastic Approximation (CSPSA) algorithm. Once converged, the measurement settings it returns are implemented directly and the inequality is re-evaluated without shadows. This two-stage structure separates a device-dependent search from a certificate that depends only on the observed statistics and on the causal structure of the network. We validate the protocol by numerically simulating it on the triangle network using the Wagon-Wheel inequality, recovering the violation achieved by the Fritz distribution, and extending the certification to non-maximally entangled and noisy states.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces 'Statistical Symmetry Release,' a rigorous framework for deciding when to relax symmetry constraints in quantum learning models based on empirical data. It combines advanced quantum estimation techniques with statistical hypothesis testing to provide certified decisions on model complexity. This is vital for building reliable quantum machine learning algorithms that can adapt their structure based on measurement outcomes.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, theoretically grounded tool for model selection in quantum machine learning, which is crucial for designing practical quantum algorithms that can adapt to noisy or incomplete physical measurements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental challenge of determining when finite quantum data and measurements justify relaxing hard symmetry constraints imposed on quantum learning models.

**Main result.** The authors developed a statistically rigorous framework that certifies the optimal, data-justified relaxation of symmetry constraints, significantly outperforming existing estimation methods on test cases.

**Method.** The methodology combines global signal detection (using specialized quantum gates like the twirl-swap gate) with local improvement analysis, all underpinned by rigorous statistical guarantees like confidence bounds and independent validation streams.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum learning framework constrained by a symmetry group G, parameterized by an equivariant index. The analysis is applied to systems like the eight-qubit Ising model.

**Key observables.** Release gradient, loss-corrected double-commutator matrix, task signal over breaking observables, and the ratio of required measurement shots.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits (e.g., eight-qubit Ising model), the size of the data streams ($D_{	ext{fit}}, D_{	ext{rel}}, D_{	ext{val}}$), and the required shot count for certification.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The statistical guarantees rely on maintaining three independent data streams to avoid post-selection bias, and the local improvement analysis assumes specific measurement access models.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes detail quantitative comparisons, such as the shot count reduction factor (6300 times fewer shots) achieved by the shared-record estimator versus scalar estimators.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds conceptually from defining the symmetry constraint problem, developing global and local signal estimation techniques, establishing statistical guarantees using multiple data streams, and concluding with concrete performance demonstrations on physical models like the Ising chain.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hard symmetry constraints reduce model complexity, but can also erase label information. Statistical symmetry release determines when finite data and quantum measurements justify relaxing such a constraint, which directions to open, and how far to move. We connect global signal detection to local, loss-dependent improvement. A two-copy twirl--swap gate estimates task information in the symmetry-breaking complement with a dimension-independent copy count under paired-state and group-unitary access; reweighting the same records resolves representation sectors. An exact duality distinguishes this Hilbert--Schmidt signal from the larger signal accessible to bounded-outcome readouts. Local improvement is governed by the release gradient and a loss-corrected double-commutator matrix. Simultaneous confidence bounds convert empirical direction selection into certified descent, using either shared Pauli measurements or scalar probes with state-independent truncation bounds. Gaussian testing lower bounds quantify the cost of searching over unknown directions in the calibrated local experiment. Independent validation controls adaptively generated models, and a fast squared-loss bound preserves the approximation--estimation rate of a nested release path. On an eight-qubit Ising model, shared measurements certify release with 6300 times fewer shots than the specified scalar estimator on the tested budget grids. Quotient quantum natural gradient then controls parameter redundancy during training. Together, these results turn symmetry relaxation into a statistically justified model-selection decision.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the Quantum Composition Paradox, which questions whether quantum probabilities derived from sequential unitary steps are mathematically consistent. It rigorously proves that perfect composition only holds for specific 'unitary monomial' operations. The work quantifies this failure using a Born-composition defect, establishing a foundation for quantum theories of music and linking the paradox to computational complexity theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The deep connection drawn between quantum process composition, information theory, and computational complexity (PromiseBQP-completeness) provides a novel theoretical lens for understanding decoherence and measurement back-action in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the Quantum Composition Paradox: the inconsistency between probability laws derived from individual unitary steps (Born rule) and the probability laws obtained by composing these steps over time.

**Main result.** Universal composition (where the Born kernel composition equals the composition of Born kernels) is restricted to unitary monomials, and the failure of this composition can be quantified by a Born-composition defect.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Born kernel $B(U)$ as a stochastic matrix, defining a composition defect $J_C(V,U)$ to diagnose when the chronological composition fails to match the composition of individual step probabilities.

**Model / system.** The system is quantum evolution described by unitary operators $U \in U(d)$ acting on a $d$-dimensional Hilbert space. The context involves analyzing the composition of these unitaries through their associated Born probability kernels.

**Key observables.** The Born-composition defect ($
u_C(V,U)$), conditional mutual information $I(S;Y | X)$, and the comparison between endpoint-checked and stepwise-checked probabilities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $d$ of the Hilbert space, and the number of fully mixing steps, which is bounded by $d$ for boundary-stable sequences.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes a context $C$ defining the measurement basis, and it distinguishes the required composition law from weaker operational tests.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the double-slit experiment as a failure of quantum-classical composition. Figure 2 shows the commuting diagram required for quantum-classical consistency.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the paradox, classifies the conditions for universal composition (unitary monomials), provides necessary and sufficient criteria for specific qubit/qutrit steps, and concludes by linking the failure of composition to quantum music theory and computational complexity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum theory does not generally permit the probability laws obtained from individual unitary steps by the Born rule to be sewn into a consistent genealogy; we classify the exceptions and show that faithful composition can hold from an initial boundary yet fail after an internal restart. For finite-dimensional, composition-closed unitary families, universal composition holds exactly for unitary monomials, the phase-dressed permutations whose Born kernels realize reversible deterministic state machines. Prescribed sequences evade this obstruction. We classify all pairs of qubit unitary steps, give a necessary-and-sufficient criterion for pairs of qutrit unitary steps, and prove that a boundary-stable unitary sequence on a $d$-dimensional Hilbert space contains at most $d$ fully mixing steps, with equality in every prime dimension. We also construct arbitrarily long genuinely mixing sequences that compose from their initial boundary but fail after an internal restart, and a qutrit-controlled two-qubit realization with active interference. We define a Born--Chapman--Kolmogorov current that vanishes exactly when coherent and stepwise-checked endpoint laws agree, together with an associated measure of how many bits the endpoint reveals about the intermediate checking schedule. This state-machine connection provides a foundation for a quantum theory of music, in which unitary operations are notes and temporal boundaries are cues. Musical-transition prediction is proved PromiseBQP-complete, and the stepwise patterns that preserve a genealogy specify rules for rhythm, whereas the exceptional failure of that genealogy from an internal cue is the quantum music paradox.

</details>
