**Summary.** This paper investigates the topological band structure of magnetic excitons in $	ext{KTb}_3	ext{F}_{10}$ using inelastic neutron scattering. By analyzing the observed 'pinch point' singularities, the authors demonstrate that the physics can be accurately modeled by a quadratic band touching Hamiltonian analogous to a Luttinger semi-metal. This provides a general theoretical framework for understanding topological features in magnetic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection drawn between the topological features (pinch points) in magnetic excitations and the Luttinger semi-metal provides a general, symmetry-protected model applicable to various magnetic systems, which is relevant for understanding emergent quantum states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand the symmetry and topology of band structures in crystalline solids, specifically extending this analysis to magnetic excitations in insulating magnets.

**Main result.** The research reveals unambiguous pinch point features in the exciton dispersion, which are shown to be a generic consequence of a quadratic band touching model related to the Luttinger semi-metal.

**Method.** The study combines high-resolution inelastic neutron scattering (INS) experiments with theoretical modeling using Crystal Field Excitations (CEF) and long-range dipolar Hamiltonians.

**Model / system.** The physical system is $	ext{KTb}_3	ext{F}_{10}$, an octochlore lattice exhibiting magnetic excitations. The theoretical model involves the long-range magnetostatic dipolar interaction, which is effectively described by a quadratic band touching point Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Dispersive bosonic excitons, 'pinch point features' in the scattering intensity, and spectral weight anomalies observed in iso-energy maps.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperature (1.5 K to 25 K), the wavevector $\mathbf{Q}=400$, and the truncation range of the dipolar interaction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dominant coupling mechanism is assumed to be the long-range magnetostatic dipolar interaction, and the physics is captured by a minimal quadratic touching point model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate experimental excitation spectra and calculated iso-E maps, clearly showing the characteristic pinched structures and their dependence on energy and scattering plane.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the general problem of band topology in magnets, presenting experimental INS data showing anomalies, theoretically modeling the system using dipole-dipole interactions, and finally demonstrating that a simplified quadratic model successfully reproduces the observed topological features.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding the symmetry and topology of band structures in crystalline solids has been among the defining themes of a generation of condensed matter physicists, with implications across many different platforms including electronic bands, photonic crystals, phonons and magnetic excitations. In the latter systems, there is usually magnetic long-range order and time reversal symmetry breaking. Here we further enrich the possible band structures available to magnetic systems by examining dispersive crystal field excitations above a ground state that is a simple product of singlets. In these systems, there is an interplay of magnetic interactions and band topology with both crystal and time reversal symmetries. Using high-resolution neutron spectroscopy we reveal the dispersion relations of bosonic excitons in KTb$_{3}$F$_{10}$, an experimental realization of the octochlore lattice. We show that there are pinch point features in the scattering intensity. The dominant coupling in this system is the long-range magnetostatic dipolar interaction, whose singularity at the zone center is passed onto the scattering intensity. However, the pinch points survive when the range of the dipoles is truncated to finite shell number in the form of a three-band quadratic band touching protected by cubic symmetry. This model, which is related to the Luttinger semi-metal, provides a general long wavelength model for the emergence of pinch point scattering intensity encompassing also the true long-range dipolar model.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses two-color high-harmonic spectroscopy on a 1D Mott insulator to observe ultrafast, sub-cycle dynamics of doublon-holon pairs. By measuring the chirp and temporal asymmetry of emitted harmonics, the authors confirm that the process is governed by d-h recombination and quantify the subsequent electronic dephasing. This provides a novel experimental window into non-equilibrium many-body physics in strongly correlated materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of HHG spectroscopy to probe correlated electron dynamics provides a powerful, non-perturbative tool for studying non-equilibrium quantum phenomena, bridging concepts from quantum optics and condensed matter physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to directly measure and understand strongly driven, sub-optical-cycle dynamics within correlated materials, specifically focusing on doublon-holon recombination.

**Main result.** The experiment reveals coherent doublon-holon dynamics characterized by a positive chirp in high-harmonic emission, and quantifies ultrafast dephasing through harmonic order-dependent temporal asymmetry.

**Method.** The research employs two-color high-harmonic spectroscopy to probe the time-domain emission profile of even harmonics, complemented by semiclassical modeling of doublon-holon trajectories.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a one-dimensional Mott insulator ($	ext{Ca}_{0.4}	ext{Sr}_{0.6}	ext{CuO}_2$), whose dynamics are modeled using a semiclassical approach based on the Hubbard model and Bethe-ansatz dispersion.

**Key observables.** High-harmonic emission chirp, temporal skewness ($\gamma_q$), and the maximum doublon-holon separation ($x_{max}$) as a function of harmonic order.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sub-optical-cycle timescales, the two-color phase delay, and the characteristic dephasing length scale ($\sim 0.5$ lattice sites).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on semiclassical models for d-h trajectories, which neglect spinon dynamics and non-local interactions; the weak second harmonic is assumed to only perturb the process.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spectral modulation of even harmonics versus two-color delay, showing shifts in peak emission time for higher orders, and compare theoretical semiclassical trajectories (short vs. long) with experimental chirp measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the experimental technique using two-color HHG to access sub-cycle dynamics, presents the semiclassical model for d-h motion, and then correlates the observed harmonic chirp and temporal asymmetry with the predicted recombination dynamics and subsequent dephasing.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Solid-state high-harmonic spectroscopy is becoming an emerging tool for probing nonequilibrium many-body dynamics. Yet, direct measurements of strongly driven, sub-optical-cycle dynamics in correlated materials during high-harmonic emission remain largely unexplored. Here, we measure high-harmonic emission chirp in a prototypical one-dimensional Mott insulator, which encodes strongly driven doublon-holon dynamics at sub-optical-cycle timescales. We observe a positive chirp for above band gap harmonics, indicating that high harmonics are dominated by doublon-holon recombinations. We further show a harmonic order-dependent dephasing, which can be understood through different doublon-holon excursion distances associated with each harmonic. These results reveal coherent doublon-holon dynamics and their ultrafast dephasing in Mott insulators, which is relevant to other nonequilibrium light-induced phenomena, such as Floquet engineering.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes how placing orientational spin systems on non-orientable manifolds, like the Klein bottle, fundamentally changes their statistical mechanics compared to standard surfaces like the torus. The key finding is that non-orientability causes 'topological caging,' which limits orientational fluctuations to a finite value, a robust effect seen in both passive and active matter models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of topological constraints fundamentally altering equilibrium dynamics (caging) without requiring an explicit external field is a rich theoretical topic relevant to understanding complex phase behavior in condensed matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the non-orientable topology of a domain, specifically the Klein bottle, alters the statistical properties and orientational fluctuations of many-body systems compared to standard orientable domains like the torus.

**Main result.** Non-orientability eliminates global rotational soft modes and induces 'topological caging' in passive systems, leading to finite saturation of orientational fluctuations, a state absent on the torus.

**Method.** The authors employ stochastic microscopic equations, mean-field theory, and spin-wave approximations, analyzing the system's dynamics by mapping topological constraints onto non-local symmetry relations on an orientable double-cover.

**Model / system.** The models include the passive XY spin model and active self-propelled particle systems (like the active Ising model) whose orientations are constrained on a Klein bottle geometry, contrasting with the torus geometry.

**Key observables.** Global orientation fluctuation $\langle\delta\Theta^2(t)angle$, polar order parameter $|M|$, and the spin polarization distribution $P(m_x, m_y)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The comparison between the torus and Klein bottle topologies, and the role of the non-local symmetry relations imposed by non-orientability.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on the spin-wave approximation for small angle variations and the mathematical mapping of topological constraints to symmetry relations on a double-cover.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the double-cover representation of the Klein bottle and compare the time evolution of orientation fluctuations ($\langle\delta\Phi^2angle$) on both the torus and the Klein bottle, showing saturation on the latter.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the topological constraints via the double-cover, then analyzing the dynamics of passive XY models to show topological caging, and finally extending these findings to active particle models like the active Ising spin model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the statistical properties of passive and active many-body systems with orientational degrees of freedom on non-orientable domains. By rephrasing topological constraints as non-local symmetry relations on an orientable double-cover, we show that non-orientability eliminates global rotational soft modes without acting like an external field. In a passive XY model, this results in topological caging, where orientational fluctuations that exhibit conventional diffusive behavior on a torus saturate on a Klein bottle to a finite value that we compute exactly in the thermodynamic limit. In models of active self-propelled particles with orientational degrees of freedom, topological caging persists despite continuously changing interaction neighborhoods. In an active Ising spin model, non-orientability enforces the coexistence of ordered anti-parallel domains with vanishing global polar order, a state that is absent on orientable domains.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the recurrence and transience of a random walk whose drift term is time and position-dependent ($ho x^\alpha/t^eta$). By employing advanced stochastic methods like martingale theory, the authors establish complete classification criteria for the process's long-term behavior under weaker assumptions than previously required. This provides a powerful mathematical tool for understanding diffusion processes in complex, non-stationary environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core subject is random walk theory, the rigorous mathematical techniques involving martingale analysis and boundary crossing probabilities are highly relevant to analyzing non-equilibrium dynamics and stochastic processes in condensed matter physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the recurrence or transience of a random walk process whose conditional drift depends on both time and position in the form $ho x^\alpha/t^eta$.

**Main result.** It establishes comprehensive recurrence and transience criteria for the process across various parameter regimes of $\alpha$ and $eta$, improving upon previous results by weakening assumptions.

**Method.** The analysis employs martingale theory, supermartingale arguments, and the construction of specific stopping times to analyze the long-term behavior of the process.

**Model / system.** The model is a discrete-time, $\mathbb{R}^+$-valued random walk $(X_t)_{t\in\mathbb{Z}^+}$ whose conditional drift is governed by the term $ho x^\alpha/t^eta$. The analysis focuses on the process's tendency to return to a bounded region or escape to infinity.

**Key observables.** Recurrence (hitting a point infinitely often) and Transience (diverging to infinity), analyzed via the process's position $X_t$ and related quantities like $Y_t = X_t^2/t$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The parameters are $\alpha$ and $eta$ governing the drift, with critical regimes defined by $2eta - \alpha = 1$ and the general scope being $-1 < \alpha < 1$ and $eta \ge 0$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proofs rely on hypotheses regarding the boundedness of conditional second moments of increments (H1) and lower bounds on these moments (H2), and the process is analyzed without assuming the Markov property.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the argument by first establishing recurrence criteria using supermartingale properties (Theorem 1.1), followed by proving transience criteria using bounds on hitting probabilities and constructing sequences of stopping times (Theorem 1.2).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Menshikov and Volkov [Electron. J. Probab. 13 (2008)] studied recurrence and transience of a class of Markovian random walks on $\mathbb R_+$ whose conditional drift depends on both time and position and is of order $ρx^αt^{-β}$ with $ρ>0$. The case on the critical line $2β-α=1$, with $α\in(-1,1)\setminus\{0\}$, remained open. We prove recurrence in this remaining case. Furthermore, we establish recurrence and transience criteria that complete the classification for $-1<α<1$ and $β\ge 0$, without assuming the Markov property and under weaker assumptions on the increments than those imposed by Menshikov and Volkov.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, amortized neural variational framework using Transformers to calculate thermodynamic properties of disordered spin systems. By training once on the disorder ensemble rather than per realization, it overcomes the prohibitive computational cost of traditional methods. This breakthrough enables accurate, scalable simulations of complex, frustrated many-body systems previously inaccessible to variational inference.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical spin systems, the methodology of using deep generative models (Transformers) to efficiently sample complex, high-dimensional probability distributions conditioned on system parameters (disorder) is highly relevant for simulating quantum many-body states or quantum field theories where disorder is present.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is evaluating quenched thermodynamic observables in disordered spin systems, which requires averaging over the disorder realization itself, a task difficult for standard variational methods.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a universal neural variational framework that amortizes inference across the entire disorder ensemble, allowing for zero-shot sampling on unseen disorder realizations.

**Method.** They employ an encoder-decoder Transformer architecture where the encoder processes the disorder couplings $\{J\}$ to generate site embeddings, which condition a causal Transformer decoder to sample the spin configuration $s$.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on disordered spin systems, specifically the 2D Edwards-Anderson model and the random-bond Ising model, governed by a Hamiltonian involving quenched random couplings $J_{ij}$.

**Key observables.** Variational free energy, Binder cumulant crossings, and ground-state energies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta$), system size ($L$), and the nature of the disorder (bimodal vs. Gaussian couplings).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that the disorder couplings can be processed by the encoder to create a universal representation $\{h_i\}$ that governs the sampling process.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the conceptual flow of the disorder-conditioned neural Boltzmann sampling framework, showing training dynamics, thermal generalization, and extrapolation across system sizes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the computational bottleneck of per-instance training for disordered systems. It then introduces the amortized Transformer architecture, details its training on various disorder types and temperatures, and validates its performance by calculating key physical observables like the Binder cumulant.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Statistical physics extracts macroscopic laws by averaging over the many microscopic degrees of freedom of a system. Disordered systems demand a second and far harder average, one over the quenched randomness itself. The classic analytical routes, the replica and cavity methods, become uncontrolled outside mean-field or tree-like limits, and conventional numerical algorithms like parallel tempering require expensive, independent equilibration for every disorder realization. In this work, we introduce a universal neural variational framework that amortizes inference across the disorder ensemble, eliminating both the need for per-instance Markov chain equilibration and the cost of retraining instance-specific variational ansatzes. Built on an encoder-decoder Transformer architecture, after training once, it produces an explicit approximation to the Boltzmann distribution given previously unseen disorder realizations without further optimization. We validate this framework on 2D Edwards-Anderson models, and apply it to the random-bond Ising model, successfully capturing the Binder cumulant crossings near the Nishimori multicritical point. These results shift the object of variational inference from the single instance to the disorder ensemble, opening a route to frustrated many-body systems where instance-by-instance computation is prohibitive.

</details>

**Authors:** Raphael De Sousa, Partha Nandi, Francesco Petruccione  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.11350v1>

**Summary.** This work develops a systematic, analytical framework to translate the structure of continuum quantum operators into the discrete language of qubits using Walsh-Pauli representations. By employing generating functions, the authors determine the spectrum and resource cost associated with operators like the momentum operator, even when subjected to nonlinear deformations such as GUP kinematics. This offers a powerful preprocessing step for designing efficient quantum simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, analytical tool to estimate the quantum resources needed to simulate complex physical Hamiltonians (like those involving GUP) by analyzing the spectral structure of their momentum components.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how the structure of a continuum operator, such as the momentum operator, is faithfully represented and characterized within a finite qubit register.

**Main result.** A generating-function framework is developed to analytically determine the Walsh-Pauli spectrum of analytic functions of the JLP momentum operator, revealing exact support and parity selection rules.

**Method.** The core method involves mapping the continuum operator to a qubit-encoded operator, then using a generating function formalism to extract the Walsh-Pauli coefficients of the resulting function.

**Model / system.** The model centers on the Jordan-Lee-Preskill (JLP) construction, which discretizes continuum degrees of freedom onto a finite qubit register. A controlled application is the Generalized Uncertainty Principle (GUP) kinematics, treated as a nonlinear deformation of momentum.

**Key observables.** Walsh-Pauli spectrum, Pauli-weight structure, Walsh compressibility, and estimated quantum circuit resource costs (e.g., CNOT gates).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits ($n_q$) defining the finite register size, and the deformation parameters characterizing the GUP.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to diagonal functions of a single JLP momentum register, and the method provides an upper bound for circuit costs.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the entire process flow: continuum momentum $ightarrow$ JLP qubit encoding $ightarrow$ Analytic function $ightarrow$ Walsh-Pauli coefficients $ightarrow$ Spectrum/Resources.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the continuum-to-qubit mapping, developing the generating-function formalism for the spectrum, deriving selection rules (support/parity), and finally applying the framework to analyze physical deformations like GUP kinematics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

How does continuum operator structure appear in a finite qubit register? We address this question for analytic functions of diagonal operators represented in a binary basis, focusing on the Jordan--Lee--Preskill (JLP) momentum operator, by developing an operator-level generating-function framework for their Walsh-Pauli spectra. The construction determines the spectrum analytically and reveals exact support and parity selection rules, together with a nontrivial intra-shell hierarchy induced by the binary encoding. This provides a systematic characterization of Walsh compressibility and its relation to Pauli locality. As a controlled application, we consider generalized uncertainty-principle (GUP) kinematics as a tunable nonlinear odd deformation of momentum, showing how higher-order momentum terms redistribute spectral weight into progressively higher Pauli-weight sectors

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a comprehensive resource theory approach to quantify symmetry breaking in mixed quantum states. It establishes that the optimal conversion rate is governed not by a single measure, but by an entire continuous family of Quantum Fisher Information matrices. This reveals a fundamental difference between pure-state and mixed-state resource quantification.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, quantitative framework for understanding how symmetry constraints dictate the fundamental limits of quantum information processing, which has implications for characterizing non-equilibrium processes in many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide a complete, operational characterization for quantifying symmetry breaking in general mixed quantum states within the i.i.d. asymptotic limit.

**Main result.** It establishes a single-letter formula for the optimal conversion rate between arbitrary mixed states, determined by an entire one-parameter family of Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) matrices.

**Method.** The analysis uses quantum resource theories, extending quantum local asymptotic normality (QLAN) to complex unitary models and characterizing convertibility via the continuous QFI family.

**Model / system.** The study concerns quantum systems in finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces subjected to compact Lie group symmetries, analyzing the conversion of many copies of input states ($ho^{\otimes n}$) to output states ($ho'^{\otimes m}$).

**Key observables.** Asymmetry measure, Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) matrices (interpolating SLD and RLD), and the generalized quantum geometric tensor (QGT).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The asymptotic regime (i.i.d.), the parameter $q$ defining the QFI family, and the group structure of the symmetry $G$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the i.i.d. asymptotic regime, and the proof relies on extending QLAN to arbitrary rank unitary models.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 summarizes the findings, contrasting the single-measure characterization for pure-state conversion (using QGT) with the full QFI family required for general mixed-state conversion.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general problem of resource quantification, develops the necessary mathematical tools (like extended QLAN), derives the single-letter formula using the continuous QFI family, and then specializes the results to pure-state distillation and asymmetry activation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantifying properties of quantum states through the limits of their manipulation is a central goal of quantum resource theories. For symmetry breaking, the quantum geometric tensor governs asymptotic pure-state conversion, but a complete characterization for general mixed states has remained elusive.   Here we fully resolve this problem for finite-dimensional systems under compact Lie group symmetries in the i.i.d. asymptotic regime. Specifically, we establish a single-letter formula for the optimal conversion rate between arbitrary states, with vanishing trace-distance error, in the resource theory of asymmetry. The rate is determined by a one-parameter family of quantum Fisher information (QFI) matrices that interpolates between the symmetric- and right-logarithmic-derivative QFIs. No state-independent finite subset of this family suffices in general, even for $U(1)$ symmetry, revealing a qualitative distinction from pure-state conversion. Our formula further yields an exact formula for pure-state distillation rates in terms of the generalized quantum geometric tensor, characterizes asymptotically reversible interconversion, and identifies bound asymmetry for quantum clocks. Complementarity among different members of the QFI family also uncovers an activation mechanism for quantum clocks.   Our proof relies on two developments of independent interest. First, we extend quantum local asymptotic normality to unitary models with arbitrary rank and spectral degeneracy. Second, we characterize convertibility between quantum Gaussian shift models in terms of the same one-parameter family of QFIs. Together, these results provide an operational characterization of symmetry breaking for general quantum states in the i.i.d. asymptotic regime and reveal how distinct QFI constraints give rise to irreversibility and activation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes a lattice realization for quantifying timelike entanglement in QFT using spacetime density matrices. By analyzing the spectrum of the reduced density matrix, the authors show that entanglement entropy moments precisely match predictions from Lorentzian twist-operator correlation functions. This provides a powerful, microscopic tool for studying quantum correlations across time separations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous lattice foundation for timelike entanglement provides a concrete, computable framework for understanding non-local quantum correlations in time, which is crucial for understanding dynamics in open or time-dependent quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the concept of timelike entanglement in Quantum Field Theory (QFT) by analyzing reduced spacetime density matrices, extending entanglement measures beyond standard equal-time spatial cuts.

**Main result.** The authors provide a microscopic lattice realization for timelike entanglement, showing that Rényi entropy moments derived from the reduced density matrix match Lorentzian twist-operator correlation functions, even in regimes where reality is not guaranteed by causal disconnection.

**Method.** The analysis uses Gaussian diagonalization techniques applied to the generally non-Hermitian reduced spacetime density matrix, determining its spectrum via a characteristic polynomial, and relating its moments to correlation functions using the real-time replica construction.

**Model / system.** The study is performed on a free real scalar field, modeled on a lattice of coupled harmonic oscillators. The formalism is extended to analyze boundary effects (Periodic, Dirichlet, Neumann) and massive integrable QFTs.

**Key observables.** Reduced spacetime density matrix ($T_{A_0 B_t}$), Rényi entropy ($S_n$), von Neumann entropy ($S$), and Lorentzian twist-operator correlation functions (four-point and two-point functions).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time separation ($t$), lattice size ($L$), mass scale ($\xi \sim m^{-1}$), and boundary conditions (DD, NN, PBC).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is initially restricted to the vacuum state of a free scalar field, and the methods rely on extending Gaussian diagonalization to non-Hermitian operators.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic setup for the reduced density matrix on a circle and a strip, and show the evolution of Rényi entropies for various boundary conditions (DD, NN, DN) as a function of time separation.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the reduced spacetime density matrix, calculating its spectrum and Rényi moments, establishing the connection to Lorentzian twist-operator correlators, and finally testing this identification against various boundary conditions and limits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate timelike entanglement in quantum field theory using spacetime density matrices and provide a microscopic lattice realization. For a two-dimensional free real scalar field, we extend Gaussian diagonalization methods to the generally non-Hermitian reduced spacetime density matrix and determine its complete nonzero spectrum in the generic regular case, together with all integer Rényi moments. The real-time replica construction identifies these moments with Lorentzian branch-point twist-operator correlation functions. We test this identification against the full four-point function on a circle, boundary two-point functions with Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions, and massive form-factor predictions, finding quantitative agreement in both magnitude and phase across distinct causal regimes. The boundary setup exhibits a finite causally connected window in which every integer Rényi entropy is real, showing that reality is not equivalent to causal disconnection. These results provide a microscopic lattice foundation for timelike entanglement and for Lorentzian twist-operator methods beyond equal-time regions.

</details>

**Summary.** This review provides an essential experimental guide for measuring chiral phonons, which are vibrations sensitive to circular polarization. It details advanced spectroscopic techniques like VCD, ROA, and circularly polarized Raman scattering. The work is crucial for harnessing the unique spin-lattice coupling enabled by these phonons in emerging fields like spintronics and quantum information.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on spin-lattice coupling and the ability to probe chirality using optical methods makes it highly relevant for understanding quantum phenomena in condensed matter systems, bridging optics and lattice dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of experimentally measuring chiral phonons, which are quantized vibrations whose atomic motion breaks improper rotation symmetries.

**Main result.** It provides a comprehensive experimental guide detailing various techniques to systematically investigate the properties of chiral phonons across diverse material systems.

**Method.** The review synthesizes multiple experimental techniques, including Optical Rotatory Dispersion (ORD), Circular Dichroism (CD), Vibrational Optical Activity (VOA) (VCD/ROA), and circularly polarized Raman spectroscopy.

**Model / system.** The study covers chiral and achiral materials, ranging from crystalline solids like $\alpha$-quartz to 2D perovskites and quantum magnets. Chirality can be inherent or induced transiently via interactions with photons or magnons.

**Key observables.** Optical rotation, differential absorption (CD), VOA spectra (VCD/ROA), and phonon frequency splitting induced by pseudoangular momentum (PAM).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The magnitude of spectroscopic signatures varies greatly across techniques; the coupling between spin and lattice degrees of freedom is key.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation discussed is the very small signal magnitude associated with VOA measurements, requiring high sensitivity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate examples of left- and right-handed chiral materials hosting chiral phonons and schematically show the measurement setup for circularly polarized Raman spectroscopy, including phonon dispersion splitting.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured as a detailed perspective review, first covering general concepts and then systematically detailing steady-state probe techniques, followed by transient time-resolved spectroscopies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Chiral phonons are quantized vibrations where the atomic motion in a solid breaks improper rotation symmetries. In many cases, chiral phonons possess angular momenta and are therefore selective to circularly polarized light. Both fundamental and applied research efforts on chiral phonons have been gaining increasing attention owing to their importance in a variety of fields including spintronics, spin-selective chemical reactions, thermal transport, quantum information processing and biosensing, where the bi-directional spin-lattice coupling enabled by chiral phonons can be harnessed in new ways, and potentially lead to new functionalities. Thus far, the studies of chiral phonons across diverse materials platforms have evolved largely independently within these fields, but the experimental techniques are often interrelated. In this perspective, we present a detailed description, as well as advantages and disadvantages of the current approaches for experimentally measuring chiral phonons in chiral and achiral materials. We conclude with a discussion of new methods for measuring chiral phonons. Ultimately, this work seeks to offer an experimental guide for systematically investigating the properties of chiral phonons in various materials systems and applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates how the spin-1 Haldane topological phase persists when holes are doped into the system. Using DMRG and Bosonization, the authors map out the phase diagram, showing that the topological order remains robust while the system develops a metallic character. The study reveals complex doping-dependent physics, including the formation of a reentrant ferromagnetic phase at high doping.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed theoretical understanding of how topological order (Haldane SPT) can survive and reorganize in the presence of mobile charge carriers, which is crucial for understanding doped quantum magnets and correlated materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the robustness and evolution of the spin-1 Haldane symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phase when the system is subjected to itinerant hole doping.

**Main result.** The doped Haldane SPT remains stable over a broad doping range, characterized by a metallic low-energy description, and its phase diagram reveals a reentrant ferromagnetic pocket at high doping.

**Method.** The research employs advanced numerical techniques, primarily Density-Matrix Renormalization Group (DMRG) calculations, supplemented by Bosonization analysis to characterize the electronic and spin correlations.

**Model / system.** The model consists of two t-J chains coupled by a ferromagnetic (FM) Hund exchange interaction, allowing for the study of charge and spin degrees of freedom under doping ($\delta$).

**Key observables.** String Order Parameter (SOP), bulk spin gap ($\Delta_s$), entanglement spectrum, and various correlation functions like hole correlations ($g_{hh}(r)$) and defect-spin correlations ($G_{dd}(r)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping ($\delta$), Hund coupling ($J_H$), and the AFM leg exchange coupling ($J$) are the key tuning parameters defining the phase diagram.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on extrapolating finite-size DMRG results to the thermodynamic limit and uses empirical fits to map out the boundaries of the ferromagnetic phase.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the global phase diagram in the $(J_H, \delta)$ plane, showing the coexistence of the doped Haldane SPT and FM phases, alongside plots detailing the evolution of topological and correlation diagnostics across doping levels.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the system's behavior by first establishing the model and key diagnostics (DMRG/Bosonization). It then maps the global phase diagram, detailing how low doping involves short-range charge correlations, while high doping shows a crossover to collective spin incorporation, culminating in the characterization of the reentrant FM boundary.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study itinerant hole doping of the spin-$1$ Haldane symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phase in two $t$--$J$ chains coupled by ferromagnetic (FM) Hund exchange. Density-matrix renormalization group calculations show that string order, a finite spin gap, and the Haldane entanglement structure coexist over a broad doping range. Combined with entanglement-entropy scaling consistent with a single gapless charge mode, these results support a metallic doped Haldane SPT with a $C1S0$ low-energy description. At low doping, short-range charge correlations reduce the density of spin-$1/2$ defect rungs, whereas at larger doping their positions become nearly uncorrelated while their spins are collectively incorporated into the correlated spin sector. The doped Haldane SPT terminates in a fully spin-polarized FM phase, which forms a reentrant pocket at high doping in the global phase diagram. Dilute-limit analyses trace the two FM boundaries to spin--charge factorization at weak Hund coupling and to rung-triplet binding with reduced pair mobility at strong Hund coupling. Our results show how mobile carriers reorganize the Haldane spin structure while preserving its characteristic signatures.

</details>

### [A Geometric Theory of Quantum Entanglement](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11720v1)

**Authors:** Lorenzo Capra, Lucio de Simone, Roberto Franzosi  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.11720v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **2/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.11720_figures/2609.11720_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Comparison between the standard spin squeezing parameter ξ2 (dash-dotted line) [28], the geometric Entan- glement Distance (solid line) and the normalized Entropy of Entanglement measure for the bi-partition 1 spin/all other spins (dotted line) as a function of dimensionless time χt for the Kitagawa-Ueda Model (N = 10). While squeezing fails to detect entanglement after its minimum, ED mono- tonically tracks the formation of macroscopic non-Gaussian correlations.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper unifies multiple entanglement measures—including Entanglement Distance (ED) and Quantum Fisher Information (QFI)—by showing they are all rooted in the geometry of the projective Hilbert space. It demonstrates that this geometric measure quantifies the total resourcefulness for local quantum sensing, providing a rigorous link between information geometry and quantum metrology.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, unified geometric tool (ED) that connects abstract concepts of entanglement to concrete, measurable quantities in quantum metrology (QFI), which is highly relevant for designing quantum sensing protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to geometrically characterize and quantify quantum entanglement, establishing mathematical equivalences between various measures like Entanglement Distance (ED), Meyer-Wallach, and Quantum Fisher Information (QFI).

**Main result.** It proves that ED is mathematically equivalent to the sum of local linear entropies and is directly proportional to the total QFI available for local unitary estimation, bridging geometry and quantum metrology.

**Method.** The analysis heavily utilizes the Fubini-Study metric on the projective Hilbert space and relates entanglement measures to the variance of local observable generators.

**Model / system.** The work considers general finite-dimensional multipartite systems, analyzing specific dynamics using the Kitagawa-Ueda (KU) model for spin squeezing, and also examining the Haar-random state ensemble.

**Key observables.** Entanglement Distance (ED), Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), Spin Squeezing Parameter ($\xi^2$), and local linear entropies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits ($N$), time evolution parameter ($t$), and the local generator structure ($\mathfrak{su}(d_\mu)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that the geometric approach is robust for extending measures to infinite-dimensional (CV) systems, though it notes a gap between theoretical saturation and experimental accessibility for random states.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares the evolution of the Spin Squeezing Parameter ($\xi^2$), ED, and normalized Entropy of Entanglement for the KU Model, showing ED's distinct behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the geometric framework using the Fubini-Study metric, derives the equivalence between ED and local entropy sums, applies this framework to the KU model to show metrological advantage, and finally analyzes the global average entanglement for Haar-random states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement Distance (ED) was originally proposed as a geometric measure of entanglement derived from the Fubini-Study metric on the projective Hilbert space. Independently, the Meyer-Wallach and Scott measures quantify multipartite entanglement via linear entropy. In this work, we demonstrate that these two seemingly distinct frameworks are mathematically identical for pure states of arbitrary finite dimensions. We prove that ED arises naturally as the trace of the Fubini-Study metric tensor over the local subalgebra of observables. Crucially, this geometric unification yields a direct operational interpretation: the global entanglement of a pure state is exactly proportional to the total Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) available for local unitary estimation. This bridges abstract information geometry with quantum metrology, demonstrating that ED dynamically quantifies resourcefulness for distributed quantum sensing, identifying Heisenberg-limited sensitivity in regimes where standard variance-based witnesses fail.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper proposes and proves the 'no-conjugating theorem,' showing that an unknown quantum state cannot be transformed into its complex conjugate state using any unitary operation. The proof uses algebraic methods applied to the qubit system, extending the body of known no-go theorems in quantum information theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work extends the fundamental limitations of quantum operations (like no-cloning) by proving a new impossibility theorem regarding state conjugation, which has implications for quantum measurement and state reconstruction.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to prove the 'no-conjugating theorem,' which asserts that it is impossible to transform an unknown arbitrary quantum state into its complex conjugate state using any universal unitary evolution.

**Main result.** The no-conjugating theorem is established, demonstrating that no universal unitary operator can map an arbitrary unknown state $|\psiangle$ to its conjugate state $|\psi^*angle$.

**Method.** The proof relies on algebraic manipulation of the unitary transformation equations derived from assuming the existence of such an operator, leading to contradictions.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on a single unknown arbitrary quantum state (a qubit) $|\psiangle = \alpha|0angle + eta|1angle$ and its unitary evolution $U$.

**Key observables.** The unknown state $|\psiangle$, its conjugate $|\psi^*angle$, and the resulting state $|\psi'angle$ after unitary evolution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coefficients $\alpha$ and $eta$ defining the unknown state, and the structure of the $2 	imes 2$ unitary matrix $U$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a universal unitary operator $U$ capable of performing the conjugation, which is then shown to be impossible.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention a Table 2 listing six specific states ($\{|\Phi_iangle\}$) that serve as targets for the no-go theorems.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon established no-cloning and no-broadcasting theorems, first proving conditions under which conjugation is possible (Theorem 1), then establishing the general impossibility (Theorem 2), and finally proving the no-conjugating theorem by setting the target state to the conjugate state $|\psi^*angle$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The no-cloning theorem, the no-deleting theorem, the no-hiding theorem, the no-flip theorem, and the no-broadcasting play a crucial role in quantum mechanics and quantum information. In this paper, we propose the no-conjugating theorem which states that it is impossible to conjugate an unknown arbitrary quantum state via a universal physical device or linear/unitary operation.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work develops a framework to realize finite 2-group symmetries on quantum lattice models in 2+1 dimensions. By constructing symmetry operators and proposing local Hamiltonians, the authors connect abstract algebraic structures (like 2-groups) to concrete physical models exhibiting topological order. This provides powerful tools for characterizing and solving complex strongly correlated quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The deep connection between higher symmetries (2-groups) and the construction of exactly solvable, symmetry-protected topological phases is highly relevant for understanding exotic quantum matter states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper constructs and studies lattice realizations of finite 2-group symmetries in $2+1$d quantum lattice systems.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that symmetry operators for split and central 2-groups can be constructed, leading to proposals for local symmetric Hamiltonians and identifying exactly solvable limits realizing 2-group gauge theories.

**Method.** The authors use advanced algebraic techniques involving cohomology, cocycle conditions, and the construction of symmetry operators on tensor-product Hilbert spaces.

**Model / system.** The focus is on quantum lattice models (QLMs) in $2+1$ dimensions defined on a lattice, characterized by finite-dimensional tensor-product Hilbert spaces.

**Key observables.** Symmetry operators, symmetry defects, ground-state degeneracies, and the structure of the dual fusion 2-category.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the 2-group (split vs. central), the Postnikov class $[eta]$, and the lattice geometry (e.g., triangular lattice).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to QLMs with tensor-product Hilbert spaces, which limits the realization of true topological operators.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the defect interpretation of symmetry actions ($ho$ and $[eta]$) and the branching structure of the underlying simplicial complex.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically addresses split 2-group symmetry, then central 2-group symmetry, followed by discussions on the mathematical structure of the symmetries, and concluding with proposals for symmetric Hamiltonians and solvable limits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct and study lattice realizations of finite 2-group symmetries in ${2+1}$d quantum lattice systems with finite-dimensional tensor-product Hilbert spaces. We focus on two broad classes of 2-groups with 0-form symmetry group $G$ and 1-form symmetry group $A$: split 2-groups with trivial Postnikov class ${[β]\in\mathcal{H}^3(G,A)}$, and central 2-groups with trivial action ${ρ\colon G\to\text{Aut}(A)}$. In both cases, we construct symmetry operators on the full tensor-product Hilbert space that become 2-group symmetry operators when restricted to the topological subspace of the lattice $A$ 1-form symmetry. While the lattice split 2-group symmetry operators are onsite, the lattice central 2-group symmetry operators are not, and can only be made onsite after introducing ancillae. We extensively explore various manifestations of $ρ$ and $[β]$ for these lattice 2-group symmetry operators and demonstrate their agreement with expectations from quantum field theory. These manifestations arise in the transformation of operators carrying symmetry charge, the structure of lattice 2-group symmetry defects, and the dual fusion 2-category symmetries obtained by gauging the lattice 2-group symmetries. We further propose families of local symmetric Hamiltonians for both classes of lattice 2-group symmetries and identify exactly solvable limits lying in phases with spontaneous 2-group symmetry breaking and nontrivial symmetry-enriched topological order. In one such limit, the gauged Hamiltonians are exactly solvable lattice realizations of the corresponding 2-group gauge theories, whose ground-state degeneracies we calculate.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes using the spectral features, specifically the standardized moments of the Density of States (DOS), of an Ising Hamiltonian derived from a signed graph to solve the NP-hard problem of structural balance. By employing a quantum spectral estimation technique (DOS-QPE), the authors demonstrate that these spectral moments can accurately predict the graph's frustration index, opening avenues for quantum machine learning on complex networks.

**Why it may be interesting.** It bridges quantum many-body physics (Hamiltonian spectra) with complex combinatorial optimization problems (graph balance), suggesting spectral methods can extract hard-to-find structural invariants.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of learning the structural balance (frustration index) of signed graphs, which is an NP-hard problem.

**Main result.** The standardized moments derived from the Density of States (DOS) of the associated Ising Hamiltonian successfully recover the frustration index for large datasets, suggesting a quantum spectral feature approach for graph analysis.

**Method.** The authors map the signed graph problem onto an Ising model and use quantum spectral feature extraction (DOS moments) as machine learning inputs. They propose DOS-QPE for efficient quantum sampling.

**Model / system.** The system is a signed graph embedded as an Ising model instance, $H = \sum J_{ij} Z_i Z_j$. The features are derived from the standardized moments of the Hamiltonian's Density of States (DOS).

**Key observables.** Standardized moments of the DOS ($\phi(G)$), the frustration index $L(G)$, and the ground-state energy $E_0$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Graph size ($n=12$ vertices benchmarked), the number of standardized moments used (five), and the field strength (zero field vs. general dynamics).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the spectral statistics (DOS) contain sufficient, invariant information to determine the graph's structural balance, and that quantum methods are necessary for general dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the entire pipeline: Signed Graph $ightarrow$ Ising Hamiltonian $ightarrow$ DOS Sampling (Quantum/Classical) $ightarrow$ Five Moments $\phi(G) ightarrow$ Linear Model $ightarrow$ Prediction of $L(G)$. Figure 2 shows how the DOS spectrum changes for graphs with different frustration levels.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem (structural balance), develops the physical encoding (Ising Hamiltonian), proposes the quantum feature extraction method (DOS-QPE), validates it against the known ground truth (zero field), and discusses its applicability to hard problems (general dynamics).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a quantum approach to spectral feature extraction from the density of states (DOS) of a problem-dependent Hamiltonian, and apply it to machine learning on signed graphs. We propose to embed a signed graph as an Ising model instance with positive and negative interactions, and use the standardized moments of the Ising DOS as features for learning. We show that these moments count signed closed walks, are switching-invariant, and are size-free by construction. As a benchmark, we target learning the frustration index, an NP-hard measure of structural balance that can be labeled exactly at moderate size. At zero field, the models can be sampled classically, allowing the quantum extraction procedure to be certified against exact ground truth. We propose DOS-QPE, a phase estimation on a purified maximally mixed probe, which samples the spectral density with orders of magnitude fewer shots than Hadamard test-based trace sampling and feeds the resulting features directly into classically trained models. On $1.4\times10^5$ labeled graphs the exact DOS determines the frustration index, and five moments recover it with a mean error of 0.4, well below one sign flip. Beyond zero field, the underlying trace-estimation problem is DQC1-complete, providing access to spectral features for which no efficient classical sampling method is known. Our work opens routes towards quantum applications in social network balance analysis, spin-glass studies, correlation clustering, and protein-interaction networks.

</details>

**Authors:** Lucas Berent, Lawrence Z. Cohen, Armanda O. Quintavalle  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.11723v1>

The first bar (yellow) shows the LER of a standard surgery experiment of Z1 on the J90, 8, 10K code with 10 rounds of syndrome extraction, all decoded at once; the second bar (blue) shows...</sub>

**Summary.** This paper presents 'lifted surgery,' a novel, algebraically derived technique to drastically reduce the time overhead associated with performing logical measurements on QLDPC quantum error-correcting codes. By leveraging block decompositions and commutative algebra, it achieves fast, parallel syndrome extraction, offering a practical path toward scalable, low-overhead fault-tolerant quantum computing.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on QLDPC codes, the underlying mathematical machinery involving homology, chain complexes, and algebraic characterization of measurable operators is highly relevant to theoretical studies of quantum error correction codes in general.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is overcoming the significant time overhead associated with performing logical operations in Quantum Low-Density Parity-Check (QLDPC) codes.

**Main result.** The authors introduce 'lifted surgery,' a method that achieves fast and parallel code surgery, requiring an order of magnitude fewer syndrome measurement rounds than standard techniques while maintaining low physical overhead.

**Method.** The method utilizes advanced algebraic techniques, including block decompositions and commutative algebra, to characterize and construct the lifted surgery process on Abelian group algebra codes.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on QLDPC codes, which are promising candidates for fault-tolerant quantum computation due to their high encoding rates. The analysis is heavily mathematical, involving chain complexes and homology groups.

**Key observables.** Logical performance, number of syndrome measurement rounds, and the dimension of the measured logical operator space.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code parameters such as $[[90, 8, 10]]$ and $[[198, 8, 16]]$, and the noise model (circuit-level depolarizing noise).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the algebraic structure of the underlying code algebra, particularly when the blocks are fields, simplifying the addressability criteria.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the graphical representation of lifted surgery, showing the codespace decomposition and the auxiliary complex used to facilitate parallel operations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the problem (time overhead in code surgery) to introducing the mathematical framework (lifted surgery using algebra), providing explicit constructions (quantum radial codes), and concluding with rigorous mathematical characterizations of addressable submodules and joint measurements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum low-density parity-check (QLDPC) codes are a leading candidate for achieving low-overhead fault-tolerant quantum computing. However, the time overhead of logical operations in QLDPC codes remains a key challenge. Code surgery, a space-efficient technique for fault-tolerant logical measurements, incurs this overhead through repeated rounds of syndrome measurement. We introduce lifted surgery, a method for fast and parallel surgery on Abelian group algebra codes that maintains the low physical overhead that makes QLDPC codes attractive. Lifted surgery preserves the symmetries of the underlying code, making searches for large instances tractable and offering a natural route towards efficient hardware implementations. We utilise block decompositions and techniques from commutative algebra to characterise lifted surgery, investigate well-behaved subfamilies, and construct explicit examples. In particular, we present quantum radial codes with parameters $[[90, 8, 10]]$ and $[[198, 8, 16 ]]$ for which surgery is fast, parallel, and addressable, allowing arbitrary sets of independent logical operators of the same Pauli type to be measured in a single round of syndrome extraction. We benchmark lifted surgery under circuit-level depolarising noise and, for the $[[ 90, 8, 10 ]]$ code, find logical performance comparable to standard code surgery while requiring ten times fewer rounds of syndrome measurement. By combining speed and parallelism, lifted surgery offers a practical route towards low-overhead fault-tolerant quantum computing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a unified theoretical framework, the class H, to study associative memories by treating hidden neurons as the order parameter. It analyzes how retrieval dynamics change between polynomial and exponential loading regimes, revealing distinct crossover behaviors in crosstalk statistics. The work provides a deep physical understanding of how stability and storage capacity are governed by separate Lagrangian components.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of statistical mechanics tools (replica method, random-energy models) to neural network dynamics provides deep insights into pattern storage capacity and phase transitions, which are foundational concepts in many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to unify the study of associative memory retrieval across different loading regimes (polynomial vs. exponential) by analyzing a generalized bipartite architecture.

**Main result.** The proposed class H splits retrieval into two distinct roles: the visible Lagrangian controls stability, while the hidden Lagrangian dictates the storage scale and disorder statistics.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced statistical physics techniques, including the replica method and mapping to random-energy models, to derive phase diagrams and capacities.

**Model / system.** The system is the 'class H,' a bipartite network combining Krotov and Hopfield architectures, modeled by an energy function involving visible and hidden neuron dynamics. The hidden neurons are treated as the order parameter for memory retrieval.

**Key observables.** Retrieval overlap ($m$), Edwards–Anderson order parameter ($q$), and crosstalk noise ($r$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Load parameter ($\alpha_k$), inverse temperature ($eta$), and the distinction between polynomial and exponential load regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the replica symmetric (RS) ansatz in the replica method, and the adiabatic limit ($	au_v \gg 	au_h$) is used for certain derivations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the bipartite network structure. Figure 2 shows the RS phase diagram in the $(\alpha_k, T)$ plane, distinguishing retrieval, spin glass, and paramagnetic phases. Figure 5 overlays dynamical escape barriers onto equilibrium phase diagrams.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the model using the replica method for polynomial loads (Models A and C), then extends to exponential loads (Model B). It develops the general framework (class H) and discusses the physical interpretation of the different Lagrangian components.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Associative memory in the Hopfield network is attractor dynamics in a disordered many-body system, and higher-order and exponential extensions turn its retrieval update into softmax attention. The polynomial and exponential regimes have been analyzed by different methods, with no common architecture in which to ask what fixes the storage scale. In this paper we study the bipartite architecture of Krotov and Hopfield, which we call the class $H$, whose model is fixed by a Lagrangian for each layer, taking the hidden neurons as the order parameter of retrieval. At polynomial load the replica method yields the replica-symmetric phase diagrams and closed-form capacities, and the crosstalk moment is common to Ising and spherical visible neurons, so their differences come from the visible entropy. With a softmax hidden layer the load is exponential, and a copy representation maps the thermodynamics onto random-energy-model counting, with paramagnetic, condensed, and frozen phases. Heating destabilizes retrieval by quantized reassignments of attention, and typical Gaussian patterns remain metastable at every load. The regimes differ in their crosstalk statistics, central-limit at polynomial load and large-deviation at exponential load, and the class $H$ splits retrieval into two roles, the visible Lagrangian fixing stability and the hidden one the storage scale, two axes that may also guide the design of new Lagrangians.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes the thermoelectric and thermodynamic properties of an interacting Tomonaga-Lüttinger liquid with an impurity. Using advanced field-theoretic methods, the authors derive relationships showing that the thermopower, entropy variation, and heat capacity all exhibit characteristic Mott-like behavior at low temperatures. The findings unify concepts of quantum transport and mesoscopic thermodynamics in 1D correlated systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection derived between non-equilibrium transport (thermopower) and equilibrium thermodynamics (heat capacity/entropy) in a strongly correlated 1D system provides a powerful tool for understanding energy dissipation in quantum conductors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the thermoelectric and thermodynamic properties associated with particle transport in an interacting Tomonaga-Lüttinger liquid (TLL) containing an impurity.

**Main result.** The study establishes a close link between thermoelectric transport (thermopower) and thermodynamic quantities (entropy variation and heat capacity), showing both exhibit Mott-like behavior at low temperatures.

**Method.** The analysis employs bosonization and refermionization techniques to derive exact expressions for transport coefficients in both linear and nonlinear regimes.

**Model / system.** The primary model is an interacting TLL with an impurity, specifically analyzed for the interaction constant K=1/2. Applications include a quantum point contact between an FQH edge state and a normal metal, and a one-channel conductor coupled to an Ohmic environment.

**Key observables.** Thermopower (Q), Seebeck coefficient, Entropy variation (ΔS), and Heat capacity density (C).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction constant K=1/2, low-temperature limit, and the energy dependence of the transmission coefficient.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis utilizes the Sommerfeld approximation for low temperatures and relies on mapping the system to a solvable TLL model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the temperature and bias voltage dependence of the thermopower, and show the variation of the heat capacity density (C) versus temperature and Fermi energy for both QPC and OCC systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically covers the theoretical framework, derives transport coefficients using Landauer formalism, analyzes the temperature and bias dependence of Q, ΔS, and C, and concludes by applying the results to specific quantum point contact and conductor models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate thermoelectric and thermodynamic properties of a Tomonaga-Luttinger liquid with interaction parameter $K=1/2$ in the presence of a localized impurity. Using bosonization and refermionization, we derive an exact expression for the thermopower in the nonlinear regime and obtain the corresponding Seebeck coefficient in linear response. At low temperature, the Seebeck coefficient follows a Mott-like relation governed by the energy dependence of the transmission coefficient and is found to be closely related to the entropy per particle. This connection allows us to derive using Kelvin formula the thermopower contribution to the entropy density variation and the charge-carriers contribution to the heat capacity density. Both quantities exhibit Mott-like behavior in the low-temperature regime. We apply our results to a fractional quantum Hall quantum point contact and to a one-channel quantum conductor coupled to an Ohmic environment. Our results demonstrate the close connection between thermoelectric transport and thermodynamic properties in interacting one-dimensional quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to quantify chaos in chemical reaction systems by analyzing information loss. It proves that the rate at which mutual information decays in the stochastic system converges to the Kolmogorov-Sinai entropy of the deterministic limit. This establishes a rigorous connection between information theory and the quantification of deterministic chaos in open, fluctuating physical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, information-theoretic bridge between the microscopic stochastic dynamics (CME) and the macroscopic deterministic chaos (KS entropy), which is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems where noise and dissipation are key.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper theoretically investigates whether an information-theoretic measure derived from the stochastic Chemical Master Equation (CME) converges to the Kolmogorov-Sinai (KS) entropy in the macroscopic limit.

**Main result.** The rate of decrease of the two-time mutual information, $-rac{d}{dt}I(X(V)_t; X(V)_0)$, is shown to converge to the KS entropy ($h_{KS}$) in the large system-size limit ($V 	o \infty$).

**Method.** The analysis involves relating the time evolution of mutual information to the conditional entropy, utilizing Gaussian approximations, and connecting the long-time growth rate to the sum of positive Lyapunov exponents.

**Model / system.** The study uses open chemical reaction networks, specifically demonstrating the results with a modified CS14 three-species system. The dynamics are governed by the Chemical Master Equation (CME) in the stochastic regime and deterministic rate equations in the macroscopic limit.

**Key observables.** Mutual Information ($I$), Conditional Entropy ($H(\mathbf{X}(V)_t | \mathbf{X}(V)_0)$), Gaussian Entropy Increment ($\Delta H_G(V)$), and the Kolmogorov-Sinai entropy ($h_{KS}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($V$), time ($t$), and the Lyapunov exponents ($\lambda_i$) derived from the deterministic rate equations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Gaussian approximation for the conditional distribution at large $V$ and assumes the convergence of the information loss rate to the KS entropy in the macroscopic limit.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows stochastic concentration time series approaching the deterministic limit. Figure 2 and Figure 4 illustrate the time evolution of the mutual information and the Gaussian entropy-growth rate, respectively, showing the convergence to $h_{KS}$ with finite-time corrections.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical link between information loss and KS entropy, derives the necessary mathematical tools using mutual information and conditional entropy, and then applies these tools to the CME in the large-system-size limit to confirm the convergence.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Open chemical reaction networks exhibit stochastic concentration dynamics at finite system sizes, whereas their macroscopic limit is governed by deterministic rate equations that can display chaos. In this Letter, we show theoretically that a rate of information loss constructed from two-time mutual information recovers the Kolmogorov-Sinai entropy in the deterministic limit. We verify this result through numerical simulations of a Markov jump process for a three-species system involving seven reactions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to determine if noise can enhance quantum computation in distributed circuits. By analyzing the second-moment dynamics, the authors separate the noise effects into dissipative (radial) and mixing (angular) components. They conclude that only amplitude damping provides a genuine, measurable advantage for mixing, while other noise types are dominated by simple information loss.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, quantitative framework for diagnosing the utility of noise in quantum hardware, which is crucial for designing fault-tolerant or noise-resilient quantum computation protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the fundamental question of whether adding noise to a random quantum circuit provides genuine computational advantage (useful randomization) or merely causes information loss (dissipation).

**Main result.** The analysis shows that useful noise-assisted randomization is highly conditional, identifying amplitude damping (AD) as the only locally favorable noise channel for angular mixing, while distinguishing this genuine effect from simple dissipation.

**Method.** The authors employ a reduced second-moment transfer-matrix theory, separating the noisy dynamics into a dissipative radial branch and a mixing angular branch to analyze the decay rates.

**Model / system.** The study models distributed random quantum circuits, relevant for modular quantum processors, subjected to local noise channels: amplitude damping (AD), depolarizing (DEP), and dephasing (DPH).

**Key observables.** Second moments of Pauli operators, the radial leakage coefficient, and the ratio ($ho$) comparing radial decay to angular mixing rates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Weak-noise regime, noise strength ($\gamma$), and the specific noise channel (AD, DEP, DPH).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes vanishing cross-correlations and transverse symmetry for the local mixing blocks, and it relies on separating the noisy spectrum into distinct radial and angular modes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison of raw vs. normalized angular gaps ($\Delta_A$ vs. $ho$) across different noise types, showing that the radial-normalized improvement ($\Delta^*_A|R$) is the key diagnostic for genuine noise assistance.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by analyzing the second-moment evolution, derives a weak-noise criterion based on noise channel properties, and then applies this to multicore architectures to distinguish useful randomization from dissipation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Adding noise can make a random quantum circuit look faster without making its unitary dynamics more random. This distinction is especially relevant in modular processors, where local gates randomize each core and scarce inter-core communication must spread that randomness across the full device. In this paper, we study this problem with a reduced second-moment transfer-matrix theory for Pauli second moments in distributed random circuits affected by the amplitude-damping, depolarizing, and dephasing noise channels. The key step is to resolve the noisy spectrum into two branches: a radial branch, describing dissipative loss of non-identity Pauli weight, and an angular branch, describing Haar-like mixing within the surviving nontrivial sector. This separation gives a simple weak-noise criterion: noise is useful for angular randomization only when it suppresses the longitudinal Bloch component more strongly than the transverse plane. Among the three channels considered, this selects amplitude damping as the only locally favorable case, while depolarizing noise is neutral and dephasing is dominated by radial loss. For multicore architectures, we derive a universal first-order law for radial leakage and track the angular branch numerically across different channels, topologies, and core partitions. The results reveal narrow windows of genuine noise-assisted Haar mixing, most clearly for amplitude damping, but rule out a generic speed-up by noise. The framework therefore distinguishes useful noisy randomization from mere dissipation.

</details>
