**Summary.** The paper introduces the twisted entropic order parameter, a refined entanglement diagnostic, to overcome the limitations of standard entanglement asymmetry in characterizing Symmetry-Protected Topological (SPT) phases. By incorporating an ancilla degree of freedom, this parameter successfully extracts topological information—the endpoint charge—that is otherwise invisible, allowing for the precise classification of 1D bosonic SPT phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, entanglement-based diagnostic tool to probe subtle topological order in condensed matter systems, extending beyond simple symmetry breaking patterns.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Ordinary entanglement asymmetry fails to distinguish between different Symmetry-Protected Topological (SPT) phases that share the same symmetry-breaking pattern. The goal is to refine this diagnostic to detect the residual SPT data protected by the unbroken subgroup.

**Main result.** The twisted entropic order parameter successfully diagnoses SPT phases by probing the endpoint charge, which is invisible to standard entanglement asymmetry measures.

**Method.** The authors introduce a twisted entropic order parameter by enlarging the system with an ancilla degree of freedom to coherently record the untwisted and twisted states associated with a topological defect.

**Model / system.** The focus is on one-dimensional bosonic gapped phases exhibiting symmetry breaking $G 	o H$. The method is applied to lattice models, such as the clock-broken $Z_N$ cluster ladder model.

**Key observables.** The twisted entropic order parameter ($\Delta S_{	ext{twi}, g, \lambda}(A)$) and the associated endpoint charge $\epsilon_{\omega}^g(k)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The residual $	ext{H-SPT}$ class $[\omega] \in H^2(H, U(1))$ and the symmetry group structure ($G, H$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method is most rigorously defined at a zero-correlation-length fixed point; the detection capability might fail for SPT phases with nontrivial Bogomolov multipliers.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the concept of a symmetry-defect endpoint charge and the construction of the enlarged state $|\Xi_gangle_{CAB}$ used for the diagnostic.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the failure of standard diagnostics, then introduces the twisted entropic order parameter using an ancilla degree of freedom to probe the endpoint charge. It provides concrete examples in 1D bosonic systems and generalizes the framework to detect residual SPT information.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement asymmetry, introduced by F. Ares, S. Murciano and P. Calabrese, provides a density-matrix diagnostic of symmetry breaking and successfully captures the Landau data associated with a broken symmetry pattern. However, it is by now well established that gapped quantum many-body systems can exhibit phases which are not characterized solely by Landau symmetry breaking. A fundamental example is a symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phase, and the ordinary definition of entanglement asymmetry is insensitive to this topological information. In this work we introduce a refined quantity, which we call the twisted entropic order parameter, designed to detect SPT phases from reduced density matrices, particularly focusing on one-dimensional bosonic systems. The key ingredient in our construction is an ancilla degrees of freedom that coherently records the untwisted state and the twisted state associated to a one-ended topological defect of unbroken symmetry, so that the enlarged density matrix retains the charge carried by the defect endpoint. We demonstrate our proposal in concrete lattice models and further generalize it beyond ordinary group symmetries, establishing its ability to diagnose SPT phases. This provides a first step toward a unified entanglement-asymmetry framework for diagnosing quantum phases of matter.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the correlated behavior in Kagome superconductors by proposing that local ionic magnetic moments, rather than just itinerant carriers, are crucial. Theoretical calculations predict these moments, and experimental doping with Sn impurities successfully confirms their existence via magnetic measurements. This suggests a major revision to existing models of superconductivity in these materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The emphasis on local moments and strong correlations in a geometrically frustrated superconducting lattice is highly relevant to understanding exotic quantum states in strongly correlated electron systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to resolve the discrepancy in understanding the correlated behavior of Kagome superconductors ($	ext{AV}_3	ext{Sb}_5$ and $	ext{ATi}_3	ext{Bi}_5$) by investigating the role of local ionic magnetic moments.

**Main result.** The study suggests a paradigm shift from purely itinerant carrier descriptions to one that must incorporate strong correlations arising from local ionic spins, which were experimentally confirmed via Sn-doping.

**Method.** The approach combines Hartree-scale electronic structure calculations to model local moments with experimental measurements (magnetic susceptibility and $\mu	ext{SR}$) on doped samples.

**Model / system.** The physical systems are Kagome lattice superconductors, specifically $	ext{AV}_3	ext{Sb}_5$ and $	ext{ATi}_3	ext{Bi}_5$. The theoretical modeling focuses on capturing short-range physics and local orbital-specific density of states.

**Key observables.** Local magnetic moments, magnetic susceptibility ($\chi$), spin relaxation rates ($\mu	ext{SR}$), and local electronic valence states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The doping concentration ($x$) of nonmagnetic Sn impurities, which is used to locally relieve geometric frustration.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial theoretical calculations are performed in a non-collinear Curie-paramagnetic phase to avoid assuming long-range magnetic order.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the local atomic orbital-specific DOS for the superconducting compounds, the mechanism by which Sn impurities relieve geometric frustration, and the temperature dependence of magnetic susceptibility showing Curie-Weiss behavior proportional to doping.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework using Hartree-scale calculations to predict local moments, then using Sn-doping to experimentally verify the existence of these moments, and finally concluding with the necessity of incorporating local spin correlations into the superconducting picture.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In order to understand the unexpected similarity and the correlated behavior in kagome superconductor families AV$_3$Sb$_5$ (A = K, Rb, Cs) and ATi$_3$Bi$_5$ (A = Rb, Cs), we investigate the Hartree-scale local electronic structure of these systems. Our result indicates that V and Ti ions are both of 2+ valence such that the corresponding itinerant carrier densities are similar, and the difference in electron count is instead reflected in their quantum fluctuating ionic magnetic moments. However, due to the frustrated lattice geometry of these materials, such local moments are difficult to experimentally observe via standard probes. For verification, we systematically introduce nonmagnetic Sn impurities to locally relieve the geometric frustration and experimentally demonstrate the existence of well-defined local magnetic moments via magnetic susceptibility and muon spin rotation or relaxation ($μ$SR) measurements. All experiments discover a systematic increase of magnetic susceptibility upon increasing nonmagnetic impurity level. Our discovered ionic moments suggest a paradigm shift from the existing itinerant carrier-only picture to one incorporating strong correlation from local ionic spins. The associated interatomic and local-itinerant correlations offer a solid ground for the emergence of the observed rich correlated behavior in this new family of superconducting materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This work generalizes quantum state transfer to entangled 's-pair' states on graph structures. It characterizes the conditions for perfect transfer, showing that the entanglement degree can be manipulated, even increased, during the process. The paper also provides tools to analyze the robustness of this transfer against time measurement errors.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of controlling entanglement transfer and quantifying the robustness of these quantum processes against measurement errors (sensitivity analysis) is highly relevant to designing robust quantum communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates perfect $(s,r)$-state transfer, which is a generalization of quantum state transfer from an $s$-pair entangled state to an $r$-pair entangled state.

**Main result.** The authors identify infinite families of graphs exhibiting this perfect transfer and demonstrate that the degree of entanglement can be boosted (increased) if $|r| 
eq |s|$.

**Method.** The study uses spectral analysis of the transition operator derived from graph Hamiltonians, along with developing algorithms to maximize transfer fidelity and performing sensitivity analysis.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as quantum state transfer on an undirected graph, where the dynamics are governed by a continuous-time quantum walk Hamiltonian (e.g., adjacency or Laplacian matrix). The states are generalized to $s$-pair states, $\mathbf u = \mathbf e_a + s \mathbf e_b$.

**Key observables.** Fidelity of transfer, degree of entanglement ($|s|$ or $|r|$), and the second derivative of the fidelity function (sensitivity to time errors).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complex parameters $r$ and $s$ defining the initial and final entangled states, and the graph structure itself.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often focuses on the single excitation subspace of the Hamiltonian, and specific results are derived assuming the initial and final states have exactly two non-zero entries.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not describe specific figures, but mention examples like P5, $K_{1,n}$, and $CP(n)$ used to calculate fidelity and sensitivity.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the generalized PST problem, characterizing it for specific graph families ($K_n, K_{a,b}$), developing algorithms for fidelity maximization, and concluding with sensitivity analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Much work has been done in the last two decades on the topic of quantum state transfer in a quantum spin network. One can model such a system of interacting qubits using an undirected graph, and studying vertex-to-vertex dynamics. This setup has recently been relaxed to allow for dynamics between linear combinations of two vertex states, i.e.\ from $\mathbf u = \mathbf e_a + s \mathbf e_b$ to $\mathbf μ=\mathbf e_α + r \mathbf e_β$, where $r=s$ is either $-1$ (which corresponds to pair state transfer) or $+1$ (which corresponds to plus state transfer), or more recently $r=s$ is taken to be any real number (which corresponds to $s$-pair state transfer).   Here, we broaden the investigation of $s$-pair state transfer to \textit{perfect $(s,r)$-state transfer}, which is perfect state transfer from $\mathbf u = \mathbf e_a + s \mathbf e_b$ to $\mathbf μ=\mathbf e_α + r \mathbf e_β$ (up to some dilation) where $r,s\in \mathbb C$. We identify infinite families of graphs with perfect $(s,r)$-state transfer and provide characterizations of cases when $|r|= |s|$ and when $|r|\neq |s|$, showing situations when the degree of entanglement between vertex states is preserved, and when it is not preserved. The latter is particularly important as it represents perfect state transfer from an entangled pair of qubits to another one where the degree of entanglement need not be the same\mdash in fact, it can be set up so as to ``boost'' (increase) entanglement. We provide an algorithm that finds the vector with two nonzero entries that maximizes the fidelity of transfer for a fixed time $t$ starting from a given $s$-pair state $\mathbf u$. Finally, we provide a sensitivity analysis, with respect to readout time errors, of perfect $(s,r)$-state transfer.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that piezomagnetism is a definitive signature of altermagnetism, using $	ext{MnTe}_2$ as a model system. By combining dilatometry, NMR, and DFT, the authors successfully measured and confirmed a linear coupling between applied strain and magnetic moment. This opens new avenues for controlling spintronic devices using coupled strain and magnetic fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the use of coupled external fields (strain and magnetic field) to control electronic properties is relevant to understanding emergent quantum phenomena in engineered materials, bridging concepts from AMO physics (field control) to solid-state physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates piezomagnetism—the linear coupling between lattice strain and net magnetic moment—as a key signature distinguishing altermagnetism from conventional antiferromagnetism.

**Main result.** The authors report observing a large piezomagnetic response in the model cubic altermagnet $	ext{MnTe}_2$, confirmed by agreement between dilatometry measurements (shear strain proportional to $H$) and NMR detection of an induced ferromagnetic moment.

**Method.** The research combines multiple experimental techniques, including dilatometry and NMR, with first-principles Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations to probe the magnetostrictive and piezomagnetic coupling coefficients.

**Model / system.** The system studied is $	ext{MnTe}_2$, a cubic material identified as a model altermagnet possessing compensated magnetic order and spin-polarized bands. The analysis utilizes symmetry constraints derived from the Landau free energy to predict the linear coupling terms.

**Key observables.** Linear magnetostriction ($\epsilon \propto H$), shear deformation, stress-induced ferromagnetic moment (measured by NMR), and the piezomagnetic coupling coefficient ($\lambda$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The measurements are sensitive to temperature, particularly near the magnetic transition temperature ($T_N$), and depend on the crystal orientation ($\langle 100 angle$ vs $\langle 110 angle$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The linear magnetostriction component is assumed to represent the genuine altermagnetic signature, distinct from quadratic or domain-related contributions. DFT calculations sometimes ignore ion-mediated strain contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the magnetic structure, magnetostriction dependence on applied field along different axes, temperature dependence of the coupling coefficient, and NMR spectra showing line splitting under applied stress.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework of piezomagnetism in altermagnets, then presenting experimental measurements (dilatometry, NMR) on $	ext{MnTe}_2$ to extract the linear coupling terms, and finally confirming these findings using first-principles DFT calculations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnets constitute a distinct class of magnetic materials that combine compensated magnetic order with spin-polarized electronic bands, hence carrying characteristics of both antiferromagnets and ferromagnets. Piezomagnetism - the linear relationship between lattice strain and a net magnetic moment - has emerged as smoking-gun evidence of altermagnetism that distinguishes it from antiferromagnetism. Here, we uncover a large piezomagnetic response in MnTe2, a cubic altermagnet with a noncollinear spin arrangement and weak spin-orbit coupling. We combine dilatometry with nuclear magnetic resonance, a bulk local probe, to observe signatures of both direct and inverse piezomagnetism consistent with symmetry considerations and first-principles calculations. The dilatometry measurements reveal a shear deformation proportional to an applied magnetic field, while magnetic resonance detects a ferromagnetic moment induced by shear strain. The respective results for the piezomagnetic coupling strength are in good agreement, and they are captured by first-principles results. These findings for simple binary MnTe2 position this material as a model altermagnet, demonstrate the utility of nuclear magnetic resonance in investigations of piezomagnetism, and open new avenues for multimodal strain and magnetic-field control in spintronic and memory applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a time-frequency framework using Gabor analysis to analyze lattice GKP codes. It proves that the logical information can be uniquely and stably recovered from a finite block of phase-space coefficients. Furthermore, it provides a method to extract the continuous displacement syndrome by analyzing the phase relationships between these coefficients, offering a theoretical blueprint for syndrome measurement.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful mathematical tool for understanding continuous variable quantum error correction, linking abstract phase-space representations (Gabor analysis) directly to measurable syndrome information in a stable, quantifiable manner.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a rigorous time-frequency framework for lattice Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill (GKP) codes, specifically addressing how to stably reconstruct the finite logical information from localized phase-space coefficients.

**Main result.** The authors prove that the normalized block map is an isometry, establishing a unique and stable determination of the logical vector from sampled coefficients, and derive a stable estimator for the continuous displacement syndrome from phase relations between coefficient blocks.

**Method.** The work employs the vector-valued Zak transform and multi-window Gabor analysis to map ideal codewords to finite blocks of adjoint-lattice coefficients, allowing for reconstruction theorems and syndrome estimation.

**Model / system.** The system is based on lattice GKP codes, which encode quantum information into highly structured states within the infinite-dimensional Hilbert space of oscillators, analyzed using modulation spaces and Gabor frames.

**Key observables.** The finite block of adjoint-lattice Gabor coefficients, and the phase relations between translated coefficient blocks used to estimate the displacement syndrome.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The lattice structure ($\Lambda_D$), the regularization parameter ($\epsilon$), and the noise level ($\eta$) governing stability bounds.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is conditional on access to the complex amplitudes (coefficients) and relies on asymptotic convergence and weak-* approximations for finite-energy states.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the Zak transform framework, then developing the adjoint-lattice coefficient model and proving the isometry, followed by deriving the block-correlation estimator for syndrome extraction, and finally quantifying the convergence of this estimator for finite-energy states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a time--frequency framework for lattice GKP codes in which ideal codewords are realized in the modulation space $M^\infty$ and identified, through a vector-valued Zak transform, with a finite logical fibre over the continuous syndrome torus. Multi-window Gabor analysis then represents the logical vector by a finite block of adjoint-lattice coefficients. We prove that the normalized block map is an isometry, obtain an explicit recovering projection, and derive stable logical reconstruction. We further construct normalizable GKP approximants as lattice-envelope Gabor multipliers and establish weak-$*$ convergence and asymptotically isometric encoding. Finally, we recover displacement syndromes from phase relations between translated coefficient blocks and quantify their stability under additive perturbations.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a highly efficient computational framework using reduced-basis emulation combined with exact diagonalization to study CFTs on the fuzzy sphere. By accurately mapping the energy landscape across coupling parameters, the authors precisely locate critical points for the Ising and O(2) models. This methodology significantly reduces the search cost for understanding quantum critical phenomena in strongly interacting systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of RBM to map out critical lines in parameter space is a powerful computational technique applicable to any quantum system whose Hamiltonian depends smoothly on multiple parameters, bridging the gap between exact diagonalization and full phase diagram mapping.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To gain numerical access to three-dimensional Conformal Field Theories (CFTs) by efficiently testing for criticality across the vast parameter space of microscopic couplings.

**Main result.** The combination of exact diagonalization and a certified reduced-basis emulator successfully reproduces known CFT results for the Ising and O(2) models, locating critical lines with high precision.

**Method.** The authors combine exact diagonalization on symmetry-adapted bases with a certified reduced-basis emulator (RBM) to reconstruct energy and observable data continuously across coupling variations, drastically reducing computational cost.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on fuzzy-sphere regularizations of 3D CFTs, specifically analyzing the $N_f=2$ Ising model and the $N_f=3$ O(2) Wilson-Fisher CFT. These models are realized via Hamiltonians involving interacting fermions in the Lowest Landau Level.

**Key observables.** Ground-state energy ($E_{GS}$), scaling dimensions ($\Delta O$), and the ratio $\chi = E_{3/2}/E_0$ used to locate critical points.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Microscopic couplings (e.g., $h$ for Ising, $D$ and $V_0$ for O(2)), and system size $N$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The fuzzy-sphere construction preserves SO(3) symmetry, and the RBM assumes the Hamiltonians are exactly affine in the couplings.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the critical lines for the Ising and O(2) models in coupling planes, showing agreement between emulator results and established DMRG values. Other figures show error analysis and energy/order-parameter surfaces.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the computational framework by first detailing the exact-L ED approach, then introducing the RBM for efficient parameter scanning, and finally applying this combined method to determine and map the critical lines for both the Ising and O(2) models using multiple diagnostic criteria.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fuzzy-sphere regularization gives numerical access to three-dimensional conformal field theories (CFTs), but locating one requires testing for criticality across the parameter space of microscopic couplings. We reduce the search cost by about two orders of magnitude, combining an exact diagonalization based on states having definite total angular momentum with a certified reduced-basis emulator that reconstructs energies, observables and their derivatives continuously across the couplings from a handful of exact solutions per system size. The combination reproduces the published ground-state-energy analysis of the fuzzy-sphere Ising model and the published conformal data of a three-flavor fuzzy-sphere realization of the O(2) Wilson--Fisher CFT, including quantities defined by parameter derivatives. Applied to the O(2) model with no CFT input, the ground-state-energy criterion locates the critical coupling to within half a percent of a density matrix renormalization group value obtained on systems more than twice the size, and maps the critical line of the model across the coupling plane. Ground-state criteria locate the critical line robustly, but the point they select along it is weakly determined, and the computed dimensions come closest to the bootstrap elsewhere along the line.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study models high-Tc superconductivity in bilayer nickelates using the $t$-$J_{\perp}$-$V$ model. It finds that an out-of-plane bond-order phase instability generates loop currents that compete with superconductivity. This competition results in a characteristic dome-shaped superconducting phase diagram, suggesting a quantum critical point governs optimal pairing.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit theoretical modeling of competing orders (charge/orbital vs. superconducting) in a strongly correlated lattice model provides a rich playground for understanding unconventional superconductivity mechanisms, which is a major focus in condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the competition between out-of-plane loop currents (LCs) and superconductivity (SC) in bilayer nickelates, a system relevant to high-Tc superconductivity.

**Main result.** The out-of-plane bond-order phase ($z$-BOP) instability drives the formation of time-reversal symmetry breaking loop currents, leading to a dome-shaped superconducting region optimal near the $z$-BOP quantum critical point.

**Method.** The study employs a controlled large-$N$ expansion, analyzing the self-consistent gap equations for both the superconducting gap ($\Delta_\perp$) and the complex $z$-BOP gap ($\phi$).

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by the strongly correlated one-orbital ($	ext{d}_{x^2-y^2}$) bilayer $t$-$J_{\perp}$-$V$ model for nickelates. The Hamiltonian includes hopping, spin exchange ($J_{\perp}$), and Coulomb interactions ($V$).

**Key observables.** Superconducting gap ($\Delta_\perp$), $z$-BOP gaps ($\phi_{r\perp}, \phi_{A\perp}$), induced in-plane currents, orbital magnetic moment, and the phase diagram structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping ($\delta$), interlayer exchange coupling ($J_{\perp}$), and the ratio of hopping parameters ($t_{\perp}/t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the $z$-BOP gap is purely imaginary ($\phi = i \phi_{A\perp}$), and that Coulomb interactions are less critical than the spin/hopping terms for the primary instabilities.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of the real and imaginary $z$-BOP gaps versus doping, showing the dominance of the imaginary component ($\phi_{A\perp}$), and map out the phase diagram showing LC, SC, and coexisting regions.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives the coupled gap equations for SC and $z$-BOP order parameters, analyzes the resulting magnetic flux and current patterns, and maps the resulting phase diagram as a function of doping.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The recent discovery of high-$T_c$ superconductivity in pressurized and thin-film bilayer nickelates, featuring a strong interlayer exchange coupling, and their potential similarities with cuprate superconductors, has made this a very active topic in condensed matter physics. In the present paper we study the strongly correlated one-orbital ($d_{x^2-y^2}$) bilayer $t$-$J_\perp$-$V$ model for nickelates, where $V$ denotes the Coulomb interactions, using a controlled large-$N$ expansion at and beyond the mean-field level. Focusing on the out-of-plane spin exchange interaction ($J_\perp$), we find that it triggers both out-of-plane $s$-wave superconductivity and an out-of-plane bond-order phase ($z$-BOP) instability. The $z$-BOP gives rise to a complex $z$-axis hopping dominated by its imaginary component, which drives out-of-plane currents and induces in-plane ones, spontaneously forming on the vertical plaquettes a loop-current state that breaks time-reversal symmetry. Competition between this loop-current phase and superconductivity yields a dome-shaped superconducting region, with optimal superconductivity occurring near the $z$-BOP quantum critical point. The resulting phase diagram features a pure loop-current region, a low-doping coexistence phase, a pure superconducting state at higher doping, and a correlated metallic state.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces 'consensus' to measure how robustly different models agree on the effective physics of disordered systems. By analyzing the variance of inferred parameters, the authors show that the best temperature for agreement is distinct from the best temperature for accuracy due to model limitations. This provides a crucial diagnostic tool for interpreting results from complex, disordered physical models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous framework for quantifying model uncertainty and disagreement (consensus) in the presence of quenched disorder is highly relevant for interpreting complex simulations in condensed matter theory, especially when data is limited or noisy.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper quantifies the reproducibility of inferred physical descriptions (effective parameters) from finite, noisy data generated by disordered systems, using the concept of 'consensus'.

**Main result.** The temperature at which independent learners agree most closely (consensus minimum) is generally different from the temperature where their shared answer is most faithful (MSE minimum) due to inherent model misspecification bias.

**Method.** A teacher-student framework is employed where multiple students independently infer a single effective coupling from finite subsets of equilibrium configurations generated by a disordered Ising system.

**Model / system.** The system is the two-dimensional random-bond Ising model on a square lattice with quenched couplings drawn from a uniform distribution. The inference process involves fitting a single uniform effective coupling to the local field statistics.

**Key observables.** Consensus Width (variance), Mean-Squared Error (MSE), Sampling Noise variance, Quenched-Disorder variance, Residual Complexity (RC).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Disorder strength ($\delta$), Temperature ($T$), Finite sample size ($M$), and the number of students ($N_s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The students are restricted to fitting a single uniform effective coupling, which introduces a systematic misspecification bias that persists even in the infinite-data limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the inferred coupling $ar{J}$ across different kernels, showing consistency at certain temperatures, and illustrate Residual Complexity (RC), which is zero for the isotropic kernel but non-zero for the identity kernel, indicating lost structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the consensus concept by decomposing the total variance into sampling and disorder contributions. It then analyzes the bias introduced by the limited hypothesis class, comparing the optimal temperature for consensus agreement versus optimal faithfulness (MSE).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

When independent learners are trained on data from the same disordered system, finite and noisy samples can lead them to different descriptions of the underlying physics. We quantify the reproducibility of such descriptions through consensus, the degree of agreement among independently trained models. In a teacher-student framework on the two-dimensional random-bond Ising model, an ensemble of students each infers a single uniform effective coupling from a finite set of equilibrium configurations drawn from one quenched bond realization. Aggregating the inferred couplings across students and realizations yields the consensus distribution, whose width quantifies the agreement among learners. The variance of this distribution separates exactly into a finite-sampling contribution, bounded below by inverse of Fisher information, and a quenched-disorder contribution. Sum of these two variance is minimized near critical region, so the consensus width has a minimum near the pseudocritical temperature that sharpens with increasing disorder. The two minima arise from different mechanisms: the sampling term inherits the critical peak of the Fisher information, while the disorder term is temperature-independent at leading order and acquires its critical minimum only at fourth order in the disorder strength, through the covariance between the linear and cubic responses to the bond disorder. Because the students fit a uniform coupling to a heterogeneous lattice, the inferred coupling also carries a misspecification bias that survives in the infinite-data limit. This bias displaces the minimum of the mean-squared error above the consensus minimum, so the temperature at which independent learners agree most closely is not the temperature at which their shared answer is most faithful.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the ground states of 1D fluids with soft repulsive interactions. It establishes that disordered ground states only exist for potentials that are concave ($
u \ge 1$) at a critical pressure $p_i$. These disordered states are non-hyperuniform and exhibit correlation functions that predict behavior over a wider range of temperatures and pressures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding that disordered ground states can be precisely characterized by non-zero susceptibility and extended to finite temperatures provides a theoretical bridge between zero-temperature quantum/classical ground states and finite-temperature liquid behavior.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the existence and properties of disordered ground states in one-dimensional (1D) exactly solvable fluids.

**Main result.** Disordered ground states exist only for concave potentials ($
u \ge 1$) at a specific incompressibility pressure ($p_i$), characterized by non-zero isothermal susceptibility and non-hyperuniformity.

**Method.** The analysis uses the isothermal-isobaric ensemble, employing Laplace transforms and the steepest descent method to analyze the $T 	o 0$ limit of the equation of state and pair correlation function.

**Model / system.** The system consists of 1D fluids of classical hard-core particles interacting via a soft, repulsive, finite-range potential $\phi(x)$. The interactions are simplified to nearest-neighbor interactions when the interaction range $a'$ is small relative to the particle diameter $a$.

**Key observables.** Equation of state, mean nearest-neighbor distance ($l_0$), pair correlation function ($g_0(x)$), and isothermal susceptibility ($\chi_T$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The potential exponent $
u$ (determining concavity/convexity), the incompressibility pressure $p_i$, and the temperature $T$ (analyzed in the $T 	o 0$ limit).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the pairwise interactions reduce to nearest-neighbor interactions ($a' \le 2a$) and relies on the exact solvability of the model in the isothermal-isobaric ensemble.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the pair correlation function $g(x)$ for different pressures around $p_i$, showing transitions between disordered, $a$-periodic, and $a'$-periodic behavior for the square shoulder potential.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the ground state behavior by examining the potential's concavity ($
u$ vs. 1), identifying the critical pressure $p_i$, and deriving the specific forms of $l_0$ and $g_0(x)$ for the resulting ordered and disordered phases.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

One-dimensional fluids of classical hard-core particles of diameter $a$, interacting in pairs via a soft repulsive (monotonically decreasing) potential of finite range $\varphi(x)=\varepsilon \left[ (a'-x)/(a'-a)\right]^{1/ν}$ $(a\le x\le a')$ with real positive parameters $\varepsilon$ and $ν$, are studied in an isothermal-isobaric ensemble. If $a'\le 2a$, the pairwise interactions are reduced to nearest-neighbour interactions, which allows for an exact solution of the thermal equilibrium. We focus on the $T\to 0$ ground states, specifically on the equation of state for the mean distance between nearest neighbours $l_0$ and the pair correlation function $g_0(x)$. If $\varphi(x)$ is concave $(ν\ge 1)$, there exists an ``incompressibility'' pressure $p_i=\varepsilon/(a'-a)$ such that the ground state is an equidistant chain of particles with spacing $l_0=a'$ for $0<p<p_i$ and with spacing $l_0=a$ for $p>p_i$. If $ν>1$ (strict concavity), the ground state at $p=p_i$ is disordered with $l_0=\left[ νa +(ν-1)a'\right]/(2ν-1)$ and $g_0(x)$ being a superposition of weighted Dirac delta functions over discrete positions. If $ν=1$ (linear ramp), the ground state at $p=p_i$ is disordered with $l_0=(a+a')/2$ and the continuous $g_0(x)$ is a superposition of Heaviside step functions multiplied by polynomials in $x$. The isothermal susceptibility at $T=0$ is nonzero for concave $\varphi(x)$ ($ν\ge 1$) at $p=p_i$ and, therefore, the corresponding disordered ground states are non-hyperuniform, i.e., they resemble disordered fluids at nonzero temperatures. It turns out that pair correlation functions of disordered ground states, which occur only at a single pressure $p_i$, extend their predictive power to thermodynamic states at nonzero temperatures over a wider range of pressures around $p_i$.

</details>

### [EFI Pairs Without One-Way Puzzles: Oracle Separations from Communication Complexity](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11901v1)

**Authors:** Atul Mantri  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.11901v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.11901_figures/2609.11901_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Dependency structure. Solid boxes are used in the proof of Theorem 7.2; dashed boxes are not.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the relationship between two key quantum cryptographic assumptions: EFI pairs and one-way puzzles. By constructing a specific oracle, the authors prove that one can have quantum states that are hard to distinguish classically without relying on the existence of one-way puzzles. The work rigorously bounds the quantum advantage using communication complexity and random matrix theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides deep theoretical insights into the computational assumptions underpinning quantum cryptography, showing that certain quantum advantages might vanish when the input/output structure is restricted to classical information.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether the existence of EFI pairs (a quantum cryptographic primitive) necessitates the existence of one-way puzzles, a fundamental open question in quantum cryptography.

**Main result.** The authors construct a scenario relative to a specific oracle where EFI pairs exist without one-way puzzles, demonstrating that quantum polynomial time offers no advantage on tasks with purely classical inputs and outputs.

**Method.** The security proof relies on reducing the distinguishing advantage problem to classical communication complexity, specifically the Vector-in-Subspace problem, and uses random matrix theory for the final coherent query bound.

**Model / system.** The model involves analyzing the indistinguishability of two quantum states (an EFI pair) relative to an oracle that provides classical query answers about quantum samplers, all within a 'Coherent-last access' computational model.

**Key observables.** Distinguishing advantage ($	ext{Advantage}$), trace distance ($	ext{TD}$), and the bias of the quantum state separation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The parameter $\lambda$ governing the state preparation, the dimension $n$ of the subspace, and the number of queries $L$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the 'Coherent-last access' model, and the paper concludes by stating conjectures to remove the restriction on superposition queries.

**Figures summary.** No figures were explicitly described in the notes, but the analysis relies on bounding sums over different cell size regimes and using various mathematical lemmas (e.g., Lemma 6.15).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem setup using EFI pairs and one-way puzzles, then introduces the oracle model. The core is the security proof, which proceeds by reducing the advantage to communication complexity (Theorem C) and bounding the coherent query advantage using random matrix theory (Theorem D).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

EFI pairs (Brakerski, Canetti, and Qian, ITCS 2023) and one-way puzzles (Khurana and Tomer, STOC 2024) are the leading candidates for the minimal assumption of quantum cryptography. The first are efficiently preparable quantum states, statistically far yet computationally indistinguishable; the second are classical puzzles, easy to sample and hard to solve. One-way puzzles imply EFI pairs, and whether the converse holds is open.   We construct a single classical oracle relative to which one-way puzzles do not exist, even with an unbounded verifier, while an EFI pair survives every distinguisher that queries the oracle classically throughout and holds advice about it, making its one superposition query at the end. The oracle answers every question about the output probabilities of quantum samplers, which removes the puzzles, and hides a Haar-random half-dimensional subspace.   To prove security we reduce it to communication complexity. An adversary whose knowledge of the subspace arrives as classical query answers can be simulated inside a two-party protocol against the party holding it, so it does no better than the best classical protocol for Vector-in-Subspace (Klartag and Regev, STOC 2011), whatever the oracle computes. That argument does not cover the superposition query, which we bound instead using tools from random matrix theory.   The same attack gives a classical simulation of any quantum party in a classical-message protocol with no entanglement shared in advance, so relative to the oracle there is no proof of quantumness either. Quantum polynomial time therefore offers no advantage on any task with classical inputs and outputs, while the two quantum states stay indistinguishable. We state conjectures on removing the restriction on superposition queries.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.11138v1>

**Summary.** This work uses TDDFT to explore collective charge excitations in the 2DEG, finding that excitonic modes appear at low densities, distinct from simple plasmons. The theory predicts that these modes signal an instability towards a charge-density-wave phase when the electron density drops sufficiently low. The findings offer concrete, measurable fingerprints for experimental detection in tunable 2D electron systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed study of collective excitations and instability mechanisms in a fundamental model system like the 2DEG provides crucial theoretical benchmarks for understanding strongly correlated electron behavior in low-dimensional materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the existence and experimental signatures of excitonic collective charge excitations in the Two-Dimensional Electron Gas (2DEG) at low densities.

**Main result.** New excitonic modes emerge beyond traditional plasmons at low densities, and the system is predicted to become unstable towards a charge-density-wave (CDW) phase when the Wigner-Seitz radius exceeds a critical value.

**Method.** The research employs Time-Dependent Density Functional Theory (TDDFT) within the framework of the Casida equation, comparing results across various approximations for the exchange-correlation (xc) kernel.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the two-dimensional homogeneous electron gas (2D HEG), a model system whose properties are tunable by varying the electron density via the Wigner-Seitz radius ($r_s$).

**Key observables.** Loss function ($	ext{Im} \epsilon^{-1}(q, \omega)$), static linear density response ($\chi(q, \omega=0)$), and induced density oscillations ($\Delta n(r)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Wigner-Seitz radius ($r_s$), wavevector ($q$), and frequency ($\omega$). The critical instability is predicted around $r_s \gtrsim 14.455$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations for the xc kernel, comparing RPA, ALDA, DPGT, and DPGT+NCT, with the most detailed results using the DPGT+NCT parametrization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the development of asymmetric peak structures in the loss function and strong Friedel-like oscillations in the static response function as $r_s$ increases, particularly near $q=2k_F$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares the predictions of various theoretical approximations (RPA vs. DPGT+NCT) for collective excitations, focusing on how excitonic effects manifest in the loss function and the static density response, culminating in the prediction of a CDW instability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The two-dimensional homogeneous electron gas (2D HEG) is a prototype system of fundamental interest that can be realized experimentally over a wide range of densities. Here, we investigate its collective charge excitations at low densities, using time-dependent density functional theory. We demonstrate that, beyond traditional plasmons, new collective excitonic modes emerge for a Wigner-Seitz radius larger than $r_{\mathrm s} \approx 1$. These excitonic modes leave characteristic fingerprints in experimentally accessible quantities, namely, asymmetric peak structures in the loss function and very strong Friedel-like oscillations in the static linear density response that increase when the system approaches a regime of instability. Indeed, at low enough densities the collective modes cross the zero energy axis, indicating an instability of the paramagnetic 2D HEG towards the formation of a charge-density-wave phase with excitonic origin. These findings provide valuable insights for the experimental detection of excitonic collective modes in tunable 2D electron systems and contribute to the fundamental understanding of many-body effects in low-density electron gases.

</details>

**Authors:** Akash Vijay, Luis Colmenarez, Jong Yeon Lee  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.11930v1>

The hi- erarchy holds for every finite stabilizer code under independent- event Pauli noise, but can fail for more general Pauli channels. (b) Postselection gadget. One arbitrary logical data state and n −1 maximally mixed logical ancilla states are encoded into separate blocks. Each block undergoes the same noise channel and syndrome measurement. Retaining only matching syndrome outcomes implements the trace-nonincreasing map; when the success probability is input-independent, division by this probability yields...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the Rényi coherent information in quantum error correction codes, proving that under specific noise conditions (independent Bernoulli faults), this information measure is nondecreasing with the Rényi index $n$. It also provides an operational interpretation by linking the information measure to the fidelity of quantum channel recovery via syndrome postselection.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides rigorous mathematical tools to analyze the performance limits of quantum error correction codes under realistic noise models by establishing concrete, computable hierarchies for entanglement measures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses two shortcomings of Rényi coherent information ($I_c^{(n)}$): its lack of monotonicity in the Rényi index $n$ and its lack of clear operational meaning compared to the von Neumann case.

**Main result.** For Pauli noise from independent Bernoulli events, the Rényi coherent information is proven to be nondecreasing in $n$. Furthermore, an operational meaning is established via postselection on matching syndromes, linking saturation of $I_c^{(n)}$ to perfect channel recovery.

**Method.** The authors employ information-theoretic tools, including Rényi entropy calculations, correlation inequalities (like Harris's), and analyzing the structure of syndrome-conditioned quantum channels.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on stabilizer codes subjected to Pauli noise, considering both independent Bernoulli noise and general stochastic Pauli noise models.

**Key observables.** Rényi coherent information ($I_c^{(n)}$), the Rényi deficit ($\Delta_n$), and the recovery fidelity ($F_n$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Rényi index $n$, the noise model (Bernoulli vs. general stochastic Pauli noise), and the code structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial hierarchy proof relies on the assumption that the Pauli noise is generated by independent Bernoulli events; counterexamples show failure when this assumption is violated.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1(a) illustrates the nondecreasing Rényi hierarchy for a code under depolarizing noise, while Figure 1(b) depicts the postselection gadget using one data block and $n-1$ auxiliary blocks.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first proving the nondecreasing hierarchy for independent Bernoulli noise, then defining the operational meaning via matched-syndrome postselection, and finally analyzing the conditions (like $n$-balance) under which these bounds and equivalences hold.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rényi coherent information, a computable proxy for the von Neumann coherent information, is widely used to study mixed-state phases of matter and decodability transitions in noisy quantum error-correcting codes. However, being a difference of two Rényi entropies, it need not be monotonic in the Rényi index, and lacks the operational meaning of its von Neumann counterpart. Here we address both issues for stabilizer codes. First, for Pauli noise generated by independent Bernoulli events, we prove that the Rényi-$n$ coherent information is nondecreasing in $n \in \mathbb{Z}^+$. This follows from a general theorem: if independent random bits are mapped linearly to a fine label $T$ and a coarse label $C$, then the Rényi entropy difference $H_n(C)-H_n(T)$ is nondecreasing in $n$. For stabilizer codes, $T$ is the joint syndrome--logical class and $C$ is the syndrome, and the difference is the Rényi-$n$ coherent information up to a constant. The same theorem covers classical linear codes and independent detector error models. Second, for arbitrary stochastic Pauli noise, we give the Rényi-$n$ coherent information an operational meaning via postselection on matching syndromes between one data block and $n-1$ auxiliary blocks. We determine when this defines a quantum channel and show that saturation of the Rényi-$n$ coherent information is equivalent to asymptotically perfect recovery of the postselected channel. Moreover, the Rényi-$n$ coherent information also upper-bounds the ordinary coherent information achievable after any syndrome-conditioned recovery.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.10948v1>

**Summary.** This work establishes that the long-wavelength charge fluctuations in quasiperiodic systems are fundamentally controlled by the arithmetic properties of the irrational modulation frequency $eta$. It derives a scaling law linking the fluctuation exponent to both the frequency's irrationality measure and the smoothness of the system's charge profile. This provides a deep theoretical link between number theory and condensed matter physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection between number theory (irrationality measure) and physical transport/fluctuation properties in quasiperiodic lattices provides a powerful, non-trivial diagnostic tool for characterizing the underlying order.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the fundamental principle governing long-wavelength charge fluctuations in quasiperiodic systems, linking it directly to the arithmetic properties of the irrational modulation frequency.

**Main result.** The scaling of infrared charge fluctuations is governed by a relationship involving the system's regularity exponent ($r$) and the irrationality measure ($\mu(eta)$) of the frequency, specifically $\alpha + 1 = 2r / (\mu(eta) - 1)$.

**Method.** The analysis uses Fourier decomposition and scaling arguments to relate the integrated low-momentum weight (charge fluctuations) to the arithmetic properties of the quasiperiodicity.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on translation-covariant quasiperiodic systems, exemplified by the Aubry-André model, described by Hamiltonians $H(	heta)$ modulated by an irrational frequency $eta$.

**Key observables.** Charge fluctuation variance $\sigma^2(R)$, integrated infrared weight $Z(K)$, and the hyperuniformity class $\alpha$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Irrationality measure $\mu(eta)$, regularity exponent $r$, and the modulation frequency $eta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the relevant hull Fourier amplitudes obey an algebraic decay bound $|c_m| \lesssim |m|^{-r}$ and that the spectral gap is stable for the deep sector contribution to be negligible.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the charge distribution and the running average of the charge variance $\sigma_2(R)$ for different irrational frequencies ($\phi$ vs. another irrational).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical link between arithmetic properties ($\mu(eta)$) and physical observables (charge fluctuations), deriving the central scaling relation by analyzing the contributions from near-Fermi states versus the deep spectral gap.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quasiperiodic order is characterized by irrational frequencies whose rational approximability known as irrationality measure defines distinct arithmetic classes. We establish that this arithmetic classification has direct physical consequences for long-wavelength charge fluctuations. In translation-covariant quasiperiodic systems, the infrared scaling of charge fluctuation is governed by the interplay between the irrationality exponent of irrational frequency and the large-harmonic decay of the hull charge profile: the latter determines the available charge weight, while the former controls how efficiently that weight is transferred to the infrared. Consequently, all algebraic irrational frequencies share the same arithmetic scaling, whereas exceptionally well-approximable transcendental frequencies can exhibit strongly enhanced infrared fluctuation scaling. We further prove that occupied states separated from the Fermi level by a gap stable throughout the hull contribute only an analytic infrared background, leaving the nontrivial scaling to near-Fermi states. Our results extend to general translation-covariant multi-frequency quasiperiodic systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors present Krylov Edge Spectroscopy, a novel, local diagnostic tool to characterize Symmetry-Protected Topological phases in 1D quantum systems. By analyzing the spectral properties of boundary operators via Krylov recursion, the method detects topological protection without requiring full diagonalization. This technique successfully classifies phases by relating boundary dynamics to fundamental topological invariants like cohomology labels.

**Why it may be interesting.** This provides a powerful, purely local, and computationally tractable diagnostic tool to probe global topological invariants in 1D quantum systems, which is highly relevant for understanding boundary physics in condensed matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper introduces Krylov Edge Spectroscopy to develop a method for detecting and classifying one-dimensional Symmetry-Protected Topological (SPT) phases.

**Main result.** The method successfully maps the transition between topological and trivial phases to a localization-delocalization transition of the Krylov edge mode, and it can recover exact cohomology labels for models like the clock chain.

**Method.** It uses a many-body operator-space protocol based on Krylov recursion applied to local boundary observables, avoiding the need for exact diagonalization or entanglement spectra.

**Model / system.** The analysis is performed on one-dimensional systems, including cluster, clock, Haldane, and spin-1 chains, using finite-range Hamiltonians in the Gibbs state.

**Key observables.** Zero-frequency normalizability criterion, boundary ratios ($R_K, B_K$), leakage residual ($\epsilon_K$), and the minimum stiffness ($\kappa_{\min}$) for classification.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Krylov depth ($K$), the system size ($L$), and the symmetry group structure ($\mathbb{Z}_N 	imes \mathbb{Z}_N$ or $D_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the existence of a unique self-adjoint realization for the infinite tridiagonal operator and relies on the exponential locality of 1D Gibbs states.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a logical progression: first, establishing the theoretical framework via Krylov recursion and boundary tests for detection; second, detailing the classification procedure by minimizing local commutator norms across symmetry sectors; and finally, demonstrating the protocol's success on benchmark models like the clock and spin-1 chains.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce $Krylov$ $edge$ $spectroscopy$, a many-body operator-space protocol for detecting and classifying one-dimensional symmetry-protected topological phases from local boundary dynamics. A Hermitian boundary operator generates a semi-infinite Krylov hopping chain whose boundary weight obeys an exact zero-frequency normalizability criterion. Open-periodic, boundary-bulk, and symmetry-preserving boundary-perturbation tests identify protected boundary memory, while a finite-depth leakage residual certifies when an explicitly reconstructed operator is already near zero frequency. Classification minimizes the normalized commutator over symmetry-resolved boundary operators in fixed charge sectors. For bosonic $\mathbb{Z}_N \times \mathbb{Z}_N$ phases, the recovered endpoint charge gives the cohomology label. The method requires neither many-body exact diagonalization, an explicit ground-state wavefunction or entanglement spectrum, nor a guessed dressed edge or string operator. For finite-range Hamiltonians, locality organizes the thermodynamic limit at fixed Krylov depth before the depth limit. We demonstrate the protocol in cluster, clock, Haldane, and trivial spin-1 chains. In the exactly solvable cluster chain, the transition between the gapped topological and trivial phases manifests as a localization-delocalization transition of the Krylov edge mode on the Krylov chain. This transition occurs precisely at the bulk gap closing, and its localization-length exponent coincides with the Ising correlation-length exponent. Through operator Krylov dynamics, our work turns local boundary evolution into a direct spectroscopy of many-body topology.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes a novel framework linking quantum states in quantum gravity to specific, finite Lorentzian spacetime regions. It defines quantum states using elliptic data manifolds and calculates entanglement entropy by relating it to geometric quantities like the maximin surface area. This offers a powerful, general tool for exploring quantum information structure in regimes beyond standard holographic assumptions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum gravity, the rigorous calculation of entanglement entropy via geometric quantities (maximin surface) and the development of a general state formalism are highly relevant to understanding quantum information structure in curved spacetime, which underpins many-body physics concepts.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish a fundamental correspondence between quantum states defined on a boundary manifold and the geometry of finite Lorentzian spacetime regions in quantum gravity.

**Main result.** A broad correspondence is proposed linking quantum states $J(\sigma)$ to Lorentzian wedges $w_\sigma$, and the von Neumann entropy is shown to be controlled by the maximin surface area, echoing holographic principles.

**Method.** The framework defines quantum states using elliptic data manifolds and calculates entanglement entropy via partial traces and the Gravitational Path Integral (GPI) evaluated on closed manifolds.

**Model / system.** The system involves defining a gravitational Hilbert space $\mathbf{H}_\sigma$ associated with a compact boundary $\sigma$, and analyzing the quantum state $\mathcal{J}(\sigma)$ which is a $d$-manifold bounded by $\sigma$.

**Key observables.** Rényi entropies ($S_n$) and the von Neumann entropy ($S_1$), which are predicted to be proportional to the maximin surface area.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The gravitational coupling constant $G_N$, the dimension $d$, and the geometry of the boundary $\sigma$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the Hilbert space factorizes over connected components of $\sigma$, and the classical limit requires the GPI to be dominated by a single saddle geometry with Lorentzian fixed points.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the construction of quantum states via complex deformations from Lorentzian wedges, the calculation of reduced states via partial gluing, and the comparison of actions between Euclidean and Wall saddles.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the mathematical framework by defining the Hilbert space and state structure, establishing the correspondence to classical spacetime regions, and then applying this structure to compute entanglement entropy for specific examples like black hole boundaries.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We associate a gravitational Hilbert space $\mathbf{H}_σ$ to any closed compact $(d-1)$-manifold $σ$ with real metric. A quantum state $\mathcal{J}(σ)$ is a $d$-manifold bounded by $σ$ and equipped with elliptic data. An inner product is defined by gluing states pairwise across $σ$ and evaluated by viewing the resulting closed $d$-manifold as a boundary condition on the gravitational path integral (GPI) over $(d+1)$-manifolds.   If $σ$ is nonempty and the GPI is dominated by a single $(d+1)$-manifold $M$ in the $G_N\to 0$ limit, then $M$ contains a Lorentzian CRT fixed-point set, providing $\mathcal{J}(σ)$ with a classical spacetime interpretation. Conversely, given a finite Lorentzian domain with edge $σ$, a state $\mathcal{J}(σ)$ may be associated to it by deforming its initial data off the real Lorentzian section and retaining only elliptic data. This establishes a broad correspondence between non-asymptotic spacetime regions and quantum states.   Assuming that $\mathbf{H}_σ$ factorizes over connected components of $σ$, our framework admits operators and partial traces. This allows us to explore the information-theoretic structure of the states we define. As an example, we construct a family of states by deforming partial Cauchy slices $Σ$ that straddle a two-sided black hole; $σ$ consists of two spheres. We construct the reduced state on one sphere and find that its Rényi entropies are positive, monotonic, and sensitive to all aspects of $Σ$ and its complex deformation. The von Neumann entropy, however, is controlled only by the maximin surface in the causal domain of $Σ$, independently of other parameters, so long as the complex deformation does not vanish. Our proposal may thus explain the efficacy of tensor network toy models of holography while transcending their limitations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a rigorous determination of the sample complexity required to learn an unknown n-qubit stabilizer state. It introduces a novel, efficient quantum algorithm that achieves the optimal scaling for this task. The work is significant for quantum information theory as it provides tight bounds and a practical method for characterizing quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum computation, the rigorous analysis of state learning complexity and the use of Fourier analysis on group structures provide deep theoretical tools applicable to characterizing entanglement and resource estimation in quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the minimum number of copies required to learn an unknown pure n-qubit stabilizer state and subsequently derives the complexity for learning associated Clifford unitaries.

**Main result.** They establish tight bounds for the sample complexity, $L_\delta(n) \approx n + \log(1/\delta)$, and present a polynomial-time quantum algorithm that saturates these bounds.

**Method.** The approach uses a multi-stage quantum algorithm involving reduction to a full-support state, isotypic compression, and applying the Quantum Fourier Transform over a specific abelian group structure.

**Model / system.** The system under study is the $n$-qubit stabilizer state, which are specific quantum states generated by the Clifford group acting on computational basis states.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the sample complexity, $L_\delta(n)$, which quantifies the minimum number of state copies needed for successful identification.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters are the number of qubits ($n$), the failure probability ($\delta$), and the structure of the underlying abelian group $\Upsilon$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to implement random Clifford operations and relies on bounding probabilities using techniques like the Hellinger overlap.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides an overview of the four core stages of the proposed stabilizer-learning algorithm, detailing the sequence of operations from state reduction to tableau reconstruction.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a logical progression: first, establishing the problem bounds; second, detailing the four-stage quantum algorithm (reduction, compression, Fourier transform, reconstruction); and finally, analyzing the computational complexity of each step.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

It is well-known that learning a pure $n$-qubit stabilizer state $|ψ\rangle$ both requires, and can be accomplished with, access to a number of copies of $|ψ\rangle$ linear in $n$. However, the precise constant coefficient of this scaling does not appear to have been determined. Here we prove that $L_δ(n)$, the smallest number of copies from which a quantum procedure can identify any stabilizer state with failure probability at most $0<δ<1/8$, satisfies $n+\lceil\log_2(1/δ)\rceil-3\leq L_δ(n)\leq n+\left\lceil\log_2(1/δ)\right\rceil+4$. We present a polynomial-time quantum learning algorithm that saturates this bound, achieving a constant factor improvement in sample-complexity over previously known approaches. As an immediate corollary, we obtain via the Choi-Jamiolkowski isomorphism an algorithm for learning an unknown $n$-qubit Clifford unitary from $2n+\left\lceil\log_2(1/δ)\right\rceil+4$ queries, the $n$-dependence of which we show to be optimal. Our proof technique, which involves Fourier analysis on the abelian group $\mathbb{Z}_4^n \times \mathbb{F}_2^{n(n-1)/2}$, seems to be qualitatively different to previous approaches to stabilizer state learning, and may be of some independent interest; in particular, it admits natural generalisations to further problems in quantum learning theory.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents Selected Coupled Cluster (SCC), a novel, sparse quantum chemistry method designed to overcome the computational limitations of high-rank Coupled Cluster theory. SCC leverages the sparsity inherent in Selected Configuration Interaction (SCI) wavefunctions to guide the optimization of CC amplitudes. It demonstrates that SCC provides highly accurate and rapidly converging energy estimates, making it a powerful, drop-in enhancement for modern electronic structure calculations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum chemistry, the development of highly efficient, sparse, and systematic many-body wavefunction methods like SCC provides algorithmic insights applicable to simulating strongly correlated quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The computational cost scaling of high-rank Coupled Cluster (CC) theory limits its application for accurate electronic structure calculations.

**Main result.** Selected Coupled Cluster (SCC) is introduced as an efficient, arbitrary-order sparse CC method that outperforms standard SCI and CCSD in the tight-threshold regime.

**Method.** SCC solves the CC residual equations by optimizing amplitudes within a compact, sparse amplitude space guided by a modern Selected Configuration Interaction (SCI) wavefunction.

**Model / system.** The method applies to electronic structure problems governed by the time-independent Schrödinger Equation, using molecular systems like butane and circumcoronene as benchmarks.

**Key observables.** Energy (E), convergence rate, and the number of selected amplitudes required for convergence.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The SCI variational threshold ($\epsilon_{var}$), which guides the amplitude selection; the rank of the CC excitations (T).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the sparsity of the ground-state wavefunction can be effectively exploited by guiding the CC amplitude space using SCI methods.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the error in correlation energy relative to extrapolated DMRG energy for butane, and the relationship between SCC amplitude count and the SCI threshold for various systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces SCC as an enhancement to SCI workflows, details the implementation using sparse data structures and JIT compilation, and benchmarks its performance against SCI, CCSD, CCSDT, and DMRG across several chemical systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Coupled Cluster (CC) theory is one of the most popular correlated wavefunction methods, yet the steep computational cost scaling of CC truncated at higher rank limits its application. Modern Selected Configuration Interaction (SCI) methods successfully exploit the sparsity of ground-state wavefunctions in the determinant space, motivating an analogous approach with the exponential CC ansatz. In this work, we introduce Selected Coupled Cluster (SCC), an arbitrary-order sparse CC method that explicitly solves the CC residual equations within a compact amplitude space guided by an SCI wavefunction. SCC optimizes the CC amplitudes in the sparse amplitude space. Using a pilot Just-in-Time (JIT) compiled implementation, we benchmark SCC against SCI, SCI+PT2, standard rank-truncated dense CC (CCSD, CCSDT), and DMRG across various chemical systems. We demonstrate that SCC exhibits fast, monotonic convergence with the SCI variational threshold, outperforming SCI and surpassing SCI+PT2 in the tight-threshold regime. SCC also provides accurate energies for high-dimensional topologies, where DMRG typically converges slowly with bond dimension. SCC thus presents an efficient drop-in enhancement for modern SCI workflows.

</details>

**Summary.** This work rigorously compares the resource costs of simulating quantum dynamics classically versus running them on quantum hardware. By analyzing multiple metrics—sample, time, and monetary cost—the authors demonstrate that classical simulability alone is insufficient to determine the best implementation strategy. This has major implications for designing near-term quantum algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a crucial, practical framework for quantum algorithm assessment, warning that theoretical polynomial-time simulation guarantees do not translate to practical hardware or resource efficiency.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether quantum dynamics are more resource-efficient to simulate classically or to execute directly on quantum hardware, moving beyond simple asymptotic polynomial-time guarantees.

**Main result.** The preferred implementation (classical vs. quantum) depends critically on the specific resource metric (sample, time, cost) and the number of circuit instances evaluated, showing no universal advantage.

**Method.** The authors analyze resource scaling by comparing the complexity of quantum simulation (QS) against various structured classical simulation techniques (CS) for different circuit families.

**Model / system.** The focus is on general quantum circuits and dynamics, analyzed through expectation value estimation of observables $f_U(ho, O) = 	ext{Tr}(Uho U^\dagger O)$. Specific models include Clifford circuits, free-fermionic dynamics, and QCNN/ADAPT-VQE architectures.

**Key observables.** Expectation values $	ext{Tr}(Uho U^\dagger O)$, Quantum Sample complexity ($N_{Q.S.}$), Quantum Time complexity ($N_{Q.T.}$), and Classical Time complexity ($N_{C.T.}$)

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of circuit instances ($T$), number of qubits ($n$), circuit depth ($L$), and approximation thresholds ($	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes repeated access to independently prepared copies of the input state $ho$ and that the classical simulation relies on structured decompositions (e.g., Pauli strings, Majorana subspaces).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically compares QS vs. CS. Figure 6 estimates the monetary cost for various hardware platforms for specific circuit families.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the core problem by noting the limitations of asymptotic complexity, then analyzes specific, well-studied classically simulable circuit families (Clifford, free-fermionic, etc.) using multiple resource metrics, and concludes by comparing costs for modern variational algorithms like QCNN and ADAPT-VQE.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Determining whether a quantum evolution can be efficiently simulated classically is central to understanding the boundary between classical and quantum computation. However, polynomial-time simulability is an asymptotic statement, and does not by itself determine whether the (quantum-inspired) classical simulation is actually practical. Indeed, different polynomial scalings can lead to vastly different computational costs, particularly when expensive preprocessing or quantum data acquisition is required. In this work, we ask whether classically simulable quantum dynamics are in practice more resource-efficient to simulate classically than to execute directly on quantum hardware. We analyze this question using three resource metrics, quantum sample, quantum time, and classical time complexity, for several widely studied classically simulable circuit families. Using representative hardware-level estimates, we identify regimes in which quantum simulation can be faster despite the existence of a polynomial-time classical algorithm, as well as regimes in which classical simulation remains more efficient. At the same time, the large quantum sampling cost needed to characterize unknown input states can make this polynomial-time classical simulation prohibitively expensive with current cloud-based hardware access prices. Ultimately, our work indicates that guarantees of classical simulability with polynomial resources alone are insufficient to determine the preferred implementation.

</details>
