**Summary.** This paper reports the synthesis and detailed characterization of the novel compound Fe0.68Pd0.80Te. Key findings include the confirmation of a 3x3x3 vacancy superlattice and the identification of a spin-glass magnetic state below 40 K. Electrically, the material is characterized as a semiconductor with a very small band gap, suggesting potential for quantum electronic applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** The combination of vacancy ordering (a form of structural disorder) leading to a spin-glass state and semiconducting behavior makes it relevant for studying disorder-induced quantum phenomena in layered materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to characterize a new ternary compound, Fe0.68Pd0.80Te, focusing on the role of vacancy ordering and its resulting physical properties.

**Main result.** The material exhibits a spin-glass ground state below 40 K, is a semiconductor with a very small band gap (< 10 meV), and possesses a confirmed 3x3x3 Pd-vacancy order.

**Method.** The research employed a combination of structural characterization (XRD, nc-AFM), magnetic measurements (MPMS), and electrical transport measurements (PPMS-14T).

**Model / system.** The physical system is the layered ternary compound Fe0.68Pd0.80Te, which adopts an alpha-Fe1+xTe-type tetragonal structure. The key structural feature is the presence of a vacancy-ordered superstructure, which influences its electronic and magnetic behavior.

**Key observables.** Spin-glass transition temperature (Tg ~ 40 K), semiconductor band gap (< 10 meV), hole-like charge carriers, and the 3x3x3 Pd-vacancy superlattice order.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (T), magnetic field (H), and the vacancy ordering parameter (3x3x3).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The structural refinement assumes the material maintains a tetragonal structure despite significant chemical substitution and vacancy ordering. The small band gap suggests semiconductor behavior.

**Figures summary.** Figures show XRD patterns confirming the structure, magnetic susceptibility curves demonstrating a spin-glass state, and resistivity vs. temperature plots indicating semiconducting behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a standard materials science structure: structural identification and characterization (XRD, AFM) $ightarrow$ magnetic property measurement (Spin-glass state) $ightarrow$ electronic transport analysis (Semiconducting gap, MR/Hall effect) $ightarrow$ Conclusion on quantum potential.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report the identification and characterization of a new compound Fe0.68Pd0.80Te with an α-Fe1+xTe prototype structure. Different from the Fe-square net and minor occupancy of interstitial Fe-sites in Fe1+xTe, Fe0.68Pd0.80Te is featured by a Pdsquare net and near 68% occupancy of the corresponding interstitial Fe-sites. Furthermore, noncontact atomic force microscopy and X-ray diffraction provide evidence for the existence of a 3*3*3 Pd-vacancy order in this layered material. A spin-glass ground state below Tg 40 K is identified via magnetic characterization. Electrical transport measurements show that Fe0.68Pd0.80Te is a semiconductor with a very small band gap below 10 meV. It has weak negative magnetoresistance and holelike charge carriers below room temperature. Our results demonstrate its potentials for further exploring various quantum phenomena.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Virtual Quantum Neural Networks (VQNNs) as an extension to standard QNNs to enhance expressivity and noise robustness in quantum machine learning. VQNNs achieve this by combining multiple quantum circuits and classically aggregating their measurement results. This generalized approach is shown to improve performance in tasks like quantum error mitigation and ground-state energy estimation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of VQNNs provides a powerful, generalized framework for quantum machine learning that explicitly addresses the limitations imposed by the restricted set of quantum operations available on near-term hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To improve upon standard Quantum Neural Networks (QNNs) by developing a more general model, the Virtual Quantum Neural Network (VQNN), to increase expressivity and improve noise robustness in quantum machine learning.

**Main result.** VQNNs extend QNNs by sampling over multiple quantum circuits and classically post-processing the measurement outcomes, allowing access to linear combinations of completely positive maps.

**Method.** The VQNN loss function is minimized by optimizing parameters $(	heta, c)$ using classical optimization methods, which involves estimating expectation values via quantum sampling.

**Model / system.** The model extends standard QNNs (parametrized by unitaries $U(	heta)$) to VQNNs, which utilize a linear combination of multiple quantum circuits, represented by coefficients $c_i$ and unitaries $U_i(	heta^{(i)})$.

**Key observables.** Expectation values of Hermitian operators $O$, and the final estimated ground-state energy $E_0$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coefficients $c = (c_1, \dots, c_N)$ and the circuit parameters $	heta = (	heta^{(1)}, \dots, 	heta^{(N)})$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the sampling overhead $\|c\|_1$ can be bounded to prevent the VQNN from worsening the barren plateau problem.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the training process comparison between standard QNNs (single unitary) and VQNNs (sampling multiple PQCs and aggregating data).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the VQNN model, details the mathematical formulation for both noiseless and noisy regimes, analyzes its performance in tasks like error mitigation and classification, and discusses theoretical aspects like barren plateau analysis and SDP formulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum neural networks are a prominent model of quantum machine learning. Their training consists in the minimization of a given loss function over a parametrized family of quantum circuits, mathematically described by unitary operators, or, more generally, completely positive linear maps. In this work, we extend the notion of quantum neural network, using random sampling and classical data processing to enlarge the optimization space in a way that includes linear combinations of completely positive maps. Our extended model, called virtual quantum neural networks, leverages its enlarged optimization space to achieve increased expressivity and improved noise robustness. These benefits are illustrated in three representative tasks: quantum error mitigation, binary classification, and estimation of ground-state energies. Overall, virtual quantum neural networks offer a flexible learning paradigm that expands the space of achievable computations and strengthens the applications of near-term quantum hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the magnetic deformation of the 3D Ising Field Theory using advanced theoretical techniques, specifically comparing results from the Fuzzy Sphere and TCSA approaches. The authors successfully extract universal quantities, finding evidence for a bound state within the theory's spectrum. This work advances the understanding of critical systems on curved manifolds by bridging CFT and lattice/geometric methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of CFT techniques (TCSA) to study critical phenomena and phase transitions (Ising model) provides deep insights into how boundary conditions (the sphere) and external fields (magnetic deformation) modify universal critical exponents and low-energy spectra.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on the magnetic deformation of the (2+1)d Ising Conformal Field Theory (CFT), or Ising Field Theory (IFT), when placed on a spatial sphere.

**Main result.** The authors find evidence that the IFT contains a bound-state with a small binding energy, and they derive quantitative relations for key observables like the vacuum energy and mass gap.

**Method.** The research employs two complementary theoretical frameworks: the Fuzzy Sphere (FS) setup and the Truncated Conformal Space Approach (TCSA) to model the system's Hamiltonian and extract universal quantities.

**Model / system.** The system is the 3D Ising Field Theory (IFT) subjected to a magnetic deformation. The analysis uses Hamiltonians derived from both the FS model and the general CFT deformation structure, involving couplings like $g$ and $h_z$.

**Key observables.** Vacuum energy ($E_{vac}$), mass gap ($m_{	ext{gap}}$), and the mass of a potential bound-state ($m_{BS}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The magnetic coupling $h_z$, the deformation parameter $g$, and the large volume limit ($N 	o \infty$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the validity of local effective field theory descriptions on the sphere and involves approximations inherent to the TCSA and FS methods.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the spectrum of Ising Field theory in 2D, while Tables 1 and 2 provide lists of conformal dimensions, spins, and OPE coefficients used in the TCSA calculations.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the machinery to calculate the Hamiltonian matrix elements for the deformed IFT using TCSA, comparing these results with those obtained from the Fuzzy Sphere formulation to extract physical observables.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the magnetic deformation of the $(2+1)$d Ising CFT, also known as Ising Field Theory (IFT), on a spatial sphere. We use the Fuzzy Sphere (FS) Ising setup and compare it to the Truncated Conformal Space Approach (TCSA). Universal, infinite volume, IFT quantities are extracted by modeling the effects of curvature, as expected from a local effective theory on the sphere. We find evidence that IFT contains a bound-state with a small binding energy. Locality also allows us to extract the same IFT quantities from different angular momentum sectors, providing additional consistency checks.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper establishes a near-quartic separation between the Certificate Complexity and the quantum query complexity for a specially constructed Boolean function. By constructing this function and rigorously analyzing its quantum query complexity, the authors resolve a long-standing open problem in computational complexity theory. The result provides a concrete measure of how much quantum computation can outperform classical certificate verification methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the topic is purely computational complexity, the techniques used—such as analyzing query complexity and deriving lower bounds—are foundational to understanding the limits of quantum computation, which is relevant to quantum information theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the open question of determining the maximum possible separation gap between Certificate Complexity ($C(f)$) and bounded-error quantum query complexity ($Q(f)$) for total Boolean functions.

**Main result.** The authors construct a specific total Boolean function $f$ demonstrating a near-quartic separation, showing that $Q(f) = \widetilde{O}(\sqrt[4]{C(f)})$, which closes the gap up to logarithmic factors.

**Method.** The methodology involves constructing the function $f$ using a 'cheat-sheet totalization' based on position lists and analyzing the quantum query complexity using graph search procedures and error bounding techniques.

**Model / system.** The analysis is purely theoretical, focusing on the complexity measures ($C(f)$ and $Q(f)$) associated with abstract total Boolean functions $f$ defined over input bits.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the quantum query complexity $Q(f)$ and the certificate complexity $C(f)$, with the derived bound $Q(f) = \widetilde{O}(\sqrt[4]{C(f)})$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size parameter $n$ of the function $f$, and derived parameters like $r$ (number of disjoint inputs) and $	au_n$ related to the function's structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction relies on the existence of position lists satisfying specific combinatorial inequalities (Lemma 3.1), and the quantum analysis assumes the existence of specific unitary circuits and error bounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon established complexity relations, details the construction of the function $f$ using position lists, analyzes the quantum query complexity via graph search procedures (FindSource), and concludes by proving the separation bound through rigorous error analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct a total Boolean function $f$ for which $Q(f) = \widetilde{O}(\sqrt[4]{C(f)})$, where $Q(f)$ denotes the bounded-error quantum query complexity and $C(f)$ denotes the certificate complexity. This resolves a longstanding open question and is tight up to logarithmic factors.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a new generation of correlation-consistent effective core potentials (ccECPs) specifically designed for heavy 5d and 6p elements. By employing a sophisticated, tiered core-valence partitioning strategy and optimizing against high-level $	ext{CCSD}(	ext{T})$ calculations, the potentials achieve exceptional accuracy and transferability. This advancement removes a major bottleneck for predictive simulations in heavy-element materials science.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous treatment of relativistic effects and the development of highly accurate, transferable potentials are crucial for any advanced many-body simulation involving heavy elements, which is a key area of condensed matter and quantum chemistry.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The development of highly accurate and transferable effective core potentials (ccECPs) for heavy elements (5d and 6p blocks) to correctly model electronic structure.

**Main result.** The new ccECPs achieve outstanding atomic precision (0.045 eV deviation) and robust molecular transferability (dissociation energy errors < 0.03 eV), overcoming limitations in modeling heavy-element systems.

**Method.** The potentials are derived by optimizing parameters against high-fidelity all-electron benchmarks calculated using $	ext{CCSD}(	ext{T})$ theory, incorporating a tiered core-valence partitioning strategy.

**Model / system.** The focus is on heavy elements from the 5d (Hf, Os, Hg) and 6p (Tl, Po, At, Rn) blocks. The underlying theory involves partitioning the semi-local SO relativistic ECP ($	ext{SOREP}$) into scalar and spin-orbit components.

**Key observables.** Global average atomic low-lying states deviation (0.045 eV), dissociation energy discrepancies (< 0.03 eV), and equilibrium bond lengths (< 0.005 Å).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The core-valence partitioning uses three resolutions: 60-core, 78-core, and 68-core, optimizing the balance between accuracy and computational efficiency.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that core-valence correlation effects can be systematically captured through the tiered partitioning strategy, and the optimization relies on $	ext{CCSD}(	ext{T})$ reference data.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare Mean Absolute Deviation (MAD), Low-Lying Mean Absolute Deviation (LMAD), and Weighted Mean Absolute Deviation (WMAD) of the new ECPs against all-electron references.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the theoretical framework for the ccECPs, outlines the tiered partitioning strategy, describes the optimization process using various objective functions, and presents benchmark comparisons demonstrating superior accuracy and transferability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We expand the correlation-consistent effective core potentials (ccECPs) library by developing semi-local pseudopotentials and matching basis sets by heavy-elements from $5d$ (Hf, Os, Hg) and $6p$ (Tl, Po, At, Rn) blocks. In order to accurately capture scalar relativistic effects, spin-orbit coupling, and electron-electron correlation, we implement a tiered core-valence partitioning strategy across three distinct resolutions. This includes a small 60-core (Hf, Os, Hg) that explicitly correlates subvalence shells, a large 78-core definition for the main-group elements that rigorously accounts for core polarization and relaxation effects in sparse valence environments, and an intermediate 68-core partition for Hg and Tl. This 68-core architecture represents a unique development in the ccECP library, optimizing the balance between accuracy and computational efficiency in a manner unexplored for ligther elements. Optimized against relativistic all-electron CCSD(T) references, the ccECPs deliver outstanding atomic precision, achieving a global average atomic low-lying states deviation of just 0.045 eV. This accuracy translates directly to robust molecular transferability, systematically restricting dissociation energy discrepancies to under 0.03 eV, equilibrium bond lengths to within 0.005 Å. By enforcing a regularized, finite potential at the origin for enhanced numerical stability in stochastic quantum Monte Carlo methods, this library removes a critical methodological bottleneck for predictive many-body simulations of heavy-element systems and materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel classical algorithm to simulate quantum routines for learning with optimized random features. It overcomes the challenge of unavailable direct matrix access by using a diagonal-truncation method to reduce the problem to a small principal block. The result provides a polynomial-time classical sampler with guaranteed accuracy, advancing the field of quantum-inspired computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete example of how to bridge the gap between quantum computational models and classical algorithms in machine learning, which is highly relevant for developing practical, resource-efficient quantum-inspired ML techniques.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a classical algorithm to dequantize quantum-advantage candidates, specifically the sampler based on Quantum Singular Value Transformation (QSVT) for learning with optimized random features, when direct sampling access to the required composite matrix is unavailable.

**Main result.** The authors developed a classical sampler that successfully dequantizes the quantum routine, achieving prescribed accuracy with a runtime polynomial in the dimension $D$. This shows that classical structure can be extracted even when direct matrix access is impossible.

**Method.** The method employs a diagonal-truncation approximation, restricting the problem to a small principal block indexed by 'heavy coordinates' identified via sampling. Error bounds are established using the Lipschitz continuity of the matrix function $f$ and the operator norm.

**Model / system.** The problem involves approximating a matrix function $f(M)$ where $M = \gamma I + D A D$. $D$ is a diagonal matrix supplied via sampling-and-query access, and $A$ is a Hermitian matrix supplied via entry queries.

**Key observables.** Approximation error bounded by $
u$, Total Variation Distance $dTV(D_\epsilon, D') \le \delta$, and the final classical sampler's runtime complexity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The error tolerance $\epsilon_D$, the approximation error $
u$, the total variation distance $\delta$, and the dimension $D$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the availability of specific oracle access ($SQ(D)$ and $Q(A)$) and relies on bounding the error using the Lipschitz constant $L$ of the function $f$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the process of Diagonal truncation, showing how samples identify heavy coordinates and restrict the computation to a principal block.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem of dequantizing $f(\gamma I + DAD)$ under limited access. It develops the diagonal-truncation approximation, establishing error bounds. Subsequently, it proves the existence of a classical randomized algorithm (Theorem 2) to sample from the required distribution $D_\epsilon$, culminating in a final complexity analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum-inspired classical algorithms have dequantized several quantum machine learning routines by replacing quantum linear-algebra subroutines with classical counterparts. However, the sampler based on quantum singular value transformation (QSVT) for learning with optimized random features is not covered by existing dequantization frameworks, because the matrix to be inverted is not itself available through sampling access. In this work, we develop a classical algorithm to address this type of quantum-advantage candidate. Our method samples heavy indices, reduces the transformation to a small principal block, and outputs a sparse classical representation with operator-norm guarantees. Applying this method dequantizes the sampler for optimized random features, giving a classical sampler with prescribed accuracy and polynomially related runtime. These results show that the factorization underlying a quantum block encoding can itself provide sufficient classical structure even when sampling-and-query access to the composite matrix is unavailable.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper unifies the theoretical description of collective excitations (magnons and orbitons) in altermagnets by developing an extended spin-orbital model. It demonstrates that the observed spectral splitting arises from two distinct physical origins: lattice asymmetry and orbital ordering. The methodology combines mean-field theory with Monte Carlo simulations to provide a comprehensive understanding of the coupled spin-orbital dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed, unified theoretical framework for understanding coupled spin-orbital excitations in magnetic materials, which is highly relevant to modern condensed matter physics research on emergent magnetism.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a unified theoretical framework to describe the collective excitations (magnons and orbitons) in altermagnets, which arise from two distinct microscopic mechanisms: lattice asymmetry and orbital ordering.

**Main result.** The authors successfully develop a unified model showing that the characteristic chiral-splitting in the excitation spectra originates from two complementary contributions: one from lattice asymmetry and one from orbital-order-induced exchange anisotropy.

**Method.** The study employs an extended Kugel'-Khomskiĭ spin-orbital model analyzed using a self-consistent mean-field spin-wave orbital-wave formalism, complemented by classical Monte Carlo simulations.

**Model / system.** The system is an altermagnet modeled on a decorated square lattice, described by an extended anisotropic Heisenberg Hamiltonian coupling spin and orbital degrees of freedom. The model incorporates both inequivalent non-magnetic environments and correlation-driven orbital ordering.

**Key observables.** Magnon and orbiton excitation spectra, chiral-splitting magnitude, and finite-temperature population densities ($\varrho_A, \varrho_B$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), lattice coupling constants ($J, K$), and the degree of orbital/spin ordering.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The mean-field approach assumes the decoupling of spin and orbital sectors, and the analysis often neglects full quantum fluctuations in favor of mean-field or classical Monte Carlo treatments.

**Figures summary.** The notes detail the derivation of symmetry relations for Bogolyubov coefficients and the calculation of temperature-dependent population densities for magnon and orbiton modes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical problem and model Hamiltonian, to applying mean-field theory to derive excitation spectra, analyzing symmetry properties using Bogolyubov transformations, and finally investigating finite-temperature behavior using both analytical methods and Monte Carlo simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnetism has recently emerged as a distinct collinear magnetic phase exhibiting momentum-dependent spin-splitting despite vanishing net magnetization, as a consequence of inequivalent non-magnetic environments. Lately, it has been proposed that strong electronic correlations may yield spontaneous altermagnetism due to orbital ordering even for equivalent non-magnetic environments. While previous studies have largely focused on the electronic structure, a unified understanding of the collective excitations associated with these two different microscopic mechanisms stabilizing altermagnetism remains absent. Here, we develop an extended Kugel'-Khomskiĭ spin-orbital model on a decorated square lattice that simultaneously incorporates inequivalent non-magnetic environments and correlation-driven orbital ordering within a common theoretical framework. Employing a self-consistent mean-field spin-wave orbital-wave formalism, we demonstrate the emergence of mutually unhybridized but interdependent magnon and orbiton excitations exhibiting characteristic chiral-splitting. We show that the splitting originates from two distinct microscopic contributions: a lattice-dependent term arising from inequivalent non-magnetic environments and an orbital-order-induced exchange-anisotropy term that survives even for equivalent non-magnetic environments. The proposed framework therefore unifies the collective excitations associated with both types of altermagnetism. We further investigate the finite-temperature evolution of the coupled spin-orbital system, revealing the breakdown of spin-wave and orbital-wave approximations through spurious 1st-order transitions, while complementary classical Monte Carlo simulations recover the expected continuous 2nd-order behaviour. Our work establishes a unified microscopic framework for understanding collective magnon and orbiton excitations in altermagnets.

</details>

### [Absolutely Maximally Entangled States of $2q$ Parties in Every Odd Prime-Power Dimension $q$](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11796v1)

**Authors:** Mykhailo Hontarenko, Karol Życzkowski  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.11796v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<sub>Figure 1. AME existence status for 2 ≤d ≤17 and 2 ≤N ≤34 [2, 7, 8, 10–12]. Black frames mark Theorem 1 and its projection (dashed for previously known q ≤7); dots mark the covered local dimensions. Black checks mark 14 points from Table I and eight from the supplementary families; four table entries lie outside the plot. Gray checks mark other 2026 results [11, 12]; orange rings mark supplementary pairs beyond the short-length range. Green: known stabilizer states; dark green: known AME states without a stabilizer realization; yellow hatching: unsettled existence with a stabilizer obstruction [14–17]. Red: nonexistence; white: unsettled existence. See also Ref. [18] on AME(4, 6)....</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper constructs a family of Absolutely Maximally Entangled (AME) quantum states for dimensions $q$ that are odd prime powers. The construction is deeply rooted in algebraic coding theory, utilizing Hermitian self-dual MDS codes over finite fields. These states are significant because they represent extreme entanglement useful for quantum error correction and defining optimal quantum resources.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous construction of highly entangled states like AME states provides fundamental resources for quantum error correction and quantum computation, pushing the boundaries of known entanglement bounds.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is the construction of Absolutely Maximally Entangled (AME) states, which represent an extreme form of multipartite entanglement.

**Main result.** The authors construct stabilizer AME$(2q, q)$ states for every odd prime power $q=p^e$, and derive corresponding AME$(2q-1, q)$ states.

**Method.** The construction heavily relies on generating Hermitian self-dual Maximum Distance Separable (MDS) codes using bordered-circulant matrices over finite fields $\mathbb{F}_{q^2}$.

**Model / system.** The system is a pure quantum state $|\Psiangle \in (\mathbb{C}^d)^{\otimes N}$ defined by its entanglement properties, specifically requiring its reduced density matrix to be maximally mixed for small subsets of parties.

**Key observables.** The entanglement structure, quantified by the maximal mixing of reduced density matrices, and the associated quantum code parameters (e.g., $[2q, q, q+1]_{q^2}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $q$ must be an odd prime power ($q=p^e, p 	ext{ odd prime}$). The state parameters are $(N, d) = (2q, q)$ and $(2q-1, q)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction relies on specific algebraic properties within finite fields ($\mathbb{F}_{q^2}$) and the existence of certain matrices satisfying specific structural equations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the existence status of AME states for various parameters, marking the newly constructed cases. Table I collects explicit examples of parent states and their one-party projections.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the AME property, linking it to MDS codes via specific matrices ($A_q$), deriving the explicit construction for $AME(2q, q)$, and then showing the reduction to $AME(2q-1, q)$ via measurement.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Absolutely maximally entangled (AME) states represent an extreme form of multipartite entanglement: every reduced system containing at most half of the parties is maximally mixed. These states provide perfect tensors and optimal quantum error-correcting codes, yet their existence is known only in restricted parameter regimes. For every odd prime power $q=p^e\ge3$, we construct a stabilizer $\mathrm{AME}(2q,q)$ state whose normalized one-party projection yields a stabilizer $\mathrm{AME}(2q-1,q)$ state. A closed-form $q\times q$ bordered-circulant matrix $A_q$ over $\mathbb{F}_{q^2}$ generates a Hermitian self-dual maximum distance separable (MDS) code $[2q,q,q+1]_{q^2}$, which lies outside the extended Reed-Solomon classes. In suitable bases, the amplitude tensors define normalized $q$-unitary complex Hadamard matrices of order $q^q$ with $p$th-root phases. Additional constructions yield $\mathrm{AME}(q+3,q)$ states and families at intermediate particle numbers through explicit rescalings of selected submatrices. We also provide nine explicit parent matrices and the corresponding one-party projections.

</details>

**Authors:** Yu Yang, Yuhang Wang, Chunxiao Du, Shikun Zhang, Zheng Qin, Rui Li, Wenxiu Li, Hao Zhang, Zhisong Xiao  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.10984v1>

Theorem 1 says that the added region has empty intersection with the finite-mode Gaussian state set: every Gaussian member of SepF already belongs to one fixed-partition separable set. The picture is schematic and the theorem holds for any finite family of partitions of finitely many finite-mode parties.</sub>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<sub>FIG. 2. Proof roadmap for Theorem 1. (a) Gaussian powers and the spectral selector produce a product vector in a square-root range. Product-range rigidity fixes a separable partition for each power; finiteness of F and trace-norm closure then fix one partition for the target. (b) The local rigidity mechanism: operator domination gives a Gaussian Husimi-Q envelope, holomorphic product contact yields a block-local pure covariance W ⪯V , and correlated displacements reconstruct a state separable across the same partition.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper proves a significant rigidity result for finite-mode Gaussian states, showing that the complex concept of full inseparability is mathematically equivalent to Genuine Multipartite Entanglement (GME). The authors achieve this by employing advanced techniques from functional analysis and quantum information theory, demonstrating that the structure of Gaussian states prevents entanglement from being activated by mixing over multiple potential separable cuts.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep structural constraints on entanglement for Gaussian states, which are highly relevant in quantum optics and continuous-variable quantum information processing, by showing that complex mixed-state entanglement measures collapse to simpler, fixed-partition tests.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether full inseparability (entanglement across every bipartition) and Genuine Multipartite Entanglement (GME) coincide for finite-mode Gaussian states, especially when considering convex decompositions over multiple partitions.

**Main result.** For finite-mode Gaussian states, the authors prove that full inseparability and GME are equivalent, demonstrating that partition mixing adds no new exact Gaussian states.

**Method.** The proof combines spectral selection techniques with holomorphic rigidity arguments, relating local product structures in the state's spectral range to global separability conditions.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on finite-mode Gaussian states of bosonic modes distributed among multiple parties. Separability is analyzed using the trace-norm-closed convex hull of product projectors ($	ext{Sep}_F$).

**Key observables.** Covariance matrices, entanglement depth, fixed-partition separability criteria (LMIs), and the existence of product vectors in the square-root range of the state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finiteness of modes and parties; the exponent $s$ used in Gaussian powers ($ho^s$); the specific structure of the Gaussian state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The target density operator is assumed to be an *exact* finite-mode Gaussian state. The analysis is restricted to finite numbers of parties and modes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates convex partition rigidity for a three-party system, showing Gaussian members of $	ext{Sep}_F$ are constrained to a single fixed-partition set. Figure 2 outlines the proof roadmap involving Gaussian powers and product-range rigidity.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general rigidity theorem for Gaussian states ($	ext{G} \cap 	ext{Sep}_F = 	ext{G} \cap igcup 	ext{Sep}_\pi$), then applies this to the bipartition case to prove the equivalence between full inseparability and GME, relying on spectral selection and holomorphic rigidity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

For general mixed states, entanglement across every bipartition need not imply genuine multipartite entanglement (GME), because a biseparable decomposition may switch the separable cut from term to term. We prove that this convex ambiguity disappears for Gaussian states of finitely many bosonic modes. More generally, for any finite family of partitions, a Gaussian density operator in the trace-norm-closed convex class generated by states separable across those partitions is already separable across one fixed partition in the family. Only the target is Gaussian; a valid decomposition may be continuous and may contain arbitrary non-Gaussian states. Thus full inseparability and GME coincide, Gaussian k-separability and k-producibility reduce to fixed-partition tests, and party-wise tensor powers cannot activate GME from a biseparable Gaussian state. The proof combines a spectral selector with a holomorphic rigidity argument that converts one product vector in the square-root range of a Gaussian state into a block-local covariance certificate. The result shows that partition mixing, a generic mixed-state mechanism, adds no new exact finite-mode Gaussian states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the sample starvation problem in Quantum Architecture Search (QAS) by introducing GenQAS. This method enhances reinforcement learning by using a learned model to generate synthetic, yet physically informed, circuit transitions. The results demonstrate that this generative replay significantly boosts the efficiency and success rate of discovering optimal quantum circuits for chemistry and condensed matter simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, scalable ML technique to overcome fundamental data scarcity issues in quantum algorithm design, which is crucial for simulating complex molecular or material properties.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Reinforcement learning for Quantum Architecture Search (QAS) suffers from sample starvation as the search space grows, making it difficult to find optimal quantum circuits.

**Main result.** The proposed GenQAS framework significantly improves the success probability of finding low-error quantum circuits across various qubit counts, showing up to a 7.0x improvement at 12 qubits.

**Method.** It introduces GenQAS, a tensor network-guided RL framework that augments standard replay buffers by using a learned local transition model to generate synthetic, on-demand circuit transitions.

**Model / system.** The system involves constructing parameterized quantum circuits (PQC) to approximate the ground state of chemical Hamiltonians (e.g., $	ext{BeH}_2$, transverse field Ising model) using qubits.

**Key observables.** Success probability, energy error, and the useful-transition density ($ho$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit count (6 to 15), generation ratio ($G_r$), and the depth of the circuit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the synthetic transitions generated by the learned model remain sufficiently consistent with the real environment to support stable off-policy learning.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how GenQAS converts passive replay into active experience generation, showing the limitations of passive replay versus the enhanced training signal from generative replay.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the sample starvation problem in QAS, then details the GenQAS methodology combining MPS warm-starts with generative replay, and finally presents quantitative results across multiple chemical and Ising model benchmarks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Reinforcement learning (RL) can automate quantum architecture search, but its scalability is limited when useful circuit trajectories become rare in the rapidly expanding search space. Existing replay mechanisms reuse observed transitions; the proposed learned model produces additional predicted one step transitions from real state-action seeds. Here we introduce GenQAS, a tensor network-guided RL framework that combines a fixed matrix product state warm-start with prioritized generative replay. A learned local transition model generates synthetic circuit transitions on demand and mixes them with real experience during Double Deep Q-Network updates. Under a random exploration analysis, near ground state circuits occupy a rapidly shrinking region of the accessible state space. We investigate whether real data anchored synthetic replay can improve the effective training signal in this regime. Across chemical Hamiltonian benchmarks from 6 to 12 qubits, GenQAS improves fixed-budget success probability and identifies compact circuits at competitive energy error. At 12 qubits, it improves final success probability by up to $7.0\times$ over passive replay. On a 15-qubit transverse field Ising model, GenQAS increases success probability from $12\%$ to $21\%$. In a noisy 6-qubit BeH$_2$ transfer experiment, generative replay reduces the steps to chemical accuracy by $92.7\%$. These results show that generative replay can mitigate sample starvation in quantum architecture search and support more resource efficient circuit discovery.

</details>

**Summary.** This work elucidates the mechanism behind record high Curie temperatures in (Fe,Ni)GeTe2 by demonstrating that the magnetism originates from strain-stabilized Fe6GeTe2 nano-precipitates. Using a combination of advanced spectroscopy and DFT, the authors show that site-specific electronic filling dictates the magnetic ground state, providing a roadmap for engineering 2D magnetic metals.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed, atomistic understanding of how structural phase separation and site-selective electronic filling can stabilize magnetic phases at record high temperatures, offering a blueprint for designing novel magnetic materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to unravel the origin of record high Curie temperature ferromagnetism in the metallic van der Waals magnet (Fe,Ni)GeTe2, which is complicated by structural complexity and mixed magnetic contributions.

**Main result.** The high-$T_C$ phase is attributed to a strain-stabilized Fe6GeTe2 nano-precipitate, whose electronic structure features localized moments on interior Fe atoms and itinerant carriers on outer Fe atoms.

**Method.** The research combines advanced experimental techniques like ARPES, XMCD, and STEM with first-principles DFT calculations to map out site-specific electronic and magnetic properties.

**Model / system.** The system is the ternary chalcogenide vdW magnet (Fe,Ni)GeTe2, which exhibits phase separation into distinct Fe-rich and Ni-rich structural domains.

**Key observables.** Curie temperature (up to 478 K), site-specific magnetic moments, spin-polarized itinerant carriers, and lattice parameters distinguishing Fe-rich vs. Ni-rich domains.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ni substitution concentration ($\delta$), temperature (T), and the energy cost associated with homogeneous vs. precipitate formation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** DFT calculations for Ni substitution were performed without full ionic relaxation, and the analysis relies on identifying the magnetic primacy of the Fe-rich domains.

**Figures summary.** Figures show XRD evidence of two distinct structural domains (Fe-rich/Ni-rich), STEM/XAS/XMCD maps confirming magnetic contrast localized in Fe-rich domains, and ARPES/DFT results detailing the electronic structure and site-specific orbital contributions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the high-$T_C$ phenomenon, then using structural characterization (XRD, STEM) to prove phase separation into Fe-rich and Ni-rich domains. Subsequently, electronic structure analysis (ARPES, DFT) is used to pinpoint the electronic mechanism—site-specific filling—that stabilizes the high-$T_C$ precipitate phase.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The discovery of high-temperature ferromagnetism in the metallic van der Waals (vdW) system Fe$_N$GeTe$_2$ has brought two-dimensional (2D) magnets into technologically relevant temperature scales. Specifically at N = 5, dilution of magnetic moments by nickel substitution counterintuitively achieves a record high Curie temperature of 478~K. Unraveling the origin of this nickel-substitution-induced enhancement is complicated by the compound's structural complexity, coexistent itinerant and local magnetic contributions, and mesoscopic compositional domains. Through coordinated structural and electronic characterization, we identify that the high-T$_C$ magnetic phase arises from a strain-stabilized Fe$_6$GeTe$_2$ nano-precipitate. Combining first-principles calculations and spin- and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES), we uncover a site-specific electronic landscape in which interior iron atoms primarily host localized moments while the outer iron atoms neighboring the tellurium layers produce spin-polarized itinerant carriers that cross the vdW gap. The large energy cost associated with homogeneous nickel substitution is found to favor the spontaneous precipitation of the crystallographically and electronically ``clean'' high-T$_C$ phase. Finally, we compare metal-rich vdW magnets with binary magnetic alloys, and discuss the unifying roles of nano-precipitates in stabilizing otherwise unattainable bulk phases. Our work provides mechanistic insights into the record-high T$_C$ ferromagnetism in (Fe,Ni)$_{5+δ}$GeTe$_2$, establishing a rigorous foundation for the atomic engineering of vdW magnetic metals informed by direct electronic signatures.

</details>

**Authors:** Hai-Yang Ma, Yuanchang Li, Hu Xu, Shengbai Zhang, Jin-Feng Jia

(e) HAMs also have alternating spin-split bands, but different from (b), its out-of-plane spin, say ⟨ˆSy⟩∈[−ℏ/2, ℏ/2], is not quantized and the Θτ symmetry requires that the “spin-up” band at k is degenerate with the “spin-down”...</sub>

**Summary.** This theoretical work introduces 'hyperspin' to overcome the limitations of studying non-collinear magnets. It proposes a new class, Hyperspin Altermagnets (HAMs), which are characterized by a conserved 'hyperspin' quantum number rather than the standard spin. This framework provides a deeper understanding of magnetic order beyond simple collinear models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of 'hyperspin' provides a powerful, generalized mathematical tool to classify magnetic order beyond simple spin conservation, which is relevant for understanding complex quantum many-body systems where standard symmetries fail.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the limitations in characterizing non-collinear magnets, which lack well-defined spin quantum numbers, by introducing the concept of 'hyperspin'.

**Main result.** It classifies non-collinear magnets exhibiting alternating spin-split bands in k-space as Hyperspin Altermagnets (HAMs), distinguished from collinear Altermagnets (AMs) by the existence of a conserved 'hyperspin' quantum number.

**Method.** The analysis involves examining the commutation relations between the Hamiltonian and generalized spin operators ($\hat{S}$ vs. $\hat{J}$) to identify conserved quantities that dictate the magnetic behavior.

**Model / system.** The study models magnetic systems, comparing collinear AMs with non-collinear HAMs, often built by merging multiple Antiferromagnetic (AFM) subsystems under $\Theta	au$ symmetry.

**Key observables.** Alternating spin-split bands in k-space, the existence of a conserved hyperspin quantum number ($\hat{J}_y$), and exotic spin current generation effects.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The symmetry $\Theta	au$ is crucial for the emergence of HAMs; the Hamiltonian is analyzed in k-space using matrix diagonalization.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the applicability of a generalized Hamiltonian and relies on symmetry constraints ($\Theta	au$) to define the new conserved quantity.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares AMs and HAMs regarding band structures and spin current generation. Figure 2 illustrates building a HAM by merging two AFM lattices related by $\Theta	au$. Table I summarizes the symmetry requirements for AMs, AFMs, and HAMs.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem with non-collinear magnets, then develops the concept of hyperspin ($\hat{J}$) via commutation relation analysis. It applies this framework to construct and analyze HAMs, deriving the k-space Hamiltonian and demonstrating the resulting collinear spin splitting.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The behavior of spin quantum in k-space is key to identifying altermagnets (AMs) as the third kind of fundamental collinear magnetism. In contrast, non-collinear magnets,though abundant in nature,lack well-defined spin quantum numbers, and the resulting spin textures are often highly complex, which limits their potential for next-generation spintronic applications. Here we propose hyperspin, which lives in a higher-dimensional space, to address these drawbacks. Through analyzing the commutation relations between spin and Hamiltonian for a class of non-collinear magnets, we reveal it is a hyperspin, rather than the usual spin, that commutes with Hamiltonian. Unexpectedly, these non-collinear magnets should also show collinear spin-split bands in k-space like collinear AMs. We therefore classify such non-collinear magnets as hyperspin altermagnets (HAMs), as opposed to the usual collinear AMs. Our theory elucidates the fundamental physics of AMs and HAMs and provides a framework for exploring the wide range of non-collinear magnets that may possess other kinds of conserved quantities.

</details>

<sub>Figure 4: Repetition-code decoder rule in the code-capacity setting. The toric-code decoder (Fig. 2) is a two-dimensional generalization of this one-dimensional decoder. (a) Message growth. A defect sources messages that grow rightward at average speed 1/𝑞, where 𝑞≥2 is the period of a constant-size clock stored at each site. (b) Defect movement. A defect at site 𝑥moves left whenever it sees a message at site 𝑥−1. For simplicity, only the message to the left of the defect is shown. (c) Message erasure. A message at a defect-free site 𝑥is erased whenever there is no message at site 𝑥−1 and no defect at site 𝑥+ 1. Therefore, once all defects produced by a cluster have been eliminated, the...</sub>

As a result, the bounding interval of the cluster’s defects steadily shrinks; in the worst case, it collapses to a single point, and by charge neutrality, this suffices to annihilate every defect in the cluster. Each cluster is eliminated within a time proportional to its initial diameter, so only large clusters can have large temporal...</sub>

**Summary.** This paper presents a breakthrough in quantum error correction by constructing a fully spatially local, fault-tolerant quantum computer using topological codes in two dimensions. The key innovation is a new time-translation-invariant cellular-automaton decoder that eliminates the need for non-local classical computation. This work significantly advances the feasibility of building scalable quantum hardware by ensuring all necessary operations are geometrically local.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, resource-efficient blueprint for building quantum computers whose error correction mechanisms are entirely local and operate on-chip, which is crucial for physical realization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is developing a scalable, fault-tolerant quantum computer based on topological codes that overcomes limitations imposed by classical decoding infrastructure.

**Main result.** The authors constructed the first fully spatially local fault-tolerant quantum computer based on topological codes in fewer than four spatial dimensions, using a novel time-translation-invariant cellular-automaton decoder.

**Method.** The core method involves designing a local cellular-automaton decoder for the surface code, which is integrated with standard fault-tolerance primitives like lattice surgery and state injection.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-dimensional architecture implementing topological codes, specifically focusing on the surface code and general translation-invariant stabilizer codes.

**Key observables.** Logical error rate, threshold noise rate, and the ability to maintain quantum information integrity under noise.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distance ($L$), noise rate ($p$), and the dimensionality ($\le 3$ dimensions).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Scalability requires constant threshold noise rate, geometrically local interactions, bounded-speed classical communication/computation, and finite resource density.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the 2D fault-tolerant quantum computer using local operations and folded surface code patches. Figure 2 details the toric-code decoding mechanism where error clusters move defects to a corner for annihilation.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the construction of the local CA decoder, proves the general local decodability of translation-invariant codes, and finally presents the full local, 2D fault-tolerant quantum computer realization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct the first fully spatially local fault-tolerant quantum computer based on topological codes in fewer than four spatial dimensions. Our construction is a two-dimensional architecture that uses only geometrically local quantum and classical operations, bounded-speed classical communication and computation, and a constant density of quantum and classical resources. The core component is a new time-translation-invariant cellular-automaton decoder for the surface code. This decoder preserves logical information for a time stretched-exponential in the code distance and operates continuously during state injection, stabilizer-state preparation, lattice surgery, and transversal readout. We also prove that every translation-invariant topological Pauli stabilizer code is locally decodable under phenomenological noise.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, low-cost framework that bridges the gap between abstract quantum algorithms and physical execution on surface-code quantum computers. By creating an 'executable workload' representation, it accounts for scheduling, resource supply, and spatial organization simultaneously. The results prove that optimizing the execution structure, rather than just the logical gate count, is key to achieving practical, low-overhead quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial algorithmic tool for quantum hardware engineers and theorists, moving beyond abstract circuit depth to address the practical, resource-constrained scheduling necessary for near-term fault-tolerant devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a low-cost, end-to-end framework to translate high-level quantum algorithms into concrete, physically constrained execution schedules for fault-tolerant quantum computing.

**Main result.** The framework demonstrates that low-cost execution depends critically on computation scheduling and organization, achieving significant reductions in space-time volume (up to 241.5 times) compared to baseline methods.

**Method.** The authors introduce an 'executable workload' intermediate representation that captures logical interactions, parallelism, and time-resolved non-Clifford demand, linking the algorithm to physical resource requirements.

**Model / system.** The work is specifically tailored for surface-code quantum computing, requiring the determination of spatial organization and resource supply alongside the logical circuit execution.

**Key observables.** Physical overhead, routed-latency estimates, space-time volume, logical interaction structure, and time-resolved non-Clifford demand.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The comparison between circuit-specific layouts versus standardized baselines, and the scale of the benchmark circuits (twenty benchmarks across seven families).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the execution cost is determined by the scheduling and organization of operations, not just the aggregate count of logical resources.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the workflow from high-level algorithms through the executable workload representation to the final physical surface-code layout, comparing optimized vs. standardized approaches.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the limitations of current separate optimization stages, then introducing the executable workload as a unifying intermediate representation, and finally applying this framework to benchmark circuits to quantify cost savings.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The execution of useful quantum algorithms on fault-tolerant processors requires more than a mapping from logical gates to encoded operations: the spatial organization, non-Clifford resource supply, and execution schedule must also be determined while keeping physical overhead within practical limits. Although the theoretical hierarchy from logical circuits to fault-tolerant operations is well established, these implementation choices are often specified and optimized separately. Here we develop a low-cost algorithm-to-execution framework for surface-code quantum computing. From hierarchical algorithm descriptions, it constructs dependency-preserving logical schedules and an executable workload capturing logical interactions, operation parallelism, and time-resolved non-Clifford demand, thereby linking logical computation to surface-code organization, resource-state preparation, and fault-tolerant execution in a traceable workflow. We apply the framework to twenty benchmark circuits across seven algorithm families and a hierarchically composed application-scale elliptic-curve discrete-logarithm workload. Physical costs vary substantially even for circuits with similar logical resource counts. Under our direct-rotation calibration, non-Clifford implementation selection reduces space-time volume by up to 241.5 times versus an all-synthesis baseline for the QAOA amplitude-amplification workload. Circuit-specific surface-code layouts reduce routed-latency estimates for all twenty benchmarks; thirteen also reduce space-time volume because communication savings outweigh added spatial overhead. These results show that low-cost fault-tolerant execution depends on computation scheduling and organization, not aggregate logical resource counts alone.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes the theoretical lower bounds on the T-gate count needed to encode and simulate complex fermionic and spin Hamiltonians using block encodings in a Clifford+T quantum circuit model. By developing advanced geometric and combinatorial arguments, the authors provide tight scaling laws for the required quantum resources. This research is crucial for understanding the computational cost limitations of simulating quantum matter on near-term quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental complexity bounds for simulating quantum many-body systems using near-term quantum hardware models (Clifford+T), directly impacting the feasibility assessment of quantum simulation algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the minimum T-gate count required to construct block encodings of structured fermionic and spin Hamiltonians within a unitary Clifford+T quantum circuit model.

**Main result.** For general fermionic Hamiltonians, the worst-case T-count scales as $\Theta(n^2 \log(n^4/\epsilon))$, while for Kitaev honeycomb spin Hamiltonians, the scaling is $\Theta(n + \log(1/\epsilon))$.

**Method.** The authors employ volume-covering arguments and circuit counting techniques, alongside an ancilla-compression theorem, to establish rigorous lower bounds on the required T-gate resources.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on simulating fermionic and spin Hamiltonians (like the Kitaev model) using block encodings within a unitary Clifford+T circuit model, avoiding mid-circuit measurements.

**Key observables.** T-count (non-Clifford gate cost), block-encoding error ($\epsilon$), and the system size ($n$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The scaling depends critically on the system size $n$ and the desired precision $\epsilon$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to use clean ancillas and that the simulation is performed in the unitary Clifford+T model without classical feed-forward.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops theoretical tools, including an ancilla-compression theorem, to derive lower bounds for the T-count complexity of encoding Hamiltonians, applying these bounds separately to fermionic and spin systems, and concluding with an application to QSVT.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We determine the non-Clifford $T$-gate cost of constructing block encodings of structured fermionic and spin Hamiltonians in a unitary Clifford$+T$ model, when arbitrarily many clean ancillas and unrestricted block-encoding subnormalization are allowed, but without mid-circuit measurements or classical feed-forward. Our main technical tool is an ancilla-compression theorem: any block encoding of an $n$-qubit operator with $a$ clean ancillas and at most $s$ $T$ gates can be compressed to use at most $\min\{a,n+2s\}$ ancillas, without increasing the absolute error or $T$-count. For general second-quantized Hamiltonians with bounded one- and two-body coefficients, at operator-norm block-encoding error $ε$, a volume-covering argument combined with circuit counting gives the worst-case lower bound $Ω(n^2\sqrt{\log(n^4/ε)})$, matching the existing upper bound at fixed precision. For the bond-dependent Kitaev honeycomb family on $n$ spins, we obtain independent lower bounds $Ω(n)$ from stabilizer nullity and $Ω(\log(1/ε))$ from one-qubit state preparation, established using different Hamiltonian instances. Together with an explicit LCU construction, they give the tight worst-case scaling $Θ(n+\log(1/ε))$. As an application, we evaluate the $T$-count of a Hamiltonian simulation circuit based on quantum singular value transformation, with each block-encoding query compiled separately. When phase synthesis and controlled queries add at most constant-factor overhead, the simulation $T$-count scales as the query count times the optimal $T$-count per query.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes rigorous separations within the Fourier hierarchy, a model quantifying quantum computation depth by Hadamard layers. It proves that increasing the number of layers strictly increases computational power relative to an oracle, distinguishing between the capabilities of phase and standard oracle queries. This advances the theoretical understanding of quantum query complexity.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental complexity results for quantum computation models that are highly relevant to understanding the limits of quantum algorithms beyond standard gate models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to prove oracle separations within the Fourier hierarchy ($	ext{FH}_k$), a model measuring quantum computation by the number of Hadamard layers used.

**Main result.** It proves that for every constant $k \ge 2$, $	ext{FH}_k \subsetneq 	ext{FH}_{k+1}$ relative to an oracle, establishing that increasing the number of Hadamard layers strictly increases computational power.

**Method.** The separation is achieved by constructing a hard problem based on Forrelation, whose query complexity is analyzed differently depending on whether the oracle is phase-based or standard.

**Model / system.** The model is quantum computation restricted by the Fourier hierarchy ($	ext{FH}_k$), where interference is limited to Hadamard layers. Two query types are analyzed: the phase oracle (sign multiplication) and the standard oracle (register write).

**Key observables.** The computational power difference between $	ext{FH}_k$ and $	ext{FH}_{k+1}$, measured by the query complexity required to solve the separating Forrelation problem.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Fourier depth $k$ (number of Hadamard layers), and the Forrelation order $K$ used in the separating problem.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on structural properties linking the number of Hadamard layers to the circuit's ability to query the oracle adaptively.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically proves separations for both phase and standard oracle models, comparing their relative strengths and establishing the strict inclusion $	ext{FH}_k \subsetneq 	ext{FH}_{k+1}$ for fixed $k$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Fourier hierarchy $\mathrm{FH}_0\subseteq\mathrm{FH}_1\subseteq\mathrm{FH}_2\subseteq\cdots$, introduced by Shi (TCS 2005), measures a quantum computation by the number of Hadamard layers it uses. Between two layers the circuit may permute basis states and attach phases, but it may not create superposition; the layers are its only source of interference. The first level is exactly $\mathrm{BPP}$, while the second already solves Simon's problem and, through phase estimation, factors integers. Shi conjectured that every additional layer strictly increases computational power, and asked, as a first step, for oracle separations between consecutive levels. To our knowledge, the question was open at every level $k\ge2$.   We prove that for every constant $k\ge2$ there is an oracle relative to which $\mathrm{FH}_k\subsetneq\mathrm{FH}_{k+1}$. The separating problem is built from Forrelation (Aaronson and Ambainis, STOC 2015): the level above solves it with a constant number of queries, whereas at level $k$ it stays hard even for circuits making exponentially many queries. This holds for both of the usual ways of giving a circuit access to an oracle, the phase oracle and the standard oracle, which writes its answer into a register. The two are not interchangeable: relative to an oracle, the standard oracle is strictly more powerful at the same number of layers. We also separate the union of all the levels from $\mathrm{BQP}$ relative to an oracle.   The lower bounds rest on a structural property of the hierarchy: the number of Hadamard layers limits how adaptively a circuit can query its oracle. With a phase oracle, a circuit with $k$ layers is reproduced exactly by an algorithm making only $k-1$ rounds of parallel queries, which brings known lower bounds for such algorithms to bear. The standard oracle lets a circuit branch on earlier answers, and that case needs a separate argument.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper constructs specific Positive-Partial-Transpose (PPT) quantum states on $\mathbb{C}^m \otimes \mathbb{C}^n$ and derives a rigorous lower bound on their Schmidt number. The result shows that this bound approaches $\min(m, n)$, dramatically improving previous estimates and demonstrating that PPT states can possess high levels of entanglement.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a significant theoretical advance in understanding the entanglement structure of quantum states that are 'weakly entangled' (PPT), which is crucial for understanding entanglement witnesses and separability criteria in quantum information theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to determine the maximum Schmidt number (MPPT) of a Positive-Partial-Transpose (PPT) state acting on a bipartite system $\mathbb{C}^m \otimes \mathbb{C}^n$.

**Main result.** The authors constructed a PPT state whose Schmidt number achieves a lower bound asymptotically approaching $\min(m, n)$, significantly improving previous bounds that were only $O(n)$.

**Method.** The construction involves perturbing a specially designed state $\sigma_{m,n}$ using orthogonal projections and relies on advanced algebraic techniques (like those involving the Lindemann–Weierstrass theorem) to prove the state's properties.

**Model / system.** The study concerns bipartite quantum states $ho$ in the Hilbert space $\mathbb{C}^m \otimes \mathbb{C}^n$. The key constraint is that the state must satisfy the PPT condition ($ho^\Gamma \ge 0$).

**Key observables.** Schmidt Number ($	ext{SN}(ho)$) and the Positive Partial Transpose (PPT) property.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The local dimensions $m$ and $n$; the asymptotic behavior as $m, n 	o \infty$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction is based on perturbing a state $\sigma_{m,n}$ and requires proving the algebraic independence of certain complex numbers $\{g_{i,j}\}$ to establish the lower bound.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the context by defining the problem of bounding $	ext{SN}(ho)$ for PPT states. It then develops the mathematical machinery, including proving algebraic independence (Lemma 5) and using geometric arguments on determinantal varieties (Lemma 6). Finally, it applies these tools to construct the state and derive the main lower bound on the Schmidt number (Theorem 7).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct PPT states on $\mathbb{C}^m \otimes\mathbb{C}^n$ that have Schmidt number asymptotically approaching the smaller local dimension. More specifically, we construct a PPT state with Schmidt number at least $$ \left\lceil \frac{m + n - ((m - n)^2 + 4(m + n - 1))^{1/2}}{2} \right\rceil. $$ In the case of equal local dimensions ($m = n$), this becomes $n - \lfloor(2n - 1)^{1/2}\rfloor$, far exceeding the previous constructions which achieved $n/2 + O(1)$. In the case of unequal local dimensions, our result shows that there exists a PPT state on $\mathbb{C}^n \otimes \mathbb{C}^{3n-4}$ with Schmidt number at least $n-1$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proves a major complexity collapse showing that several advanced quantum proof systems, including PureSuperQMA and BellPureSymQMA, are all equivalent in computational power to QMA. This equivalence implies that checking for exact local consistency in pure quantum states is as hard as the general QMA-complete problems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The result provides a deep connection between fundamental problems in quantum many-body physics (like N-representability) and the established complexity class QMA, suggesting that these physical constraints are computationally hard.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the computational complexity of pure-state consistency problems, which are related to determining if a set of local quantum states can arise from a single global state.

**Main result.** The authors prove a significant complexity collapse: QMA = PureSuperQMA = PureSuperQMA(exp) = BellPureSymQMA(poly), establishing that exact k-local pure-state consistency is QMA-complete.

**Method.** The proof relies on applying a dimension-free stability bound for symmetric tensor states to simulate the complex verification procedures of PureSuperQMA(exp) and BellPureSymQMA(poly) into the standard QMA framework.

**Model / system.** The models are quantum proof systems ($	ext{PureSuperQMA}$, $	ext{BellPureSymQMA}$) used to characterize the difficulty of quantum marginal problems (local-to-global consistency checks) for pure states.

**Key observables.** The extremal verification value, the gap size between YES/NO instance probabilities, and the total variation distance between probability distributions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of local measurements ($k$), the witness size (polynomial vs. exponential), and the violation margin ($\epsilon$) and fraction ($\delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the validity of dimension-free stability bounds for symmetric tensor states and relies on techniques from quantum information theory and harmonic analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical motivation (CLDM/quantum marginal problem), defines the complexity classes ($	ext{PureSuperQMA}$, etc.), and then proceeds through technical sections detailing the dimension-free stability bound, followed by the explicit simulations proving the stated complexity equalities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Pure-state consistency problems naturally lead to quantum proof systems in which a single pure witness must satisfy many acceptance constraints. The corresponding class $\mathsf{PureSuperQMA}$ was previously known to lie between $\mathsf{QMA}$ and $\mathsf{QMA}(2)$, and Kamminga and Rudolph (ITCS'26) conjectured that both containments are strict. In this paper, we prove the following surprising complexity collapses $$   \mathsf{QMA} = \mathsf{PureSuperQMA} = \mathsf{PureSuperQMA}(\text{exp}) = \mathsf{BellPureSymQMA}(\text{poly}) $$ Here $\mathsf{PureSuperQMA}(\text{exp})$ allows exponentially many checks which are uniformly indexed and efficiently generated, while requiring an inverse-polynomial violation margin and an inverse-polynomial fraction of violated checks for the NO cases. $\mathsf{BellPureSymQMA}(\text{poly})$ is a related model that requires the prover to give the verifier polynomially many copies of a pure state, which the verifier measures separately with logarithmic output length for each local measurement, before processing the outcomes jointly.   The main technical ingredient is a dimension-free stability bound for symmetric tensor states. Our simulations use polynomially many witness registers and combine a random-pair SWAP test with a permutation-invariant lift of the original verification procedure. The key step is to show that, on the symmetric subspace, the extremal verification value is close to that of some tensor-power witness with dimension-independent error. Applying this argument to the two verification models yields both simulations. As a consequence, exact $k$-local pure-state consistency is $\mathsf{QMA}$-complete for every fixed $k\ge2$, and so are the corresponding exact bosonic and fermionic pure $N$-representability problems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves an exponential strong converse for quantum capacity, showing that exceeding the channel's capacity leads to an exponentially decaying fidelity for any quantum code. The proof is highly technical, relying on a 'fully quantum blowing-up lemma' and advanced polynomial approximation techniques to control error accumulation over many channel uses.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly theoretical, the techniques involving projector approximation and tensor norms are mathematically sophisticated and could inspire new ways to bound error propagation in complex quantum systems or quantum network protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to prove an exponential strong converse for quantum communication capacity through any finite-dimensional memoryless quantum channel.

**Main result.** It establishes that if the transmission rate exceeds the quantum capacity by any positive gap, the entanglement-transmission fidelity of the code must decay exponentially with the number of channel uses.

**Method.** The proof combines a 'fully quantum blowing-up lemma' (to convert low-fidelity codes to high-fidelity ones) with a low-degree polynomial construction to approximate the tensor power of the channel's projection operator.

**Model / system.** The model is a point-to-point quantum channel N used multiple times (memoryless channel). Communication is analyzed using entanglement-transmission codes.

**Key observables.** Entanglement-transmission fidelity ($F_n$), communication rate (qubits per channel use), and the quantum capacity $Q(N)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The rate gap ($\gamma > 0$) and the exponential decay constant ($\alpha_\gamma > 0$) are central parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof relies on the ability to approximate the tensor power of the projection operator using low-degree polynomials while controlling the projective tensor norm.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the entanglement transmission process through the channel N, using the Stinespring isometry.

**Paper structure.** The structure builds from defining the strong converse problem, introducing the fully quantum blowing-up lemma, and then using polynomial approximation techniques to control the error terms necessary for the final exponential decay bound.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove an exponential strong converse for quantum communication through every finite-dimensional memoryless channel: at any fixed rate above the quantum capacity, the entanglement-transmission fidelity of every code decays exponentially with the number of channel uses. The proof has two main ingredients. First, a fully quantum blowing-up lemma converts a low-fidelity code into a high-fidelity code, with a loss in the number of transmitted qubits controlled by the projective tensor norm of the orthogonal projection on the image of the Stinespring dilation of the channel across the receiver--environment bipartition. Second, a low-degree polynomial construction approximates the tensor power of this projector with exponential accuracy while controlling its projective norm, providing the approximation needed for the blowing-up argument.

</details>
