**Summary.** This theoretical paper disproves the generalized semi-Clifford conjecture concerning quantum gates in the Clifford hierarchy. The authors construct a specific five-qubit gate that is mathematically proven to be in the fifth level of the hierarchy but lacks the required algebraic structure. This finding significantly refines our understanding of the limitations and boundaries of fault-tolerant quantum computation models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep structural insights into the limitations of gate sets used in quantum error correction and computation, revealing that the assumed algebraic constraints (GSC) are too restrictive for the full complexity of the Clifford hierarchy.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to disprove the generalized semi-Clifford conjecture, which posits that all gates in the Clifford hierarchy must have a specific algebraic structure.

**Main result.** The authors construct a concrete five-qubit gate that belongs to the fifth level of the Clifford hierarchy but does not possess the generalized semi-Clifford property. Furthermore, they show the hierarchy is not closed under inverses.

**Method.** The proof uses advanced algebraic techniques involving symplectic linear algebra, Pauli groups, and analyzing the structure of commutators of quantum gates to characterize membership in the hierarchy and the GSC property.

**Model / system.** The study concerns quantum gates acting on $n$ qubits, specifically focusing on the structure and properties of the Clifford hierarchy ($\mathcal{C}_k(n)$) within the unitary group $U(\mathcal{H}_n)$.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the gate structure itself, the membership level in the Clifford hierarchy ($\mathcal{C}_k$), and the existence of a common invariant Lagrangian for the associated Pauli algebras.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits ($n=5$ for the counterexample) and the level of the hierarchy ($k=5$) are crucial parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis proceeds by developing equivalent characterizations for hierarchy membership and the GSC property, building upon established results for lower-level gates.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the five-qubit counterexample circuit, which is constructed using controlled-Clifford gates.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the necessary algebraic machinery to characterize the Clifford hierarchy and the GSC property, then constructs a specific five-qubit unitary gate that satisfies the hierarchy condition but violates the GSC condition, concluding with a proof that the gate's inverse also falls outside the hierarchy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Clifford hierarchy is a nested sequence of sets of quantum gates that can be fault-tolerantly performed using gate teleportation within standard quantum error correction schemes. The importance of these gates has motivated numerous studies of their structure. Zeng-Chen-Chuang conjectured in 2007 that all hierarchy gates are generalised semi-Clifford, i.e. take the form $C_1 ΠD C_2$ for Clifford gates $C_1, C_2$, a permutation gate $Π$, and a diagonal gate $D$; Beigi-Shor proved in 2008 that this holds for all third-level gates.   We construct a five-qubit gate that is in the fifth level of the Clifford hierarchy but is not generalised semi-Clifford. Rather than simply present and verify our counterexample to the generalised semi-Clifford conjecture, we show how its form can be deduced. Our counterexample also demonstrates that the Clifford hierarchy is not closed under inverses.

</details>

### [The Quantum Overlap Gap Property and Algorithmic Hardness for the Quantum Hypergraph Max-Cut Problem](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10838v1)

**Authors:** Mikhail Mints, Eric R. Anschuetz  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.10838v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.10838_figures/2609.10838_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>

<img src="2609.10838_figures/2609.10838_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops theoretical hardness results for the Quantum Hypergraph Max-Cut problem by applying the Quantum Overlap Gap Property (QOGP). It proves that achieving near-optimal solutions requires quantum algorithms to overcome significant computational barriers, establishing concrete depth lower bounds for popular quantum approaches.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides rigorous theoretical lower bounds on the performance of quantum algorithms for fundamental optimization problems, which is crucial for understanding the limits of quantum computation in physics simulations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes the average-case hardness of approximation for the Quantum Hypergraph Max-Cut problem.

**Main result.** It establishes both weak and strong hardness results, showing that for certain classes of quantum algorithms, approximating the optimal solution on $k$-uniform hypergraphs is computationally hard.

**Method.** The analysis utilizes the theoretical framework of the Quantum Overlap Gap Property (QOGP) and employs techniques from Ramsey theory for the strong hardness result.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the Quantum Hypergraph Max-Cut problem, which is a generalization of the Quantum Max-Cut problem used to model ground state problems in quantum many-body systems.

**Key observables.** Approximation ratio ($\gamma$), Lipschitz constants ($L$), and the energy expectation value $V(\mathbf{X}, s)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The hypergraph uniformity $k$, and the order of the quantum Wasserstein distance (order 2 and order $\infty$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on adapting the classical Overlap Gap Property (OGP) to the quantum setting (QOGP) and sometimes assumes Gaussian random variables for coefficients.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the Overlap Gap Property (OGP), showing near-optimal solutions separating into disconnected clusters.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews the classical OGP, develops the QOGP framework, presents the weak hardness result (Theorem 1.1) based on resampling, and establishes the strong hardness result (Theorem 1.2) using Ramsey theory.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we analyze the average-case hardness of approximation for the Quantum Hypergraph Max-Cut problem using the theoretical framework of the Quantum Overlap Gap Property (QOGP). We establish two main results. Our first result applies to a wide class of stable quantum algorithms, satisfying a Lipschitz property with respect to the quantum Wasserstein distance of order $2$. We show a weak hardness result, demonstrating that for any Lipschitz constant $L$, there is some $k$ such that $L$-stable algorithms cannot approximate the optimal solution to Quantum Hypergraph Max-Cut on $k$-uniform hypergraphs in the average case. Additionally, we establish a strong hardness result where $k$ is independent of $L$, but only for a more restricted class of local quantum algorithms defined using the quantum Wasserstein distance of order $\infty$. We apply these results to establish concrete depth lower bounds for popular quantum algorithms for preparing near-optimal states for this problem.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes how tailored surface codes, specifically comparing ZX and ZY variants, perform under realistic circuit-level noise, including bias and crosstalk. The study finds that while the ZY code theoretically captures more error information, current matching decoders fail to utilize this correlation. Ultimately, the paper stresses the need for advanced decoders capable of handling complex, correlated syndrome data.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of how specific stabilizer structures (ZY vs ZX) interact with directional noise biases and crosstalk provides concrete, actionable insights for designing more robust quantum error correction protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the threshold behavior of tailored surface codes (ZX and ZY) when subjected to realistic circuit-level biased and crosstalk noise.

**Main result.** The ZY code shows a Y-memory threshold nearly constant across bias, while the ZX code shows an increasing X-memory threshold with bias; furthermore, the analysis highlights that standard decoders cannot fully utilize the correlated syndrome information available in the ZY structure.

**Method.** The authors simulate performance using the minimum-weight perfect matching (MWPM) decoder, comparing results across varying bias parameters and incorporating crosstalk noise.

**Model / system.** The system uses surface codes, comparing the standard ZX code against a constructed ZY code (replacing X-stabilizers with Y-stabilizers). Noise models include Pauli-X biased noise and additional gate-based XX crosstalk noise, moving beyond idealized code-capacity noise.

**Key observables.** Memory thresholds ($p_{X}^{th}, p_{Z}^{th}, p_{Y}^{th}$) for X, Y, and Z logical memories, and the logical error rate ($p_L$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bias parameter ($\eta$), crosstalk probability ($p_x$), and code distance ($d$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation is that the MWPM decoder cannot process the correlated syndrome information generated by the ZY code, which forms a hypergraph structure.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the 2D structure of the unrotated surface code with Z and X stabilizers, while Figure 2 shows the stabilizer measurement circuits for Z, X, and Y stabilizers.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the problem of circuit-level noise, then comparing the threshold behavior of ZX and ZY codes under biased noise, analyzing the effect of crosstalk, and finally discussing the limitations of current matching decoders for the complex syndrome structure of the ZY code.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Studying the threshold behavior of surface codes under biased noise models is an active area of research. Previous work Tuckett et al. (2018), using an optimal tensor-network decoder, demonstrated that replacing $Z$-type stabilizers with $Y$-type stabilizers significantly improves the surface code threshold under code-capacity level dephasing noise. In this work, we construct and study a $ZY$ surface code by replacing the $X$-type stabilizers with $Y$-type stabilizers. We compare it with the standard $ZX$ surface code under circuit-level Pauli-$X$ biased noise, with and without an additional gate-based $XX$ crosstalk noise. We find that for the $ZX$ surface code, the $X$-memory threshold increases monotonically with bias while the $Z$-memory threshold decreases and saturates. For the $ZY$ surface code, the $Y$-memory threshold is nearly constant across all bias values. The $Z$-memory thresholds of the $ZX$ and $ZY$ codes are consistent within the uncertainty. Adding $XX$ crosstalk reduces the $Z$-memory threshold beyond the fitting uncertainty while leaving the $X$ memory threshold largely unaffected. The choice of CNOT ordering redistributes threshold performance between the two logical memories. Our work extends prior observations from code-capacity level noise to circuit-level noise. It also indicates the need for decoders capable of jointly reasoning over correlated syndrome information so that tailored stabilizer structures could be fully utilized for quantum error correction.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.10808v1>

**Summary.** This paper derives fundamental, optimal time-space lower bounds for quantum algorithms solving collision finding and Element Distinctness, restricted to label-symmetric algorithms. It achieves this by developing a space-sensitive compressed oracle technique combined with representation theory. The results clarify the inherent resource trade-offs for these core problems in quantum complexity theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly theoretical, establishing fundamental lower bounds on computational resources (time vs. memory) is crucial for understanding the limits of quantum computation, which underpins many advanced quantum simulation and control techniques.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Determining the optimal time-space tradeoff for quantum algorithms solving problems like collision finding and Element Distinctness, specifically under the constraint of label symmetry.

**Main result.** The paper establishes lower bounds $T = \Omega(N^{1/3})$ and $T^2S = \Omega(N \log N)$ for collision finding, and $T = \Omega(n^{2/3})$ and $T^2S = \Omega(n^2 \log n)$ for Element Distinctness, showing these bounds are optimal within the label-symmetric class.

**Method.** The proof develops a space-sensitive version of the compressed oracle technique, combining it with label symmetry and representation theory to bound the information retained by limited quantum memory.

**Model / system.** The analysis is conducted within the quantum query-complexity model, focusing on quantum algorithms applied to functions $f:[M]	o [N]$ or the decision problem of injectivity.

**Key observables.** Query complexity ($T$), Qubit space complexity ($S$), and the size of the domain/codomain ($N$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The bounds depend critically on the structure of the problem (collision finding vs. Element Distinctness) and the assumption of label symmetry.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are proven only for the class of label-symmetric algorithms; the general case without this symmetry remains an open problem.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by first analyzing the required information storage using a space-sensitive compressed oracle technique, then applying this to derive the specific time-space tradeoffs for collision finding and Element Distinctness, concluding with the optimality proof.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

How much memory is needed to retain the quantum speedup for collision finding? For a uniformly random function $f:[N]\to [N]$, the BHT algorithm finds a collision using $O(N^{1/3})$ queries and a quantumly accessible classical table containing $O(N^{1/3})$ input-output pairs, whereas a logarithmic-space Grover search uses $O(\sqrt N)$ queries. Determining the optimal query-space tradeoff between these extremes remains a major open problem.   We resolve this equation within the class of label-symmetric algorithms, which treat the function $f$'s output labels as interchangeable. We prove that such algorithm that makes $T$ queries, uses $S$ qubits, and finds a collision in a uniformly random function $f:[M]\to [N]$ with constant probability satisfies $$T=Ω(N^{1/3}) \qquad\text{and}\qquad T^2S=Ω(N\log N).$$ For the setting where $M=N$, these bounds are matched by a space-efficient implementation of the BHT algorithm. As a consequence of our tradeoff, any label-symmetric algorithm for the search version of Element Distinctness on $f: [n] \to [n^2]$ must satisfy $$T=Ω(n^{2/3}) \qquad\text{and}\qquad T^2S=Ω(n^2\log n),$$ matching Ambainis's quantum walk. Thus, both tradeoffs are optimal within the class of label-symmetric algorithms.   To prove these results, we develop a space-sensitive version of the compressed oracle technique. The compressed oracle records the information learned by the algorithm in an evolving superposition of databases. Using label symmetry and representation theory, we show that an algorithm using $S$ qubits can effectively retain information about only $O(S/\log N)$ collision-free database entries. Substituting this estimate into the compressed oracle technique yields the stated tradeoffs.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work explores how coupling a topological superconductor (modeled by a Kitaev chain) to a dissipative bath can enable nonlocal, phase-coherent transport via Majorana bound states. The key finding is that dissipation can substitute for the need for Coulomb blockade to mediate this transport. This establishes a mechanism for controlling quantum information transfer in topological quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems dynamics by showing how environmental coupling (dissipation) can induce and control non-trivial quantum phenomena like nonlocal transport, which is highly relevant for quantum computing architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether dissipation can replace the necessity of Coulomb blockade to achieve nonlocal, phase-coherent transport mediated by Majorana bound states (MBS) in a topological superconductor.

**Main result.** The authors show that dissipation facilitates nonlocal transport, reaching a regime where nonlocal conductance contributions match local ones, confirming dissipation as a tool to control quantum teleportation in Majorana systems.

**Method.** The analysis involves calculating local and nonlocal conductance contributions using extensions of the Meir-Wingreen formula for dissipative systems, coupled with analyzing the reduced density matrix of the low-energy subspace.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a Kitaev chain, representing a topological superconductor, which is coupled to an external Markovian bath. The dynamics are governed by the Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** Nonlocal and local conductance contributions ($G_{EC}^j, G_{CAR}^j, G_{LAR}^j, G_B^j$), average fermion parity ($ho_{red}$), and Aharonov-Bohm oscillations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Zero bias voltage ($V_j=0$), orthogonal coupling vectors ($\mathbf{b}_1 \perp \mathbf{b}_N$), and the coupling strength to the bath.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes weak coupling to the bath and involves projecting the system dynamics onto the Majorana bound state subspace.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup, the effect of dissipation on conductance contributions at zero bias, and the dependence of conductance on magnetic flux to confirm Aharonov-Bohm oscillations.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory using Majorana representations and Green's functions, calculates the various conductance components, identifies the dissipative regime where nonlocal transport is maximized, and finally proposes a protocol for Majorana qubit readout.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonlocal phase-coherent transport via Majorana bound states, so-called teleportation, requires Coulomb blockade to suppress local Andreev reflection. We show that dissipation can replace the need for Coulomb blockade. We study a Kitaev chain coupled to a Markovian bath and identify a regime where the magnitude of nonlocal conductance contribution reach the local one. In this regime, the lowest-energy subspace is maximally mixed, reminiscent of a directly-grounded nontrivial topological superconductor. Phase coherence is confirmed by Aharonov-Bohm oscillations. Our results establish dissipation as a tool for controlling quantum teleportation in Majorana systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to model the AC Josephson effect by treating the system's dynamics through the dissipative quantum mechanics of Andreev bound states. It successfully calculates the non-equilibrium current-phase relation, highlighting a specific dissipative current component that becomes important near the superconducting gap edge. The methodology involves advanced Green's function techniques to handle the complex, time-dependent quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed, non-equilibrium quantum description of current transport in superconducting junctions, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems where dissipation and tunneling dynamics are crucial for understanding macroscopic quantum phenomena.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a microscopic scheme to describe the AC Josephson effect in superconducting junctions by analyzing the dissipative quantum dynamics of subgap Andreev bound states.

**Main result.** It identifies a non-trivial, phase-dependent dissipative contribution to the current, $I_{diss}[\chi(t)]$, which is significant near the superconducting gap edge.

**Method.** The analysis uses the quasiclassical Eilenberger-Keldysh formalism, supplemented by advanced techniques to calculate the inverse operator of the system's Green's function, especially near singular phase points.

**Model / system.** The system is a single-channel symmetric non-magnetic superconducting junction biased by a time-dependent voltage. The dynamics are governed by Andreev bound states whose energies depend on the Josephson phase $\chi$.

**Key observables.** Current-Phase Relation (CPR), $I-V$ curve, and the dissipative current contribution $I_{diss}[\chi(t)]$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superconducting gap $|\Delta|$, junction transmission $D$, and the Josephson phase $\chi(t)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations valid in the low bias regime ($eV \ll |\Delta|$), and involves complex mathematical procedures to handle singularities near $\chi = 2\pi n$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the energy of Andreev bound states versus phase $\chi$, showing adiabatic dynamics, Landau-Zener tunneling, and the dissipative contribution near the gap edge.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical model and the total current components ($I_J + I_{diss}$) to deriving local, time-dependent effective Hamiltonians. It then tackles the complex mathematical machinery of calculating the inverse operator and finally derives the explicit, regularized forms for the dissipative current contribution by analyzing contributions from different time intervals.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a microscopic scheme that allows to describe ac Josephson effect in superconducting junctions in terms of dissipative quantum dynamics of subgap Andreev bound states. This approach is particularly useful for highly transparent junctions at subgap bias voltages in which case a non-trivial combination of Landau-Zener tunneling between Andreev levels and their instability due to quasiparticle escape into continuum may play an important role. In the low bias regime, we evaluate the non-equilibrium current-phase relation of superconducting junctions at arbitrary transmissions and identify a sub-Ohmic phase-dependent dissipative contribution to the current controlled by quasiparticle dynamics near the superconducting gap edge.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses ultrasonic attenuation measurements on the superconductor CsV3Sb5 to study how its superconducting order parameter relaxes after being perturbed. By fitting the data to the Landau-Khalatnikov model, the authors extracted a characteristic scattering time of 25 ps. This result suggests that the relaxation is governed by inelastic scattering, implying that simple elastic scattering processes are ineffective at damping the superconducting condensate.

**Why it may be interesting.** The analysis of non-equilibrium dynamics using the LK model provides a concrete example of how order parameter relaxation can be probed experimentally, linking macroscopic measurements (ultrasound) to microscopic scattering physics, which is relevant to open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the superconducting order parameter relaxes back to equilibrium after being driven out of equilibrium by a time-varying strain field.

**Main result.** The authors discovered an ultrasonic attenuation peak in CsV3Sb5, successfully modeled by order-parameter relaxation, yielding a scattering time (25 ps) inconsistent with simple elastic scattering mechanisms.

**Method.** The primary method involves using pulse-echo ultrasound to measure the ultrasonic attenuation ($\alpha$) across the superconducting transition temperature.

**Model / system.** The system is the kagome superconductor CsV3Sb5. The dynamics are analyzed using the Landau-Khalatnikov (LK) model, which describes attenuation when the relaxation timescale ($	au_\Delta$) matches the inverse drive frequency ($\omega$).

**Key observables.** Ultrasonic attenuation ($\alpha$) as a function of temperature and ultrasonic frequency ($\omega$); the relaxation time $	au_N$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The extracted microscopic scattering time $	au_N = 25 	ext{ ps}$; the comparison between $	au_N$ and elastic/inelastic scattering times.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the applicability of the Landau-Khalatnikov model, requiring $	au_\Delta$ to be comparable to $1/\omega$.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the crystal structure and strain effects (Fig. 1); the fractional change in the longitudinal modulus (Fig. 2); and the key comparison of sound attenuation, showing a frequency-dependent peak in CsV3Sb5 versus monotonic behavior in RbV3Sb5 (Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework using the LK model, applies it to experimental ultrasonic attenuation data on CsV3Sb5, extracts the characteristic scattering time $	au_N$, and interprets this time by comparing it to other known scattering mechanisms (resistivity vs. thermal transport).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The application of a time-varying strain field drives a superconducting order parameter out of equilibrium. How the order parameter relaxes back to equilibrium depends both on the structure of the superconducting gap and on the nature of quasiparticle scattering. We report the discovery of an ultrasonic attenuation peak inside the superconducting state of the kagome superconductor CsV$_3$Sb$_5$. This peak is the natural consequence of the order parameter relaxation time matching the ultrasonic drive frequency near $T_{\rm c}$. From the measured frequency dependence of the peak, we extract a microscopic scattering time of $τ_N = 25$ ps. This timescale is two orders of magnitude longer than the elastic scattering time as determined by resistivity measurements, but is comparable to the inelastic scattering time determined by thermal transport. Within the conventional framework of order-parameter relaxation, this implies that elastic scattering is ineffective at relaxing the superconducting condensate, consistent with a sign-preserving $s$-wave state obeying Anderson's theorem.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work demonstrates how the d-wave altermagnetic order in a topological insulator creates distinct electronic properties on different crystal facets. By coupling this material into a Josephson junction, the authors show that tuning the superconducting phase bias can induce a superconducting mass inversion at the crystal hinges, thereby realizing and spatially controlling Majorana zero modes.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to engineer topological superconductivity and control MZMs using geometric/structural properties (facets) and external biases ($\phi$) is highly relevant for realizing fault-tolerant quantum computing platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to exploit facet-dependent Andreev phase shifts to control topological superconductivity in a Josephson junction formed by an altermagnetic topological insulator.

**Main result.** The study establishes a mechanism where the altermagnetic order induces distinct phase responses on different crystal facets, leading to a superconducting mass inversion at the facet hinges that hosts Majorana zero modes (MZMs).

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving low-energy effective Hamiltonians by projecting the full lattice model onto the surface-state subspace and analyzing the resulting Andreev bound-state quantization conditions.

**Model / system.** The system is a Josephson junction (SC/AMTI/SC) where the AMTI is modeled using a Hamiltonian incorporating d-wave altermagnetic order. The physics is analyzed by examining the Andreev spectra and the resulting superconducting gap structure.

**Key observables.** Facet-dependent gap closings in Andreev spectra, spatial localization of MZMs, and the sign change of the effective superconducting mass across crystal facets.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superconducting phase bias ($\phi$), altermagnetic coupling strength ($t_J$), and the chemical potential ($\mu$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on projecting the full Hamiltonian onto the low-energy surface-state subspace and retaining only the leading low-energy Dirac expansion for the final bound-state expressions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of anisotropic AMTI surface states showing facet-dependent Dirac-cone shifts, and the Josephson junction setup with phase difference $\phi$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first modeling the AMTI surface states, showing how the altermagnetic term causes momentum shifts on lateral facets but not on top/bottom facets. It then applies this to the Josephson junction, deriving the phase-dependent Andreev spectra and demonstrating that the resulting mass inversion at the hinges hosts MZMs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We exploit facet-dependent Andreev phase shifts to control topological superconductivity with a phase bias in a three-dimensional altermagnetic topological-insulator Josephson junction. In the weak link between two conventional s-wave superconductors, the d-wave altermagnetic order produces facet-dependent momentum shifts of the surface Dirac cones. The resulting net momentum of the states involved in Andreev reflection generates additional propagation phases that differ between facets. Consequently, the facet-resolved Andreev spectra exhibit gap closings at distinct phase biases, giving rise to topological superconducting regimes that host Majorana zero modes(MZMs). We further show that tuning the phase bias controls the spatial localization of these modes. The associated topological superconducting transitions are only weakly affected by moderate variations in the chemical potential, obviating the need for fine-tuning to the Dirac point. Our results establish a platform for realizing and spatially controlling MZMs by tuning the superconducting phase bias in altermagnetic topological-insulator Josephson junctions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes a novel platform for realizing topological moiré minibands using noncollinear spin-orbit coupling in centrosymmetric bilayers, moving beyond traditional valley-based models. By modeling twisted $	ext{HgI}_2$ and optimizing the system using machine learning, the authors predict the formation of robust topological phases in $	ext{PbI}_2$. This provides a general, material-designable route for engineering correlated topological insulators.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on engineered topological phases driven by strong spin-orbit coupling and correlated electron behavior makes it highly relevant to condensed matter theory, particularly for understanding emergent quantum states in artificial lattices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a general theoretical route to realizing topological moiré minibands that bypasses the reliance on valley degrees of freedom or hexagonal lattice symmetries.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that noncollinear spin-orbit coupling in centrosymmetric bilayers can generate topological moiré minibands, which can be optimized using machine learning to support tunable quantum spin Hall and Chern insulating phases.

**Method.** The research combines theoretical modeling of moiré Hamiltonians with advanced computational techniques, including the use of a physics-informed machine-learning surrogate for topological classification.

**Model / system.** The system involves Moiré superlattices formed by twisting type-II SOC bilayers, initially modeled with $	ext{HgI}_2$ and optimized for $	ext{PbI}_2$. The physics is governed by the interplay between interlayer hybridization, noncollinear SOC, and the resulting moiré potential.

**Key observables.** Topological $\mathbb{Z}_2$ invariant, localized Berry curvature hotspots, topological flat bands, and the magnitude of the superconducting/insulating gap.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strength of SOC ($\lambda_{	ext{SOC}}$), lattice mismatch/twist angle, and the gap threshold (1 meV).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The mechanism relies on interlayer hybridization opening pseudogaps, and the ML model assumes that SOC strength is the primary determinant of topology.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically summarizes the mechanism from type-II noncollinear-SOC bilayers to topological moiré minibands. Figure 2 illustrates the pseudo-gap opening and localized Berry curvature in the D-2 homobilayer.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the limitations of valley-based moiré systems, proposes the noncollinear SOC route using $	ext{HgI}_2$, develops the moiré Hamiltonian, characterizes the topology using Wilson loops, and finally employs a physics-informed ML surrogate to guide material optimization towards $	ext{PbI}_2$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Moiré superlattices provide a powerful platform for flat bands and correlated topological phases, yet most established examples rely on valley-contrasting Berry curvature in hexagonal lattices. Here, we propose a different route to achieve topological moiré minibands based on noncollinear spin orbit coupling in centrosymmetric type II SOC bilayers. Interlayer hybridization opens local pseudogaps and produces sharply localized Berry curvature, which twisting reconstructs into isolated topological minibands without requiring valley degrees of freedom or hexagonal symmetry. We demonstrate this mechanism in tetragonal Dresselhaus-SOC HgI2, where a Lieb-like moiré potential yields topological flat bands. To improve miniband isolation, we develop a physics-informed machine-learning surrogate that identifies stronger SOC as a key design principle and guides the replacement of Hg by Pb. The resulting PbI2 minibands are narrower and better isolated, supporting correlation-driven magnetism and tunable quantum spin Hall and Chern insulating phases, thereby providing an optimized material realization for experimental exploration.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses molecular dynamics simulations to study the thermal stability of molecular chains stabilized by hydrogen bonds, particularly those formed by molecules like paracetamol. By encapsulating these chains between layers of hexagonal boron nitride (h-BN), the researchers demonstrate a massive enhancement in thermal stability. This suggests a viable pathway for designing next-generation, high-temperature anhydrous proton-exchange membranes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the focus is on classical thermal stability, the use of molecular dynamics simulations to model phase transitions (chain dissociation) and high-temperature material properties touches upon non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and energy landscape exploration.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop materials with high proton conductivity at elevated temperatures and in the absence of water, suitable for Proton-Exchange Membranes (PEMs).

**Main result.** Encapsulation of molecular hydrogen-bond chains within h-BN significantly enhances thermal stability, allowing the creation of anhydrous PEMs operating up to 1140 K.

**Method.** The study employs coarse-grained molecular dynamics simulations to model the thermal stability and dynamics of molecular chains.

**Model / system.** The system involves linear molecular chains (e.g., paracetamol) forming OH...OH...OH hydrogen-bond chains, adsorbed on a substrate and encapsulated by a hexagonal boron nitride (h-BN) sheet. The dynamics are governed by a total Hamiltonian incorporating bond, angle, dihedral, and Lennard-Jones potentials.

**Key observables.** Dissociation temperatures ($T_1$ and $T_2$), and the specific number of hydrogen bonds ($ho_{hb}$) as a function of temperature ($T$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The simulation uses various force field parameters (e.g., LJ parameters, stiffness coefficients $K_0$) and compares stability regimes (open vs. encapsulated).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on coarse-grained molecular dynamics simulations and assumes the applicability of the modeled potentials to describe the physical interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the linear structure of the molecular chains adsorbed and covered by h-BN. Temperature dependence plots (Fig. 14) show the retention of hydrogen bonds under different conditions, with encapsulation providing the highest stability.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the scientific problem (high-temp PEMs), details the physical model (molecular chains + h-BN encapsulation), presents the simulation methodology (coarse-grained MD), and concludes by quantifying the enhanced thermal stability of the encapsulated structures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using a coarse-grained model, we performed numerical simulations of the dynamics of linear molecular chains adsorbed on a flat substrate (on the surface of an h-BN crystal). It is shown that molecules containing benzene rings and hydroxyl groups in their structure can form stable hydrogen-bond chains OH$\cdots$OH$\cdots$OH. Such chains can be formed by phenol C$_6$H$_5$OH, 4-phenylphenol C$_6$H$_5$--C$_6$H$_4$OH, paracetamol CH$_3$C(O)NHC$_6$H$_4$OH, and 4-hydroxybenzanilide C$_6$H$_5$C(O)NHC$_6$H$_4$OH molecules. The dissociation of these chains occurs at temperatures above $T_1=190$, 240, 300, and 400K, respectively. Coating such molecular systems with a hexagonal boron nitride sheet (their van der Waals encapsulation) significantly enhances their thermal stability. Such encapsulated molecular structures retain hydrogen-bond chains up to temperatures of $T_2=470$, 800, 880, and 1140K, respectively. The simulations allow us to conclude that h-BN-encapsulated chains of these molecules can be used to create anhydrous proton-exchange membranes capable of operating at high temperatures. The most promising are encapsulated chains of paracetamol and 4-hydroxybenzanilide molecules.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops and applies a phase-field model to simulate how desiccation cracks mature during repeated drying-wetting cycles. By incorporating crack healing and a 'scar effect' from past damage, the model accurately predicts the relaxation of the crack intersection angle towards 120 degrees. The work demonstrates the power of advanced numerical techniques in modeling complex, history-dependent material failure.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the physics is classical mechanics, the rigorous application of phase-field modeling, coupled with complex iterative numerical solvers (FEM, Newton's method), provides a detailed case study in advanced computational physics techniques applicable to many non-equilibrium systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the relaxation of the characteristic intersection angle of desiccation cracks from near 90 degrees toward 120 degrees under repeated drying-wetting cycles.

**Main result.** The model successfully predicts that the angle distribution develops a peak near 120 degrees, and both the angle standard deviation and crack energy decay exponentially with the cycle number.

**Method.** A phase-field model is employed, solved using a finite element method (FEM) with an alternating iteration scheme to track crack evolution and material damage.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-dimensional isotropic elastic body (paste) undergoing desiccation. The model incorporates a phase-field damage variable, crack healing, and a 'scar effect' (memory field) that modifies local fracture toughness in subsequent cycles.

**Key observables.** Crack intersection angle distribution, standard deviation of the intersection angle, and crack energy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Shrinkage strain ($\epsilon_{sh}$), degradation function $g(d)$, and parameters controlling the scar effect ($a_G, a_P$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The paste is treated as a thin, two-dimensional elastic body, and the simulation relies on iterative numerical solvers (Newton's method) to solve coupled displacement and damage equations.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the analysis involves tracking spatial distributions of damage and calculating intersection angles from the damage field.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the formulation of the total energy functional, describes the desiccation process incorporating history/scar effects, and outlines the numerical solution procedure using alternating iterations for the displacement and damage fields.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The characteristic intersection angle of the desiccation crack relaxes from near \ang{90} toward \ang{120} under repeated drying--wetting cycles. However, the theoretical understanding of this relaxation is insufficient, especially the modeling of the drying--wetting cycles. Here we introduce a phase-field model of desiccation fracture, extending the model proposed in previous studies by adding crack healing and a scar effect left by past cracks. By repeating drying--wetting cycles in a finite element simulation, we find that the angle distribution develops a growing peak near \ang{120} as the cycle number increases, consistent with experiments. The standard deviation of the intersection angle from \ang{120} relaxes exponentially with a characteristic time of about 2.85 cycles. These results are consistent with experiments, except that the characteristic time is slightly smaller than the experimental value. Crack energy dominates the total energy and also relaxes exponentially with nearly the same characteristic cycle as the angle relaxation. This decay is driven mainly by a shortening of the effective crack length rather than a change in effective fracture toughness.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses performance limitations in SOT-MRAM caused by intrinsic stray magnetic bias fields. The authors propose a novel, non-invasive method using magnetic filling in interconnect vias to compensate for this bias field. This technique significantly reduces switching current asymmetry, making the memory more energy-efficient and scalable for future integration.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on spintronics, the concept of engineering local fields to control switching dynamics is relevant to understanding engineered quantum potentials or controlling interactions in artificial quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** SOT-MRAM suffers from an intrinsic bias field due to stray fields, causing asymmetric critical switching currents and increased energy consumption.

**Main result.** The authors propose engineering local stray magnetic fields via magnetic filling in VIA channels, reducing the write-current bias ratio from 21.6% to 1.3% and maintaining performance at nanoscale dimensions.

**Method.** The study uses micromagnetic simulations to model the effect of engineered local stray fields on the magnetization switching dynamics of SOT-MRAM.

**Model / system.** The physical system is Spin-Orbit Torque Magnetic Random-Access Memory (SOT-MRAM) devices, analyzed using the Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation incorporating SOT terms for both PMA and IMA structures.

**Key observables.** Write-current asymmetry, write-current bias ratio, power consumption, and data-retention stability.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bias magnetic field (H_s), critical current densities (J_AP->P, J_P->AP), and device size (down to 10 nm diameter).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that modifying VIA channels with ferromagnetic material can effectively modulate the stray field without altering the sensitive MTJ thin films.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the asymmetry in switching fields and currents due to bias fields, and show the modulation of the bias ratio and current density using the proposed magnetic filling structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of bias-induced asymmetry in SOT-MRAM, then proposes the non-invasive BEOL-compatible field engineering technique, and finally quantifies the resulting improvements in switching symmetry and power consumption.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin-orbit torque magnetic random-access memory (SOT-MRAM) offers high speed, ultrahigh endurance, and compatibility with advanced semiconductor processes, making it a promising candidate for next-generation nonvolatile memory. However, intrinsic bias fields in magnetic tunnel junctions (MTJs), originating from reference-layer stray fields and interlayer coupling, cause asymmetric critical switching currents and increased energy consumption. Existing compensation approaches usually introduce additional magnetic layers into the MTJ stack, which increases fabrication complexity and limits wafer-scale integration. Here, we propose a bias-compensation strategy without modifying the MTJ stack by engineering local stray magnetic fields through magnetic filling materials in vertical interconnect access (VIA) channels during the back-end-of-line process. Micromagnetic simulations show that the proposed magnetic filling layer can provide the required auxiliary field for deterministic switching and significantly suppress write-current asymmetry. By optimizing the MTJ position relative to the magnetic filling structure, the write-current bias ratio is reduced from 21.6% in the conventional design to 1.3%. The approach is also applicable to in-plane magnetic anisotropy SOT-MTJs, reducing the bias ratio from 19.8% to -0.2%. Scaling analysis further demonstrates that the compensation effect remains effective when the device size is reduced to 20% of the original dimension (MTJ diameter approximately 10 nm), indicating its potential for high-density SOT-MRAM integration.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically investigates how Kekulé lattice distortions in graphene simultaneously induce flat electronic bands and enhance electron-phonon coupling. The findings show that the density of states at the Van Hove singularity drives this coupling enhancement, suggesting that the system may enter a nonadiabatic regime where standard approximations fail.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong coupling between lattice structure (Kekulé order) and electronic correlations (flat bands, enhanced coupling) provides a concrete platform for studying emergent correlated quantum phases, which is central to many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To provide a unified, microscopic description linking Kekulé ordering, flat band formation, and the resulting enhancement of electron-phonon coupling in graphene.

**Main result.** The enhancement of electron-phonon coupling is predominantly governed by the increasing density of states at the Van Hove singularity, suggesting potential nonadiabatic effects beyond the conventional Migdal approximation.

**Method.** Theoretical calculation involving the analysis of the electronic band structure and the application of an effective Hopfield-McMillan formula for electron-phonon coupling.

**Model / system.** The system is graphene subjected to Kekulé order, modeled by a spinless Hamiltonian incorporating hopping up to third-nearest neighbors, leading to a $\sqrt{3} 	imes \sqrt{3}$ superlattice distortion.

**Key observables.** Density of States $N(E)$ at the VHS, global width of the $\pi^*$ band ($W$), and the dimensionless electron-phonon coupling ($\lambda$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Kekulé distortion magnitude ($\Delta$), filling fraction ($1/4$), and the ratio governing adiabaticity ($\lambda\Omega_0/W$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses an effective Hopfield-McMillan formula and assumes the deformation potential can be approximated by $\alpha$, while also noting the potential breakdown of the adiabatic approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the $\sqrt{3} 	imes \sqrt{3}$ supercell and band folding, showing band renormalization and the evolution of $N(E)$ and $\lambda$ as the distortion parameter $\Delta$ increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem, models the system using a modified Hamiltonian, calculates key observables like $N(E)$ and $\lambda$ as functions of the distortion, and concludes by discussing the implications for nonadiabatic physics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Kekulé order in graphene is known to give rise to extended flat bands near the van Hove singularity (VHS) and enhanced electron-phonon coupling. However, the microscopic mechanism underlying the coexistence of these effects remains unclear. Here, it is shown that they jointly emerge as a consequence of the lattice distortion manifested through the differentiation of carbon bonds and associated electronic hoppings. In this picture, the conduction band around the $M$ point decreases almost linearly in width with increasing Kekulé order, while the VHS and dimensionless electron-phonon coupling exhibit approximately quadratic upward responses. The resulting enhancement of the electron-phonon coupling is governed predominantly by the increasing density of states at the VHS, with the effective deformation potential remaining weakly affected by the distortion. As such, the obtained results are consistent with previous related theoretical and experimental studies, while also pointing toward a potential shallowing of the conduction band under the characteristic $1/4$ filling and an increasing relevance of nonadiabatic electron-phonon effects beyond the conventional Migdal approximation. Altogether, these findings highlight the potential of controlled Kekulé ordering for engineering emergent correlated quantum phases in graphene.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical model for internal waves propagating in stratified ocean layers affected by a background shear current, specifically assuming a quadratic current profile. Using advanced fluid dynamics techniques, the authors derive nonlinear evolution equations, such as the Gardner's equation, governing the wave dynamics. The key finding is that the resulting wave behavior is highly sensitive to the parameters of the background current.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on geophysical fluid dynamics, the derivation of integrable nonlinear equations (like Gardner's) from physical constraints and the analysis of their soliton solutions share mathematical techniques with those used in nonlinear wave physics relevant to quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study models the propagation of long interfacial internal waves in a two-layer fluid system that is subjected to a background shear current.

**Main result.** The analysis derives nonlinear evolution equations, specifically leading to the Gardner's equation, whose solutions (solitons) are shown to be highly sensitive to the parameters defining the background current profile.

**Method.** The authors employ fluid dynamics principles, utilizing stream functions, complex potentials, and Dirichlet-Neumann Operators (DNO) to derive the governing equations in the long wave asymptotic limit.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-layer fluid (upper and lower) separated by an interface (thermocline), where the background current profile is assumed to be a quadratic function of depth, mimicking the Equatorial Undercurrent (EUC).

**Key observables.** Thermocline elevation $\eta(x, t)$, and the associated nonlinear wave solutions (solitons).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The quadratic coefficients ($\kappa, \gamma, eta$) defining the background current profile, and scaling parameters like amplitude/depth ($\epsilon$) and depth/wavelength ($\delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The current profile is assumed to be quadratic in depth, and the analysis relies on long-wave asymptotic expansions (Boussinesq/KdV scaling).

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the two-dimensional internal wave model, the quadratic flow profile of the EUC, and the general concept of internal waves interacting with a shear current.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical model and the quadratic current assumption, through the rigorous mathematical derivation using potential theory and boundary operators, culminating in the derivation and analysis of the resulting nonlinear evolution equations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Internal waves often propagate in the presence of currents. Internal waves and currents are widespread in the world's oceans. The equatorial internal waves and the equatorial undercurrent (EUC) are perhaps the most representative illustration of this phenomenon. The current profile in this study is a quadratic function of depth, which is in agreement with the known results of the EUC. With this assumption the integrable nonlinear Gardner's equation is derived as a model for the wave motion of the thermocline, separating the two layers of slightly different densities. In addition, we derive the generalised Gardner's equation in the case of variable bottom. We demonstrate that the coefficients of the equation are very sensitive with respect to the parameters of the EUC.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores how altermagnetism, typically identified by spin-split bands, can hide in one-dimensional magnetic nanoribbons due to geometric confinement. By incorporating Spin-Orbit Coupling (SOC), the authors demonstrate that a robust, SOC-driven spin splitting can reappear, establishing a mechanism for 1D altermagnetism hidden within seemingly conventional antiferromagnetic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the concept of symmetry-protected topological phases and the interplay between lattice confinement and relativistic quantum effects (SOC) is relevant to understanding engineered quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the diagnostic signature of altermagnetism (spin-split bands in nonrelativistic spectra) can disappear under one-dimensional (1D) confinement, and how relativistic spin splitting can reappear despite this nonrelativistic degeneracy.

**Main result.** The study shows that 1D fully compensated altermagnetic functionality can exist, characterized by spin-degenerate nonrelativistic bands but sizable SOC-induced spin splitting when the Néel vector is oriented along specific crystal directions.

**Method.** The research uses first-principles Wannier Hamiltonians with spin matrices to model the electronic structure and analyze the effect of symmetry operations on spin splitting.

**Model / system.** The system studied is fully compensated [110] Ta2TeSeO nanoribbons, modeled using a Lieb-based construction, which is analyzed in the 1D limit.

**Key observables.** Spin expectation value along the Néel vector ($\sigma \cdot \hat{N}$), SOC-induced spin splitting, and right-moving-mode spin polarization ($P_{	ext{mode},q}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** One-dimensional confinement, Néel-vector orientation ($\mathbf{N}$), and the presence/absence of Spin-Orbit Coupling (SOC).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ribbon is wide enough to retain the parent easy-axis magnetic order, and transport calculations rely on ideal Landauer conductance models.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dimensional reduction process, the spin-degenerate non-SOC bands, and the emergence of spin-split relativistic spectra for various Néel vector orientations, alongside mode polarization plots.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical problem of 1D confinement suppressing the altermagnetic signature, then uses a Lieb-based model to calculate the electronic structure, demonstrating the SOC-induced splitting, and finally analyzes the resulting spin polarization in transport measurements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnetism is normally identified by nonrelativistic spin-split bands in a compensated collinear magnet. Under one-dimensional confinement, however, this diagnostic can disappear: a boundary-compatible sublattice-exchange operation may leave the only Bloch momentum unchanged, forcing the nonrelativistic spin-up and spin-down spectra to coincide. We show that nonrelativistic spin degeneracy can coexist with relativistic spin splitting in a compensated one-dimensional magnet. In a Lieb-based construction, confinement along the diagonal cancels the projected $d$-wave spin splitting while preserving the real-space sublattice-exchange motif. Spin-orbit coupling (SOC) then locks spin to the lattice, so the Néel-vector orientation selects a magnetic line group that can reveal spin-split bands. Using fully compensated $[110]$ Ta$_2$TeSeO nanoribbons as a prototype, we find spin-degenerate nonrelativistic bands and sizable SOC splitting in the inherited easy-axis domain, $\mathbf N\parallel[100]$ or $[010]$. For sufficiently wide ribbons that retain the parent easy-axis order, this splitting is an equilibrium property. The easy-axis ribbon also supports a right-moving-mode spin polarization of about $35\%$ at finite ideal conductance. Our results establish one-dimensional fully compensated altermagnetic functionality hidden inside apparently conventional antiferromagnetic bands.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work explores how quantum interference between localized electronic states—specifically those bound by vortices or impurities—can dramatically boost the thermoelectric response in superconductors. Using the BdG framework, the authors demonstrate that the overlap of these defect states breaks particle-hole symmetry, offering a pathway to engineer local thermoelectric energy conversion in superconducting devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on local transport properties (Seebeck coefficient) driven by quantum interference in superconducting defects provides a concrete, measurable physical mechanism for controlling electronic properties in superconducting materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quantum interference between localized bound states induced by vortices or nonmagnetic impurities modifies the local thermoelectric response in superconductors.

**Main result.** The thermoelectric response is strongly enhanced when defect-induced states overlap and interfere, particularly in the vortex case, suggesting that spatial structure controls local thermoelectricity.

**Method.** The authors employed a lattice Bogoliubov-de Gennes approach combined with linear-response tunneling theory to calculate the spatially resolved density of states and Seebeck coefficient.

**Model / system.** The system models $s$- and $d$-wave superconductors on a lattice, incorporating superconducting pairing, hopping terms, and perturbations from vortices (via Peierls substitution) or impurities (via site-dependent chemical potential).

**Key observables.** Spatially resolved density of states ($ho(\mathbf{r}, E)$) and the Seebeck coefficient ($S$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Vortex/impurity separation distance ($d$), superconducting pairing symmetry ($s$-wave vs. $d$-wave), and the coherence length ($\xi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations assume the linear-response regime and utilize a mean-field approximation for the superconducting state.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the enhancement of the Seebeck coefficient as vortex separation decreases, showing spectral reconstruction and LDOS changes; comparisons are made between vortex and impurity separation effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the general suppression of thermoelectricity due to particle-hole symmetry, then analyzes the enhancement mechanism separately for overlapping vortex-bound states and for impurity-induced states, comparing the magnitude and spectral signatures of both effects.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Thermoelectric effects in superconductors are generally suppressed by the approximate particle-hole symmetry of the quasiparticle spectrum, but can become pronounced near defects that host particle-hole asymmetric bound states. Here, we investigate how the local thermoelectric response is modified when multiple vortices or nonmagnetic impurities are brought into proximity. Using a lattice Bogolioubov-de Gennes approach combined with linear-response tunneling theory, we calculate the spatially resolved density of states and Seebeck coefficient in $s$- and $d$-wave superconductors. We find that the thermoelectric response can be strongly enhanced when spatially extended defect-induced states overlap. For vortices, this enhancement persists beyond the immediate core regions and originates from interference between vortex-bound states. For impurities, the thermoelectric response exhibits a comparable dependence on impurity separation in the $s$- and $d$-wave cases, although the associated spectral reconstruction is considerably more localized in the $s$-wave superconductor. Our results show that the spatial extent and interference of defect-induced quasiparticle states provide a means of controlling local thermoelectricity in inhomogeneous superconductors, with potential relevance for cryogenic thermoelectric sensing and energy conversion.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a novel substrate engineering approach by coupling a 2D ferromagnetic semiconductor ($	ext{Cr}_2	ext{Ge}_2	ext{Te}_6$) with an antiferromagnetic insulator ($	ext{CrOCl}$). This interface acts as a 'booster,' converting the device into a high-performance P-type FET. Crucially, the coupling allows for the electrical tuning of the magnetic coercive field by over 200%, demonstrating a powerful pathway for gate-tunable spintronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to electrically tune magnetic anisotropy energy via interfacial charge transfer in a 2D system provides a solid-state platform for studying field-effect control over magnetic ground states, relevant to correlated electron physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing functional spin transistors whose charge and spin properties are tunable via electric fields, which is challenging due to the inherent limitations of magnetic semiconductors.

**Main result.** Interfacing the 2D ferromagnetic semiconductor $	ext{Cr}_2	ext{Ge}_2	ext{Te}_6$ with an antiferromagnetic insulator $	ext{CrOCl}$ creates a substrate booster that enables high-performance P-type FETs with giant gate-tunability of the magnetic coercive field ($>200\%$).

**Method.** The study combines electrical transport measurements (FET characterization) with magnetic characterization using Polar MOKE microscopy, supplemented by DFT calculations to model interfacial charge transfer.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a dual-gated Field-Effect Transistor (FET) heterostructure composed of few-layered $	ext{Cr}_2	ext{Ge}_2	ext{Te}_6$ (FL-CGT) coupled with an antiferromagnetic insulator $	ext{CrOCl}$. The mechanism relies on interfacial charge transfer coupling to modulate the magnetic anisotropy energy.

**Key observables.** ON-OFF ratio, coercive field ($H_c$), transconductance ($G_m$), and the relative change in coercive field ($\Delta H_c/H_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate voltage ($V_{bg}$), temperature ($T$), and the coupling strength derived from interfacial charge transfer.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The enhancement mechanism is attributed to an efficient interfacial charge transfer that acts as an active, electric-field-controlled modulator, rather than simple passive pinning.

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate the p-type switching in the FET structure, show MOKE hysteresis loops illustrating $H_c$ dependence on $V_{bg}$, and present temperature-dependent scaling analyses of the coercivity tunability.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents the device structure, demonstrates the electrical switching to a p-type regime, quantifies the giant gate-tunability of the magnetic coercive field, and finally analyzes the temperature and doping dependence using critical scaling laws.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin transistors with its both charge and spin properties tuned via electrostatic gating are believed capable for widespread use, which however have proven challenging due to the extreme rareness of their physical base -- magnetic semiconductors. The latter are limited within very few systems including diluted magnetic semiconductors (DMS) and two-dimensional ferromagnetic semiconductors (2D-FMS), and known to suffer from inadequate gate-tunability of their electric and/or magnetic properties. Here, we show a substrate engineering paradigm by interfacing few-layered Cr$_{2}$Ge$_{2}$Te$_{6}$ (FL-CGT) with an antiferromagnetic insulator CrOCl. Owing to the subtle interfacial charge transfer couplings, CGT can be drastically turned from an ambipolar semiconductor into a high performance P-type semiconductor. When cooled below the Curie temperature, the ON-OFF ratio in such substrate-boosted FMS field-effect transistor (FET) reaches 10$^{5}$ with its coercive field $H_{c}$ of magnetic hysteresis loop tunable by a factor of more than 200$\%$, enabling {gate-assisted magnetic switching in the prototype semiconducting spin transistor architecture}. A crossover from critical power-law scaling to a dual power-law behaviour under heavy hole doping was further observed. Our findings {signify} an efficient interfacial charge transfer and electrically modulated magnetic anisotropy energy supported by calculations. This high performance P-type FMS-FET system suggests that active substrate-boosting paradigm might be a powerful path for the investigation of future gate-tunable spintronic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports a novel, low-temperature vapor-phase method to grow metastable 2D hexagonal Copper(I) Iodide (h-CuI) directly onto graphene. The research combines synthesis, advanced microscopy, and DFT/AIMD calculations to confirm the structural integrity and electronic stabilization of this semiconductor/graphene hybrid. This establishes a promising platform for next-generation wide-bandgap electronic and optoelectronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a detailed understanding of interfacial electronic and structural coupling between a semiconductor and a 2D material, which is crucial for designing novel heterostructures for optoelectronics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was to establish a bottom-up, low-temperature synthesis route for the metastable 2D hexagonal Copper(I) Iodide (h-CuI) directly onto chemically inert graphene templates.

**Main result.** The authors successfully synthesized and characterized epitaxial h-CuI domains on both monolayer and bilayer reduced oxo-graphene (r-oxo-G) using a vapor-phase process, confirming its stability and structure.

**Method.** The work combines experimental synthesis (HI vapor exposure and annealing) with advanced characterization (HRTEM, STEM-EDX) and theoretical modeling (DFT calculations and AIMD simulations).

**Model / system.** The system involves the epitaxial growth of h-CuI, a wide-bandgap semiconductor, on graphene substrates (SLG and BLG). Theoretical studies model the van der Waals adhesion energy and vibrational dynamics within these heterostructures.

**Key observables.** Atomic structure, local twist angles, lattice anisotropy, Cu:I stoichiometry, van der Waals adhesion energy ($	ext{E}_{	ext{vdW}}$), and Mean-Square Displacements ($	ext{MSD}_{xy}, 	ext{MSD}_z$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low synthesis temperatures ($180^\circ	ext{C}$), which prevent the formation of the bulk $\gamma$-phase; the stabilization energy provided by van der Waals adhesion.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The growth is limited by remaining interfacial adsorbates, and AIMD simulations are restricted to specific time segments.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the two-step synthesis process, structural characterization of the graphene templates, and the resulting epitaxial growth of h-CuI on the graphene layers.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the synthesis method, presents structural characterization comparing growth on SLG vs. BLG, quantifies the adhesion energy using DFT, and analyzes the thermal stability and vibrational properties using AIMD simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Copper(I) iodide (CuI) is a wide-bandgap semiconductor crystallizing in the 3D $γ$-phase under ambient conditions; its layered van der Waals bulk phase $β$-CuI is stable only between 643 and 673 K. The two-dimensional (2D) h-CuI form has been obtained via liquid-phase exfoliation of mechanochemically prepared precursors and via encapsulation between graphene sheets, whereas bottom-up growth of 2D h-CuI on open surfaces has not yet been demonstrated. Here, we report a vapor-phase synthesis of h-CuI directly on low-defect, large-area monolayer and within bilayer reduced oxo-graphene (r-oxo-G) at low temperatures. Using a copper TEM grid as the solid-state precursor for copper, HI-vapor exposure at 40 $°$C initiates nucleation, while annealing at 180 $°$C promotes the growth of extended h-CuI domains. Aberration-corrected HRTEM resolves the atomic structure, local twist angles, and lattice anisotropy of the CuI/r-oxo-G nanohybrid, while STEM-EDX yields a Cu:I ratio consistent with 1:1. First-principles calculations show that van der Waals adhesion to graphene stabilizes the supported hexagonal layer. Under the presented low-temperature precursor conditions, pathways for nucleation of the $γ$-phase are not available, allowing the hexagonal phase to form selectively at the graphene interface. Ab initio molecular dynamics simulations show that the heterostructure retains its hexagonal lattice order at 600 K, including on an open monolayer graphene support. The lateral extent of the growth is limited mainly by remaining interfacial adsorbates. These results establish a route to metastable 2D h-CuI on a chemically inert graphene template, which may be useful for wide-bandgap electronic and optoelectronic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This research investigates the controlled defect engineering of monolayer hBN using ultra-low-energy ion irradiation to create quantum emitter sites. The authors report that experimental defect creation deviates significantly from theoretical predictions, highlighting the complex role of impurity-induced vacancy filling. The study is vital for advancing the reliable synthesis and characterization of defect-based quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly addresses the material science challenges in creating quantum emitters by providing empirical data on defect control, which is crucial for understanding localized electronic states relevant to quantum physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to achieve selective creation and characterization of specific point defects in hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) for use as a host for quantum emitters, as a direct correlation between defect structure and quantum emission properties is currently lacking.

**Main result.** Experimental irradiation predominantly creates boron single vacancies (V_B), which contradicts previous simulation predictions, and the process is complicated by significant, uncontrolled vacancy filling by impurity atoms like Silicon.

**Method.** The study combines experimental techniques, including ultra-low-energy Ar+ ion irradiation and STEM imaging, with computational analysis using CNNs and molecular dynamics simulations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is monolayer hBN, which is prepared via electrochemical delamination and analyzed after controlled irradiation with low-energy noble gas ions. The analysis compares experimental defect statistics against theoretical predictions from collision simulations.

**Key observables.** Defect density and distribution (e.g., V_B, V_N, Si_B), mean intensity profiles across atomic arrangements, and displacement threshold energies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Irradiation energy ($\approx 150$ eV), ion fluence ($0.8 \pm 0.2 	ext{ ions}\cdot	ext{nm}^{-2}$), and defect concentrations ($	ext{V}_{	ext{B}}$ vs. $	ext{V}_{	ext{N}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that observed intensity variations can be modeled by atomic displacement, electron beam interactions, and potential impurity substitution (e.g., Oxygen for Nitrogen).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate sample preparation, STEM imaging setups, defect density comparisons (pre- vs. post-irradiation), and simulation results comparing ideal, vibrating, and oxygen-substituted atomic arrangements.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for controlled defect creation in hBN, detailing the experimental irradiation and characterization methods, presenting quantitative comparisons between observed and simulated defect densities, and finally delving into advanced image analysis to interpret the nature of the observed defects.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) has recently become the focus of intense research as a material that can host quantum emitters. It is known that such emission is related to point defects, but in order to conclusively correlate specific defects to their spectra, having control over the defect creation mechanism is required. Here, we prepare freestanding, monolayer hBN samples and irradiate them with ultra-low-energy (150 eV) Ar+ ions. The samples are characterized before and after irradiation via scanning transmission electron microscopy to assess the defect density and distribution. Contrary to what analytical potential molecular dynamics simulations have predicted, we predominantly observe boron single vacancies after ion irradiation, followed by double vacancies at half the count. Moreover, we also observe that vacancy filling with Si and C impurity atoms plays a more significant role in the created defects than previously assumed, potentially posing a problem for selective creation of quantum emitters in hBN.

</details>
