**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates how spin-orbit coupling (SOC) manifests in Janus MXenes, revealing a complex correlation between spin, orbital, and Bloch dynamics. The key finding is the emergence of a unique, trigonally patterned spin-momentum locking that deviates significantly from conventional SOC models. This suggests these materials are promising platforms for advanced spintronic applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding of a novel, trigonally locked spin-momentum coupling mechanism, distinct from standard symmetries, provides a new platform for engineering spin-related phenomena in 2D materials, which is highly relevant for advanced spintronics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the emergence of spin-orbit coupling (SOC) and its resulting correlation among electronic spin, orbital, and Bloch dynamics in specific Janus double-transition-metal MXenes.

**Main result.** The SOC leads to a unique, trigonally alternating spin-momentum locking pattern with the spin axis perpendicular to the layer plane, which cannot be described by conventional SOC forms like Rashba or Dresselhaus couplings.

**Method.** The authors employed first-principles DFT calculations combined with the development and analysis of a reduced, effective three-band model Hamiltonian near the $\Gamma$ point.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the Janus double-transition-metal MXenes ($	ext{Mo}_2	ext{HfC}_2	ext{OS}$ and $	ext{W}_2	ext{HfC}_2	ext{OS}$). The model involves deriving and diagonalizing an effective Hamiltonian that incorporates multiple SOC terms ($	ext{H}_{	ext{LS}}^{	ext{soc}}$, $	ext{H}_{	ext{R}}^{	ext{soc}}$, etc.) to capture the complex spin-orbital coupling.

**Key observables.** Spin-split band structure, staggered spin configuration with a trigonal pattern, and the spin polarization angle ($\theta$) as a function of momentum ($k$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of coupling constants ($\rho$) is crucial for accurate polarization, and the analysis is performed under the low-energy and small wave number condition.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on developing an effective model Hamiltonian parameterized by first-principles DFT results, and the initial functional choice (non-hybrid) was noted to underestimate the band gap.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare band structures calculated with and without SOC, showing the characteristic spin-split dispersion and the trigonal anisotropy around the $\Gamma$ point. Another figure illustrates the spin angle ($\theta$) dependence on momentum.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from initial DFT calculations establishing the SOC effects in Janus MXenes, to developing a comprehensive effective Hamiltonian that includes multiple coupling terms, and finally analyzing the resulting unique spin-momentum locking and polarization patterns.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We found a spin-orbit coupling to cause a simultaneous correlation among three degrees of freedom, the electronic spin, orbital, and Bloch dynamics in an investigation into the electronic structure of Janus double-transition-metal MXenes, Mo$_2$HfC$_2$OS and W$_2$HfC$_2$OS. In this paper, it is also revealed that the spin-orbit coupling causes a staggered spin configuration with a trigonal pattern around the $Γ$ point near the insulating gap. We developed a reduced Hamiltonian describing the electronic states and show that the spin-orbit coupling cannot be equated with conventional forms for a single electron in solids, LS, Rashba, and Dresselhaus couplings, even in the approximation under the low-energy and small wave number condition. Because of the intrinsic shape of the conduction band, a trigonally alternating spin-momentum locking emerges with the spin axis perpendicular to the layer plane. The theoretical analysis shows that these Janus materials can provide a platform for exploring the spin-related phenomena due to the trigonal spin-momentum locking other than Rashba and Dresselhaus types.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel covariant renormalization technique to dramatically improve the accuracy of k.p models used to calculate electronic band structures from first-principles data. By renormalizing the momentum matrix spectra, the method overcomes the convergence and non-locality issues of traditional k.p approaches. It successfully reproduces band structures for semiconductors like GaP and AlN, even generalizing to systems with strong spin-orbit coupling.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics, the renormalization technique itself is a sophisticated parameter fitting and model reduction technique applicable to any physical system described by an effective Hamiltonian where high-fidelity data is available.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The standard k.p method for electronic band structure suffers from slow convergence with the number of bands and inaccuracies when the underlying Hamiltonian is nonlocal.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a covariant renormalization scheme that significantly improves the accuracy of k.p models, allowing for the reproduction of band structures and density of states with high fidelity.

**Method.** The core method involves renormalizing the eigenvalue spectra of the Hermitian momentum matrices using a loss function that balances energy and velocity errors against DFT reference data.

**Model / system.** The study applies to the electronic band structures of various semiconductors, including GaP, AlN (zincblende and wurtzite), and PbTe, covering systems with and without strong spin-orbit coupling.

**Key observables.** Band energies, band velocities, Density of States (DOS), and hole conductivity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required number of bands (e.g., 15 to 66 bands for AlN) and the fitting energy window are critical parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The renormalization process assumes that scaling the gauge-invariant eigenvalue spectra preserves degeneracies and crystal symmetry, and that the model can be accurately described by a renormalized k.p Hamiltonian.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare bare vs. renormalized k.p models across different band counts for GaP and AlN, demonstrating improved agreement with DFT. They also show the generalization to SOC systems like PbTe and the ability to generate full-zone models.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of raw k.p models, then details the covariant renormalization scheme, validates it on multiple materials (GaP, AlN, PbTe), and finally demonstrates its utility in generating compact, full-zone models for transport calculations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The k$\cdot$p method can be applied directly to first-principles energies and momentum matrix elements, but the resulting models converge slowly with the number of bands and are inexact wherever the underlying Hamiltonian is nonlocal. We show that both limitations can be largely removed by renormalizing the eigenvalue spectra of the Hermitian momentum matrices. These spectra are gauge invariant, and are identical for symmetry-related Cartesian components, hence scaling the recurring magnitudes provides a modest set of parameters that preserves degeneracies and crystal symmetry without needing to construct a symmetry-adapted basis. We choose to renormalize the models against reference eigenvalues and band velocities on rays out of a high-symmetry point. For GaP, a 15-band model can reproduce the band structure in the near-gap reference regions within a few meV. For zincblende and wurtzite AlN, similar accuracy requires 30- or 66-band models, which in the case of zincblende is traced to the strong warping in the [110] direction. The scheme readily generalizes to rocksalt PbTe, which includes spin-orbit coupling, and has L-centered valence and conduction band extrema. Further, we demonstrate, for GaP, how compact four-band Hamiltonians can be obtained by downfolding within the same renormalization scheme. Moreover, by changing the fitting regime to encompass the entire Brillouin zone, the scheme can be used to generate full-zone models that reproduce the density of states. The practical utility is illustrated with a 59-band k.p model for GaP, which can be evaluated on meshes far denser than the reference calculation, that in turn can be used both to resolve fine features in the density of states and to compute the hole conductivity at low temperature.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on magnetotransport studies of Tb/Co multilayers where the magnetic anisotropy has been engineered to be tilted. The key finding is that this built-in tilt breaks symmetry, enabling the anomalous Hall effect to be controlled by applying an in-plane magnetic field. This establishes the material as a promising platform for advanced Hall effect sensors.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the concept of using engineered anisotropy to control symmetry-breaking mechanisms that govern transport responses (like AHE) has parallels in controlling quantum states or coupling strengths in quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to control the anomalous Hall response in magnetic multilayers using an in-plane magnetic field by engineering the magnetic anisotropy.

**Main result.** The anomalous Hall effect observed under an in-plane field originates from the tilt of the uniaxial anisotropy axis, which breaks symmetry and allows for in-plane field control over the out-of-plane AHE sign.

**Method.** Magnetotransport measurements of anomalous Hall resistivity ($ho_{xy}$) and longitudinal resistivity ($ho_{xx}$) are performed as a function of external magnetic field angle and temperature.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a ferrimagnetic [Tb/Co] multilayer grown with a Tb thickness gradient, inducing a wedge-shaped tilt in the uniaxial anisotropy axis. The analysis is supported by a simplified macrospin model.

**Key observables.** Anomalous Hall resistivity ($ho_{xy}$), longitudinal resistivity ($ho_{xx}$), coercive field ($H_C$), and angular dependence of transport coefficients.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature range (80 K to 300 K), in-plane field orientation ($\phi_H$), and the degree of anisotropy tilt.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a simplified macrospin model, and the AHE is assumed to be dominated by the Co sublattice in the measured temperature window.

**Figures summary.** Figures show $ho_{xy}$ loops revealing spin reorientation transitions, and angular dependence measurements ($ho_{xx}, ho_{xy}$ vs. angle) demonstrating the influence of the tilted anisotropy.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically investigates magnetotransport responses ($ho_{xx}, ho_{xy}$) under both out-of-plane and in-plane field rotations, using angular dependence analysis and comparison with a macrospin model to pinpoint the role of the anisotropy tilt.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Engineering magnetic anisotropy provides a powerful route to control magnetization orientation and unlock emerging functionalities in opto-spintronic and current-driven devices. Beyond its role in magnetization reversal, the effective anisotropy can strongly influence the magnetotransport response, offering an additional degree of freedom to tune new device functionalities. In this work, we report a magnetotransport study of a ferrimagnetic [Tb/Co]$_{\times 5}$ multilayer grown with a Tb thickness gradient, whose wedge-shaped tilts the uniaxial anisotropy axis slightly away from the film normal. Anomalous Hall resistivity measurements from 80 K to 300 K reveal a spin reorientation transition, while the angular dependence of the magnetotransport responses exposes the crucial role of the tilted anisotropy. A simplified macrospin model reproduces the full angular response across the transition and shows that the observed anomalous Hall effect when the in-plane magnetic field is applied originates from the tilt of the uniaxial anisotropy axis, which supplies a built-in symmetry-breaking mechanism, enabling in-plane field control over the out-of-plane anomalous Hall response, sign included. These findings establish tilted magnetic anisotropy as a promising route toward Hall effect-based sensor applications and highlight Tb/Co multilayers as a versatile platform for anisotropy-engineered spintronic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes the existence of self-similar vector solitons in optical fibers whose properties vary along the propagation direction. By solving the coupled higher-order nonlinear Schrödinger equations using similarity transformations, the authors demonstrate that the pulse dynamics can be precisely controlled by tuning the distributed dispersion and gain/loss parameters. This provides a powerful framework for managing light pulses in complex, real-world optical media.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mathematical techniques used (similarity transformations on variable-coefficient PDEs) are highly relevant to modeling complex, non-uniform physical systems encountered in quantum optics and nonlinear wave physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to prove the existence and analyze the dynamics of self-similar vector solitons propagating in inhomogeneous optical fibers.

**Main result.** Two types of self-similar vector solitons—bright-W-shaped and kink-antikink waveforms—were found, demonstrating that pulse control can be achieved by manipulating the distributed third-order dispersion and gain/loss parameters.

**Method.** The authors employed the similarity transformation method to reduce the variable-coefficient coupled Higher-Order Nonlinear Schrödinger equations into solvable forms, allowing for the derivation of exact analytical solutions.

**Model / system.** The system models light pulse propagation in inhomogeneous optical fibers, governed by coupled higher-order nonlinear Schrödinger (HNLS) equations that incorporate varying second/third-order dispersions, self/cross-phase modulation, and gain/loss effects.

**Key observables.** Bright-W-shaped and kink-antikink soliton waveforms, pulse intensity profiles, and the evolution of the pulse shape and trajectory.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Distributed third-order dispersion parameter ($D_3(z)$) and the gain/loss coefficient, which are key factors controlling the pulse shape and intensity, respectively.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on using specific ansatz solutions and making assumptions about the functional form of the variable coefficients (e.g., periodic or exponential profiles).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the intensity evolution of the found soliton solutions, showing profiles for bright/W-shaped solitons and kink/antikink solitons under various parameter settings.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the coupled HNLS equations, applies similarity transformations to handle variable coefficients, derives general self-similar wave solutions, and then analyzes the dynamics under specific parameter variations (periodic or exponential dispersion/loss).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove the existence of two kinds of self-similar vector solitons in an inhomogeneous optical fiber medium, where light propagation is governed by a pair of coupled higher-order nonlinear Schrodinger equations with varying second- and third-order dispersions, self- and cross-phase modulation non linearities, self-steepening, and linear gain/loss effects. The newly found self-similar waves comprise bright-W-shaped and kink-antikink waveforms with nonvanishing amplitudes. As a practical exam ple, we discuss the propagation dynamics of these soliton structures in a periodically distributed fiber system as well as an exponential dispersion-decreasing fiber. The results demonstrate that the parameter functions of gain/loss and third-order dispersion serve as a key factor in determining the nonlinear dynamics of self-similar vector solitons. In particular, we find that precise control over the shape and dynamic evolution of self-similar pulses can be achieved through a proper choice of the distributed third-order dispersion parameter, while the gain/loss coefficient controls their intensity.

</details>
